Day 72: The Glory of God in the Shadow of Death
The Valley of Death is But a Shadow
Key Passage
Even though I walk through the darkest valley, I will fear no evil, for you are with me.
Psalm 23:4
Teaching
Psalm 22 was all about the cross. Now let's go to Psalm 23, "the valley of the shadow of death", and see our Lord buried in the tomb with our sin. Remember, we want to see the glory of God in the gospel of Jesus Christ because this sets us free. We are transformed by seeing and savoring!
Psalm 23: The Shepherd Who Saves, Sustains, and Secures
I. The Saving Shepherd (Psalm 23:1-3) – Redemption

    Possession – “The Lord is my shepherd” (Psalm 23:1). Jesus said, “I am the Good Shepherd. The Good Shepherd lays down His life for the sheep” (John 10:11). You can picture a good shepherd more concerned about his sheep than his own life, willing to die to protect them. This is what Jesus did for us, His sheep, and all who believe can own Him as "my Shepherd", even as Paul called the gospel "my gospel" (Romans 16:25) and Thomas owned Jesus as "my Lord and my God" (John 20:28). 

    Provision – “I shall not want” (Psalm 23:1). Picture the entire world screaming "I want". I want a thrill so I engage in sexual impurity, I want to feel full so I engorge on food, I want to relieve stress so I smoke, I want to drown out the voice of guilt in my head so I get drunk. But when people come into the fold and family of Jesus Christ, our Shepherd knows our needs and meets them. Jesus met our greatest need—salvation (John 10:9), and this satisfies our hearts. And we should know that the cross of Jesus is the promise of future provision: "He who did not spare his own Son, but gave him up for us all—how will he not also, along with him, graciously give us all things?" (Romans 8:32). "I shall not want" is the creed and cry of the satisfied believer.

    Peace – “He makes me lie down in green pastures. He leads me beside still waters” (Psalm 23:2). A characteristic trait of mine while engaged in a lifestyle of gratifying my flesh was a lack of peace. I was always trying to make my life peaceful externally, using calming music, breathing apps, soothing environments, etc. and that is because I had no internal peace. Jesus did the hard work on the cross, He sweat blood for us, that we might now rest in Him (Matthew 11:28). Green pastures and still waters indicate total calm and real peace, for all who follow the Shepherd. "Peace I leave with you; my peace I give you. I do not give to you as the world gives. Do not let your hearts be troubled and do not be afraid" (John 14:27).

    Purification – “He restores my soul. He leads me in paths of righteousness for His name’s sake” (Psalm 23:3). Jesus restores us by taking our sin and giving us His righteousness (2 Corinthians 5:21). True purity is not merely ceasing a behavior, it's being washed at the cross and purified from all sin and receiving the righteousness of Jesus. "who gave himself for us to redeem us from all wickedness and to purify for himself a people that are his very own, eager to do what is good" (Titus 2:14). See Revelation 1:5 where the word "freed" also means "washed". 

II. The Suffering Shepherd (Psalm 23:4) – The Cross and the Tomb

    The Valley of Death – “Even though I walk through the valley of the shadow of death, I will fear no evil” (Psalm 23:4). This foreshadows Jesus’ suffering and death, as He lay in the tomb for three days (Matthew 12:40). “He was cut off out of the land of the living” (Isaiah 53:8). But Jesus, in His death and burial, changed the believer's death into nothing but a shadow. The "shadow of death" is a dark valley, yes, but it can't hurt you! Nobody has ever been run over by a shadow, stabbed, shot, or killed by a shadow. And please remember, believers always walk through the valley of the shadow of death, we never get stuck in it.

    The Presence of God – “For You are with me” (Psalm 23:4). The Father, having abandoned His Son at the cross, as He poured out His wrath on the One He loves, did not abandon Him to the grave (Psalm 16:10, Acts 2:27). The presence of God is now a promise for all who believe, as God promises "I will never leave your nor forsake you" (Hebrews 13:5). 

    The Rod and Staff – “Your rod and Your staff, they comfort me” (Psalm 23:4). The rod symbolizes God’s justice, the staff His guidance and rescue—both fulfilled and purchased for us in Jesus' death and resurrection. We do not have to wonder which direction to go, how to live our lives, whether or not we are making the right decision; the God who executed judgment at the cross for you is the God who guides you in every way through your life by His Spirit. "When the Spirit of truth comes, he will guide you into all the truth, for he will not speak on his own authority, but whatever he hears he will speak, and he will declare to you the things that are to come" (John 16:13). 

Jesus entered the darkest valley for us so that we never have to fear death (Hebrews 2:14-15).
III. The Sovereign Shepherd (Psalm 23:5-6) – Resurrection and Reign

    Victory Over Enemies – “You prepare a table before me in the presence of my enemies” (Psalm 23:5). Here is the boldness of the believer; we know our enemy has been defeated, so we do not fear them. This verse pictures a believer sitting down to a feast, leaning back to enjoy fellowship with other believers, enjoying the banquet of love prepared for us, and right in the middle of our spiritual enemies. Jesus defeated sin and Satan at the cross, and He defeated death through His resurrection (1 Corinthians 15:54-57), so we can feast on the cross, and rejoice in our victory.

    The Anointing – “You anoint my head with oil” (Psalm 23:5). Jesus is the Risen and Anointed King (Acts 2:36).

    Overflowing Grace – “My cup overflows” (Psalm 23:5). One of the characteristics of all who turn to false gods to satisfy themselves is a draining emptiness in their lives. They live for the thrill, for the pleasure of sin, because they are inherently empty. But coming to faith in Jesus, and learning to go to the Rock that was struck (the cross), we experience Jesus pouring out the Holy Spirit on us (John 7:37-39) so that we drink Him in daily. This provides us with fullness and satisfaction, symbolized by an overflowing cup.

    The Eternal Home – “I shall dwell in the house of the Lord forever” (Psalm 23:6). Through Christ, we have eternal life and a home in heaven (John 14:2-3).

Conclusion:
Psalm 23 is ultimately about Jesus—our Good Shepherd who:

    Laid down His life to redeem us (John 10:11).
    Walked through death’s valley to rescue us from it (Colossians 1:13).
    Rose in victory and now leads us home (Revelation 7:17).

Let us trust, follow, and rejoice in our Shepherd, who saves, sustains, and secures us forever!
How does seeing Jesus as the Good Shepherd, who saves, sustains, and secures you, impact your daily walk with Him?

Biblical Illustration: The Roman Centurion
The Roman centurion who stood at the foot of the cross had seen many men die, but never like this. He had heard the mockery, witnessed the agony, and yet, saw Jesus respond with love: praying for His enemies, forgiving a thief, and entrusting Himself to the Father. When Jesus breathed His last, the earth quaked, the sky darkened, and the veil was torn.
The centurion, once hardened, now saw clearly. Overwhelmed by what he had witnessed, he declared, “Truly, this was the Son of God!” (Matthew 27:54). The Shepherd had walked through the valley of death, and this soldier’s heart was forever changed.
Like him, when we see Christ’s suffering for us, we are transformed.
Because Jesus took our death and was buried in a borrowed tomb, we never see death (John 8:51). Death is but a shadow for us, and shadows can't hurt us.
Dr. Barnhouse's wife died and he was driving their young children to the next town over where he would be conducting her funeral. Notice what he said to his children:

    As he drove his children to his wife’s funeral Barnhouse stopped at a traffic crossing. Ahead of them was a huge truck. The sun was at such an angle that it cast the truck’s shadow across the snow-covered field beside it. Dr. Barnhouse pointed to the shadow and spoke to his children: “Look at the shadow of that truck on the field, children. If you had to be run over, would you rather be run over by the truck or by its shadow?” The youngest child responded first, “The shadow. It couldn’t hurt anybody.” “That’s right,” said Barnhouse. “And remember, children, Jesus let the truck of death strike him, so that it could never destroy us. Mother lives with Jesus now—only the shadow of death passed over her.” Bryan Chappell - Christ Centered Preaching

How does understanding that Jesus took on the "truck of death" for us, allowing only the shadow of death to pass over, impact your perspective on death and the hope of eternal life?

Practical Application
Seeing Jesus like this, over and over, woos our hearts and draws us after Jesus. But we must respond. How do we respond practically?

    Renew your mind in God’s Word, letting truth replace the lies of the old self (Ephesians 4:22-24). Daily meditate on His sacrifice on the cross and His promises in the Word, and allow them to shape your thoughts, actions, and identity.
    Walk in resurrection power, walking on top of your previous sin habits just as Peter walked on top of the waves (Matthew 14:22-33). By God's grace, let His power enable you to overcome temptation and live victoriously in Him. Jesus is no longer in the grave, you no longer live in it!
    Rest in His finished work, ceasing from self-righteous striving (Hebrews 4:10). Trust that His grace is sufficient and that His work on the cross is all you need to stand righteous before God.

Our Shepherd’s burial was not the end—it was the beginning of our freedom. Live today as one whose grave has been left behind! 
Share your plan for renewing your mind, walking in resurrection power, and resting in the finished work of Christ today.

Day 73: The Glory of God in the Resurrection and Ascension of Christ
The King of Glory Returns to Heaven
We are looking at the glory of Christ in our study of Psalms 22, 23, and 24. Psalm 22 points to the death of Jesus, Psalm 23 His burial, and Psalm 24 His resurrection and ascension into heaven. 
Are you seeing Jesus in these Psalms? Seeing what He did for you? The length He went to rescue you, the depth He sank to save you? This is what sets us apart from habitual sin, seeing Jesus. Let's continue in Psalm 24:
Psalm 24
Theme: Jesus, the Risen and Reigning King of Glory
I. The Sovereign King Over Creation (Psalm 24:1-2) – Dominion

    His Possession – “The earth is the Lord’s and the fullness thereof” (Psalm 24:1). To apply this, think of what you might need, and realize the earth is the Lord's, and everything in it, so He can provide you what you feel is missing. Jesus, the risen Lord, has all authority (Matthew 28:18) and owns all things.

    His Power – “He has founded it upon the seas and established it upon the rivers” (Psalm 24:2). Jesus, the Creator, founded the earth, and He is the Sustainer, upholding all things by the power of His Word (Colossians 1:16-17). He purposefully uses His power for you, in rescuing you from sin and creating in you a new heart that follows and obeys Him.

II. The Sanctified King Leading His People (Psalm 24:3-6) 

    The Question of Access – “Who shall ascend the hill of the Lord?” (Psalm 24:3).No sinful man can approach God apart from Christ (Romans 3:23).

    The Qualification of Holiness – “He who has clean hands and a pure heart” (Psalm 24:4). The Jews of old read this qualification and set out to clean their hands (what they did) and their hearts (what they loved). If you've ever thought, yes I need to clean up my life, get sober, live better, etc. please remember that only Jesus, the sinless One, meets this requirement (Hebrews 4:15). He alone ascended to His Father. But the good news is, He had you and I in Him! "Since, then, you have been raised with Christ, set your hearts on things above, where Christ is, seated at the right hand of God. 2 Set your minds on things above, not on earthly things. 3 For you died, and your life is now hidden with Christ in God" (Colossians 3:1-3). 

    The Blessing of Righteousness – “He will receive blessing from the Lord and righteousness from the God of his salvation” (Psalm 24:5). Look at Jesus rising from the dead, for the grave had no hold on Him. Through His resurrection, and ascension to the Father, He gives us His Spirit and His righteousness (2 Corinthians 5:21). Praise the Lord!

III. The Supreme King Entering His Throne Room (Psalm 24:7-10)

    The Call to Open the Gates – “Lift up your heads, O gates! And be lifted up, O ancient doors, that the King of glory may come in” (Psalm 24:7). This pictures Jesus returning to heaven after His resurrection (Acts 1:9-11). The gates were opened to Him because He suffered death for our sins and accomplished all the Father set out for Him to do.

    The Conquering King – “Who is this King of glory? The Lord, strong and mighty, the Lord, mighty in battle” (Psalm 24:8). Jesus defeated sin, Satan, and death at the cross (1 Corinthians 15:54-57).

    The Triumphant Entry – “The Lord of hosts, He is the King of glory!” (Psalm 24:10). The risen Christ was welcomed into heaven by the angels and the redeemed (Revelation 5:9-10).

Conclusion:
Psalm 24 is a resurrection and ascension Psalm, revealing Jesus as:

    The Creator King who owns all things (Psalm 24:1-2).
    The Righteous King who grants us access to God (Psalm 24:3-6).
    The Triumphant King who returned to glory in victory (Psalm 24:7-10).

Let us worship, follow, and proclaim our risen, reigning, and returning King of Glory!
How does seeing Jesus as the Creator, Righteous, and Triumphant King in Psalm 24 impact the way you live and worship Him?

Illustration: The Vision of Isaiah and the King of Glory
Isaiah, a prophet of God, had a life-changing vision in Isaiah 6. He saw the Lord seated on His throne, high and lifted up, with the train of His robe filling the temple (Isaiah 6:1). Angelic beings cried, “Holy, holy, holy is the Lord of hosts; the whole earth is full of His glory!” (Isaiah 6:3).
At that moment, Isaiah was overwhelmed by his unworthiness, crying, “Woe is me! For I am lost; for I am a man of unclean lips, and I dwell in the midst of a people of unclean lips; for my eyes have seen the King, the Lord of hosts!” (Isaiah 6:5). He knew that no one could stand before the King of Glory in sin.
But God, in His grace, provided a solution. One of the seraphim touched Isaiah’s lips with a lump of burning coal, declaring, “Your guilt is taken away, and your sin atoned for” (Isaiah 6:7).
This vision foreshadows Jesus, the true King of Glory, who not only ascended to heaven but also came down to cleanse sinners. Through His death and resurrection, He made a way for us to enter God’s presence, not in fear, but in righteousness (2 Corinthians 5:21, Hebrews 10:19-22).
How does Isaiah’s vision of the holy, exalted King of Glory and his cleansing by God's grace point to the work of Jesus in providing a way for us to stand before God righteous and free from fear?

Practical Application: Beholding the Glory of King Jesus
Just as Isaiah was transformed by seeing the King of Glory, we, too, are changed as we behold Jesus. How do we do this?

    Meditate on Christ’s Word – “We all, with unveiled face, beholding the glory of the Lord, are being transformed” (2 Corinthians 3:18).
    Worship the Risen King – Fix your heart on Jesus, the victorious and ascended Lord (Revelation 5:12).
    Proclaim His Glory – Share the gospel, declaring, “The King of glory has come!” (Matthew 28:19-20).

As we contemplate His glory, He sanctifies, sustains, and strengthens us for His kingdom!
How does focusing on Christ’s glory impact your daily life and growth in Him?

Day 74: Jesus Became Poor To Make Us Rich
The Poverty of Jesus = The Wealth of His People
Key Passage
For you know the grace of our Lord Jesus Christ, that though He was rich, yet for your sake He became poor, so that you through His poverty might become rich.
2 Corinthians 8:9
Teaching
We Are Rich!
One of the universal problems of all humans is the feeling of emptiness, poverty, and lack, and it is one of the major reasons we turn to sinful things. We are trying to be full, satisfied, and rich. That is why understanding 2 Corinthians 8:9 is critical to helping us grow past turning to empty things to fill us. 
Imagine a wealthy king, clothed in splendor, seated on a throne of gold, with endless resources at His command. Servants rush to fulfill His every desire, and His palace overflows with treasures beyond measure. Now picture that same king willingly stepping down, trading his royal robes for rags, his feasts for famine, his riches for ruin, in order to make all the subjects of his kingdom wealthy. This is exactly what Jesus did for us (Philippians 2:6-7).
Jesus, the King of Glory, had everything—perfect unity with the Father (John 17:5), the worship of angels (Isaiah 6:1-3), and the limitless wealth of heaven (Psalm 50:10-12). But in an act of unimaginable grace, He emptied Himself, becoming a humble man (Luke 2:7). Born in a stable, raised as a carpenter, walking dusty roads with no place to lay His head (Matthew 8:20)—Jesus embraced poverty so that we might inherit true riches.
Nowhere is His poverty more evident than at the cross. Stripped of His garments (John 19:23-24), abandoned by His friends (Mark 14:50), mocked, beaten, and nailed to a piece of wood (Isaiah 53:3-5), Jesus had nothing. Not even the comfort of the Father’s presence, as He cried out, "My God, My God, why have You forsaken Me?" (Matthew 27:46; Psalm 22:1). He gave up everything so we could gain everything.
And what are these riches He offers? They are far greater than gold or silver (1 Peter 1:18-19). Through His poverty, we receive the wealth of grace—sins forgiven, washed clean by His blood (Ephesians 1:7). We gain the riches of love as He adopted us as sons and daughters of God (1 John 3:1). We inherit the treasures of truth, His Word lighting our path (Psalm 119:105). And above all, we receive the eternal riches of life forever in His presence, where no thief can steal and no moth can destroy (Matthew 6:19-20).
The world chases after material wealth, filling their barns while their souls remain empty (Luke 12:16-21). I lived like this for many years, always chasing after the physical, the material, the temporary, like some wicked prince who would rather give up his place in Paradise than his one in Paris. 
But we, as followers of Christ, now have a different kind of wealth—a treasure that never fades (2 Corinthians 4:18). Jesus gave up everything so that we could be rich beyond measure.
Take a moment today to thank Him, as I am doing in my heart right now. You are not spiritually bankrupt. You are not impoverished in grace. You are rich in Christ! Walk in that wealth, knowing that your riches are eternal and your inheritance is secure (Romans 8:17). 
You do not need alcohol or cigarettes to fill your emptiness, you are full in Christ (Colossians 2:10). You don't need porn or impurity, which only bankrupts you. You don't need excess food, you have Jesus the Bread of Life, and you don't need anything when you have everything. "The Lord is my Shepherd I shall not want" means Jesus is all I need!
Prayer: Lord Jesus, thank You for giving up everything to make me rich in grace, love, truth, and eternal life. Help me treasure these riches above all else and live in gratitude for all You have given. Amen.
What does 2 Corinthians 8:9 teach us about true riches and how to overcome emptiness?

Biblical Illustration: Moses
Consider Moses, the prince of Egypt who became a wanderer (Hebrews 11:24-26). He had the wealth of Pharaoh’s palace, the luxuries of royalty, and a life of ease. Yet, by faith, he chose to give it all up, identifying instead with the enslaved people of Israel. He turned away from worldly riches, choosing instead to suffer for the sake of God’s people.
For years, Moses wandered the desert, shepherding flocks instead of ruling a kingdom (Exodus 3:1). He had nothing but a staff in his hand, yet through God’s power, that staff would part the Red Sea, bring water from a rock, and lead an entire nation to freedom (Exodus 14:21, 17:6). Moses exchanged earthly wealth for spiritual riches—becoming a servant of the living God, leading His people to the promised land.
This is what you and I need to do: exchange earthly wealth (that is, false gods that promise satisfaction) for true spiritual riches. "Do not work for food that spoils, but for food that endures to eternal life, which the Son of Man will give you. For on him God the Father has placed his seal of approval" (John 6:27). 
This illustration of Moses is a true reflection of Jesus Christ, who left the glory of heaven to humble Himself as a servant, leading us out of the slavery of sin and into the riches of His grace (Philippians 2:8-9). Just as Moses forsook Egypt for something greater, Jesus gave up everything to bring us into an eternal inheritance. In Christ, we have riches far beyond what this world or Satan can offer.
How does Moses giving up earthly wealth for spiritual riches help us understand what Jesus did for us?

Practical Application
Since Christ gave up everything for us, let us respond by living in the riches He provides. This means not seeking out something we think we lack on our own terms, not turning to empty things, but instead rejoicing in the eternal inheritance and full riches purchased for us by Jesus. As we reflect on His great sacrifice, we are reminded that we are spiritually rich, and nothing in this world can compare to the treasures He has freely given us.
Having all we need, we can:

    Be rich in generosity, giving to those in need (2 Corinthians 9:7). 
    Be rich in good works, serving others as Jesus served (Galatians 5:13). 
    Treasure God’s Word daily, storing up the wealth of truth in your heart (Colossians 3:16). 
    Walk as heirs of the kingdom, knowing that your true wealth is in Christ, not in this fading world (Matthew 6:33).
    Above all, proclaim the riches of the gospel, sharing the good news of Christ’s sacrifice and resurrection (Romans 10:15). 

How can you live today in the richness of Christ, focusing on eternal wealth rather than temporary, empty pursuits?

Day 75: Sin Steals, Jesus Gives
Sin and Satan Steal All, Jesus Stuffs Full
Key Passage
The thief comes only to steal and kill and destroy; I came that they may have life, and have it abundantly.
John 10:10
Teaching
Sin Takes, Jesus Gives
 John 10:10-11
Yesterday, we looked at the riches that are ours by way of Jesus becoming poor for us. Knowing our riches and where they are can keep us from looking for them in all the wrong places. Today we will see a powerful contrast between what sin and Satan promise and what they actually provide, vs. what Jesus promises and provides.
Sin is a thief (John 10:1). It creeps in like a shadow at night, promising freedom but locking us in chains (Psalm 107:10-11). It whispers of pleasure but hands us poison. You've no doubt experienced this in your own life as I have. Satan, the ultimate thief, has been conning humanity since the Garden of Eden (Genesis 3:1-6), luring us with the fruit of rebellion, only to rob us of peace, joy, and life itself (John 8:44).
Here are three ways Satan steals from us:

    He steals our peace (Isaiah 48:22). Like a pickpocket in a crowded street, sin robs us of rest. Guilt gnaws, shame lingers, and the past haunts (Psalm 38:4).
    He kills our joy (John 10:10). What starts as thrilling turns sour. The temporary high of sin leaves behind an empty pit, a hunger that never satisfies (John 4:13).
    He destroys our future (Galatians 6:7-8). Sin is a wrecking ball, demolishing relationships, reputations, and hope. It promises gold but delivers gravel (James 1:15).

Yet, in the darkness of our loss, Jesus steps in.
John 10:11 – “I am the good shepherd; the good shepherd lays down His life for the sheep.”
Jesus doesn’t just offer life—He dies to give it to us (Romans 5:8). Imagine a shepherd in the hills, watching over his sheep. When the wolves come, a hired hand flees (John 10:12). But the Good Shepherd stands firm, placing Himself between the flock and the fangs (Isaiah 53:7). At the cross, Jesus took every bite of sin’s destruction, every claw of Satan’s accusation, every wound we deserved (1 Peter 2:24).
Here are three ways Jesus stuffs us full of life:

    He fills us with peace (John 14:27). His forgiveness wipes away guilt, like a fresh snowfall covering the muddy ground (Isaiah 1:18).
    He floods us with joy (Romans 15:13). No more counterfeit highs—Jesus is the fountain of living water, satisfying the soul’s deepest thirst (John 7:37-38).
    He secures our future (1 Peter 1:3-5). Instead of destruction, He prepares a home for us, where love, laughter, and life never fade (John 14:2-3).

Sin takes. Jesus gives. The thief drains us dry, but Christ overflows our cup (Psalm 23:5). Run to the Good Shepherd today and experience the fullness of life He purchased with His blood (Acts 20:28).
How does the contrast between what sin and Satan promise versus what Jesus provides highlight the difference between destruction and abundant life?

Biblical Illustration: Judas
Judas Iscariot: The Tragic Theft of Sin and the Abundant Life He Missed
Judas Iscariot is a chilling example of John 10:10 in action. He walked with Jesus, listened to His teachings, and witnessed His miracles. But sin crept in like a silent thief, whispering promises of wealth and power. Greed took root in his heart, and for thirty pieces of silver, which was about four months’ wages, he sold out the Son of God.
Sin stole from Judas in three ways: it stole his peace (he was tormented by guilt), it killed his joy (his reward brought him no happiness), and it destroyed his future (he ended his own life in despair). Sin dangled riches before him but left him bankrupt in the soul. The thief came to steal, kill, and destroy—and Judas was his prey.
Yet, standing in contrast is the Good Shepherd, who laid down His life even for traitors like Judas and deniers like Peter. If only Judas had run to Jesus, as Peter did, instead of to the priests, if only he had believed that the cross was for sinners like him, he could have found the life Jesus came to give. The gospel tells us that no betrayal is too great, no sin too deep—Jesus gives life even where Satan has stolen everything.
What do you learn from the life of Judas?

Practical Application
When we feel the thief of sin lurking, we must remember that Jesus came to restore what sin steals, to give life (John 10:10), and to turn to Him instead. Sin seeks to rob us of peace, joy, and fulfillment, leaving us empty and searching for more.
First, repent—acknowledge the emptiness sin brings and turn to Jesus for forgiveness (1 John 1:9). Recognize that true freedom comes not from what we pursue in sin but from what we receive through grace.
Second, believe in the fullness of life He offers, trusting that His death on the cross was enough to heal our brokenness (Isaiah 53:5; John 3:16). His sacrifice is the ultimate demonstration of love, showing us that no matter how far we've fallen, His grace is sufficient to restore us.
Finally, choose to abide in Him daily through prayer, Scripture, and worship (John 15:5; Colossians 3:16). By staying connected to Him, we find our strength, purpose, and the joy that sin can never offer.
As we fix our eyes on Jesus, the Good Shepherd, we will experience peace, joy, and a secure future (Philippians 4:7; Romans 15:13), knowing His gospel gives us everything sin tries to take (2 Corinthians 5:17).
What specific area of your life do you need to surrender to Jesus today, trusting that He alone can restore and fulfill you?

Day 76: The Abundant Life
Jesus Gives Life to the Full
Key Passage
The thief comes only to steal and kill and destroy. I came that they may have life and have it abundantly.
John 10:10
Teaching
The Abundant Life
In the beginning, Adam and Eve turned to Satan's offer of an abundant life. They thought the forbidden fruit looked beautiful, that it would make them wise, they would become like God, and that it would fill them up and satisfy them. They believed in a lie, for their one act of disobedience brought loss, ruin, and death to all people. You can imagine them standing over the grave of their youngest son, Abel, sobbing and weeping, and thinking "What have we done? If only we had not sinned our son would still be alive!" 
Jesus, on the other hand, spoke of an abundant life—one overflowing with His presence, power, and purpose. His offer is true and real, it is life to the full with no aftertaste of guilt, shame, and death. What are you turning to? The lie of Satan? Or the provision of abundant life in Jesus?
In Ezekiel 47:7-12, we find a stunning picture of this abundant life, portrayed as a river flowing from the temple, bringing healing and fruitfulness. Let’s explore four aspects of this abundant life in Christ.
1. The River’s Source: Life from God’s Presence (Ezekiel 47:7-8)
Ezekiel sees a river flowing from the temple, its waters growing deeper and stronger. This river represents the life-giving power of God, flowing from His presence (Psalm 36:8-9). Just as a spring-fed river transforms a barren land into a lush paradise, so the Holy Spirit, poured out through Jesus, brings spiritual life where there was once dryness and death (John 7:38-39). Without Christ, our souls are like cracked earth, incapable of sustaining life. But when we come to Him, His Spirit floods our hearts with peace, joy, and righteousness (Romans 14:17).
2. The Healing Waters: Restoration in Christ (Ezekiel 47:8-9)
The river does not remain stagnant; it moves outward, transforming everything in its path. Even the Dead Sea—so salty that nothing can survive in it—is healed by these waters. This is a picture of the gospel’s power to restore even the most broken lives (Isaiah 55:1-3). Like the woman at the well, whose past was a wasteland of sin, we also are renewed by the living water of Christ (John 4:14) like she was. His grace turns spiritual deserts into gardens of grace (Isaiah 35:1-2).
3. The Abundant Harvest: Fruitfulness in Christ (Ezekiel 47:10, 12)
Fishermen stand by the river, casting their nets and drawing in an abundance of fish—symbolizing the life-giving mission of the gospel (Matthew 4:19). Trees grow along the banks, producing fruit every month. This is the result of abiding in Christ (John 15:5). A life connected to Jesus will overflow with love, joy, peace, and good works (Colossians 1:10). Just as trees draw nourishment from the river, so we must root ourselves in Christ, allowing His Word to nourish and sustain us (Psalm 1:3).
4. The Unfailing Provision: Eternal Life in Christ (Ezekiel 47:12)
The leaves of these trees are for healing, their fruit never fails. This points to Jesus, the true source of eternal life (John 6:35). His sacrifice on the cross was the ultimate healing for our sin-sick souls (1 Peter 2:24). The abundant life He gives is not temporary but everlasting—an eternal, ever-flowing river of grace (Revelation 22:1-2).
Are you thirsty for true life? Then absolutely ignore the lies, deception, scheming, and plotting of Satan, and instead come to Jesus, and let His river of grace flood your soul, bringing healing, fruitfulness, and eternal abundance (Isaiah 58:11).
What are the key characteristics of the abundant life that Jesus offers, as illustrated in Ezekiel 47:7-12?

Biblical Illustration: Abraham
Genesis 25:8 tells us that Abraham "breathed his last and died in a good old age, an old man and full of years." Interestingly, the phrase "of years" is supplied by translators; the original text simply states that Abraham died "old and full." The Hebrew word "full" (sabea) means satisfied, satiated, enriched, and content. His life was not merely long but full—full of God’s blessings, full of purpose, full of faith (Hebrews 11:8-10).
This is what it means to live the abundant life that Jesus gives. Abraham’s satisfaction did not come from material wealth or human success, but from walking with God (Genesis 17:1-2). From the moment he left Ur to follow God's calling, Abraham’s life was marked by faith, trust, and obedience. He experienced trials, failures, and victories, but through it all, he walked with the Lord and found true strength in Him (Romans 4:20-21).
The gospel of Jesus Christ is the key to this same fullness, Jesus having emptied Himself on the cross for the specific purpose of filling us (Philippians 2:7). Without Him, life is empty, no matter how much we possess (Ecclesiastes 2:11). But as we come to Christ every day, we find a peace that surpasses understanding (Philippians 4:7), joy that circumstances cannot shake (Habakkuk 3:17-18), and a satisfaction that money and success can never provide (Psalm 63:5). Just as Abraham’s life ended in fullness, so can ours—if we drink deeply from the river of life found in Jesus (Revelation 7:16-17).
How can we experience the fullness of life that Abraham had, through faith in Jesus Christ?

Practical Application
To live in emptiness and loss, simply look at the temptation presented to you, give in, and follow the devil, do that over and over until you are a captive with no repentance. You'll come to the end of your life having lost it all. But if you want the fullness of Christ’s abundant life, you must actively walk with Him daily, learning to reject temptation in preference for following Jesus. Here are practical steps rooted in Scripture:

    Drink deeply from God’s Word – Meditate on Scripture daily, hourly (Psalm 1:2-3; Joshua 1:8). This is what the Spirit uses to bring the river of life to us.
    Remain in Christ through biblical prayer – Stay connected to Jesus in constant communion (1 Thessalonians 5:17; John 15:7), praying His Word back to Him (John 15:7).
    Walk in obedience – Follow His commands, trusting in His Word, His will, and His ways (John 14:15; Deuteronomy 10:12-13).
    Share the gospel – Be a witness of Christ’s sacrifice, and His life-giving resurrection power (Matthew 28:19-20; Acts 1:8).

True abundance is found in Jesus. Seek Him daily, and you will be satisfied, enriched, and full, enjoying true pleasures for all of eternity (Psalm 16:11)!
What daily habits help you walk in the fullness of Christ’s abundant life?

Day 77: Sin Brings Wrath and Ruin
Sin Causes Total Devastation
Key Passage
What misery is mine! I am like one who gathers summer fruit at the gleaning of the vineyard; there is no cluster of grapes to eat, none of the early figs that I crave.
Micah 7:1
Sin Brings Wrath, Ruin, Captivity, and Destruction
Micah 7:1-6
Have you found that your sin has brought about severe consequences? That you can look back at great pain because of it? Has it left you confused and aghast because of what it has brought to your life? I have, and so has every sinner to have ever lived, to one degree or another. That's what Micah 7 is all about. It follows this statement from the Lord in chapter 6:
"Therefore, I have begun to destroy you, to ruin you because of your sins. 14 You will eat but not be satisfied; your stomach will still be empty. You will store up but save nothing, because what you save I will give to the sword. 15 You will plant but not harvest; you will press olives but not use the oil, you will crush grapes but not drink the wine" (Micah 6:13-15).
Micah paints this tragic picture of sin’s devastation. It’s as if a farmer walks into his vineyard, expecting ripe, juicy grapes, only to find shriveled vines and empty branches (Micah 7:1; Isaiah 5:1-2). His heart sinks, for what should be a feast is a famine. Sin promises a harvest but always leaves us empty (Galatians 6:7-8), ruined and devastated. 
1. Sin Brings Wrath – A Shattered Society
"Woe is me! For I have become as when the summer fruit has been gathered, as when the grapes have been gleaned: there is no cluster to eat, no first-ripe fig that my soul desires. The godly has perished from the earth, and there is no one upright among mankind; they all lie in wait for blood, and each hunts the other with a net" (Micah 7:1-2).
Micah laments that the godly have vanished, and the land is filled with bloodshed and corruption (Micah 7:2-3; Psalm 14:3). Like a storm tearing through a city, leaving broken homes and shattered lives, sin brings God’s righteous wrath because it destroys everything good (Romans 1:18). Have you ever watched or experienced sin unraveling a friendship? A marriage? A family? I've experienced it firsthand because of my sin and others. The devastation is real, not just in Micah’s time but in ours.
2. Sin Brings Ruin – A World Turned Upside Down
"Their hands are on what is evil, to do it well; the prince and the judge ask for a bribe, and the great man utters the evil desire of his soul; thus they weave it together. The best of them is like a brier, the most upright of them a thorn hedge. The day of your watchmen, of your punishment, has come; now their confusion is at hand" (Micah 7:3-4).
The prophet describes a society where even trusted relationships crumble: neighbors deceive, friends betray, and families turn against each other (Micah 7:5-6; Jeremiah 9:4-5). It’s like a house infested with termites—what seemed strong begins to collapse from within. Sin erodes trust, love, and peace, leaving only rubble (Isaiah 59:2-4). Jesus Himself quoted Micah 7:6 when warning His disciples that the gospel would expose the sinfulness of men’s hearts and divide even families (Matthew 10:34-36).
3. Sin Brings Captivity and Destruction – A Prison Without Bars
"Put no trust in a neighbor; have no confidence in a friend; guard the doors of your mouth from her who lies in your arms; for the son treats the father with contempt, the daughter rises up against her mother, the daughter-in-law against her mother-in-law; a man’s enemies are the men of his own house" (Micah 7:5-6).
"Therefore I will give you over to ruin and your people to derision; you will bear the scorn of the nations" (Micah 6:16).
Micah’s words echo Jesus' warning—sin divides homes and enslaves hearts (John 8:34). It leads not to freedom but to chains, and to ruin and derision (being mocked). Think of a man who once walked freely but now drags heavy shackles behind him, bound by guilt, regret, and slavery to sin (Romans 6:16). That’s the captivity of sin. It promises freedom but delivers death and destruction (Proverbs 14:12).
Anyone in this situation feels tremendous regret, intense shame, thorough humiliation, and a very real loss of hope. It's as if he or she is just sitting alone in darkness (Micah 7:8) and despair. We long for help from the Lord: for vindication, for restoration, for hope.
And there is hope in this condition, for which I'm so thankful or I'd still be sitting hopelessly in the darkness as I did for years. Notice how it is presented in Micah 7:
The Answer: Look to the Lord!
Micah 7:7 shines like dawn breaking through a long, dark night: 
"But as for me, I will watch expectantly for the Lord; I will wait for the God of my salvation. My God will hear me."
Amid all the loss and destruction around him, as he sits in the darkness, Micah exercises faith! He watches and waits for the Lord, knowing that God will hear him, advocate for him, vindicate him, and restore him. 
This is our only hope. The Lord hears our cries to Him and He will help us, no matter how far our sin took us! Through repentance we will see that where sin brings wrath, Jesus bore it for us on the cross (1 Peter 2:24) and removed it from us (1 Thessalonians 1:10). Where sin ruins, Jesus restores (Joel 2:25). Where sin enslaves, Jesus sets captives free (Luke 4:18). He is the Messiah who died, rose, and will vindicate all who trust in Him (Romans 8:33-34).
So, watch and wait for the Lord! Though sin has left destruction in its wake, Jesus Christ is the Redeemer who brings beauty from ashes (Isaiah 61:1-3). We'll see this laid out very clearly in tomorrow's devotional.
How can we turn to the Lord for hope and restoration when we experience the devastating consequences of sin?

Biblical Illustration: Manasseh
A strong biblical illustration of this teaching is found in the life of King Manasseh (2 Chronicles 33:1-20). Although I've used his life before, I want to show it in a different context. His sin brought such deep devastation and destruction to himself and the nation of Israel, but just look at the restoration! 
Manasseh, son of Hezekiah, became king of Judah and plunged the nation into deep sin. He tore down the altars of God and replaced them with idols, filled Jerusalem with bloodshed, and even sacrificed his own sons to false gods (2 Chronicles 33:1-6). Like the people in Micah 7, he led the nation into destruction, where sin poisoned families, corrupted leaders, and turned society upside down.
Because of his rebellion, God’s wrath fell—the Assyrians invaded, captured Manasseh, and led him away in chains with a hook in his nose (2 Chronicles 33:11). Here is sin’s captivity in full force: a king who once sat on a throne now bound in a foreign prison, enslaved by the consequences of his sin.
Yet, in his distress, Manasseh turned to the Lord in humble repentance (2 Chronicles 33:12-13). And God, in His mercy, restored him! Manasseh was freed and returned to his throne, spending the rest of his days removing idols and rebuilding the worship of God, restoring the nation of Israel to sanity and right living.
This is the gospel: Sin brings wrath, ruin, and captivity—but Jesus died on the cross to bring mercy, restoration, and freedom to all who turn to Him (Ephesians 2:4-5, Colossians 1:13-14). If you find yourself in a cycle of devastation cry out to God, watch and wait for the Lord—He is mighty to save!
What does the story of King Manasseh teach us about sin, repentance, and God's mercy?

Practical Application
Turning from Sin and Looking to Christ

    Examine Your Life – Ask God to reveal any sin that is bringing destruction (Psalm 139:23-24). Like Manasseh, acknowledge where sin has led you.
    Repent and Turn to the Lord – Confess your sin and trust in Christ’s forgiveness (1 John 1:9). Repentance is the path to restoration.
    Watch and Wait for the Lord – Trust in Jesus daily, seeking His strength and deliverance (Micah 7:7).
    Walk in Obedience – Remove idols, pursue holiness, and restore what sin has broken (2 Corinthians 7:1).
    Rest in the Gospel – Jesus bore our wrath, freed us from captivity, and restores all who trust in Him (Ephesians 2:4-5).

Today's lesson showed us the extent of the devastation and loss brought about by sin. Tomorrow will show us the extent of grace and restoration that come over time for all who put their faith in Jesus. For as we sing at Christmas time:
He comes to make His blessings flow
Far as the curse is found
Far as the curse is found
Joy to the World by Issac Watts
What is one specific step you can take today to turn away from sin and walk in greater obedience to Christ?

Day 78: Jesus Is Our Hope In The Darkness
Jesus Brings Hope and Restoration
Key Passage
Rejoice not over me, O my enemy; when I fall, I shall rise; when I sit in darkness, the Lord will be a light to me.
Micah 7:8
Teaching
Micah 7:8-13
In a world shattered by sin and its consequences, when devastation surrounds us, we might feel there’s no way out. We can't undo what we've done, we can't make things right or change our past; we simply can't unscramble the egg. It can lead to despair and desperation.
But here is hope. Jesus is our hope in the darkness, the light that pierces through despair. He really does rescue, vindicate, and raise us up, even after our sins have devastated our lives.
Look at the five ways He brings hope to those who have faced destruction, as seen in Micah 7:8-13.
1. Jesus Offers Hope in THE MIDST OF Our Shame (Micah 7:8)
"Rejoice not over me, O my enemy; when I fall, I shall rise; when I sit in darkness, the Lord will be a light to me."
Sin brings shame, leaving us in darkness. Like a person stumbling in the night, trying to find their way, we often fall and experience shame amidst the consequences. But through repentance and forgiveness we experience the Lord rescuing us and delivering us from it all (Psalm 34:19). Jesus says to the brokenhearted, "When you fall, I will lift you up" (Psalm 34:18). In the deepest darkness of sin’s consequences, He is our light—the one who brings us out of despair and sets us on the path to restoration (John 8:12).
2. Jesus Is Our Advocate (Micah 7:9)
"I will bear the indignation of the Lord because I have sinned against him, until he pleads my cause and executes judgment for me. He will bring me out to the light; I shall look upon his vindication."
Micah confesses sin, but he knows the Lord will plead his case. Jesus is the ultimate advocate, standing before God in our place (1 John 2:1). He bore the wrath we deserve, taking our punishment so that we could be forgiven (Romans 3:25-26). He’s our defender, bringing us out of the darkness of judgment into the light of grace (Romans 8:34). Maybe we should all memorize Micah 7:9 and use it to fight the accuser!
3. Jesus Is Our Deliverer (Micah 7:10)
"Then my enemy will see, and shame will cover her who said to me, ‘Where is the Lord your God?’"
The enemies of our souls—sin, death, and shame—mock us, asking, "Where is your God now?" But when Jesus rose from the grave, He silenced all mocking (1 Corinthians 15:55). In Him, we have victory and vindication, and our enemies are defeated (Colossians 2:15). Jesus will put to shame the powers of sin and death and lift us into the light and make us strong in Him (Hebrews 2:14).
4. Jesus Restores What Sin Has Stolen (Micah 7:11)
"A day for the building of your walls; in that day the boundary shall be far extended."
Sin tears down what God has built—homes, relationships, trust. But Jesus restores what is broken, even when it feels impossible (Joel 2:25). Like a builder carefully reconstructing a ruined city, He restores peace and wholeness in our lives, extending the boundaries of His grace beyond what we could imagine (Isaiah 61:7). While we can't unscramble the egg, Jesus can make a delicious omelet out of our mess.
5. Jesus Brings Peace Amidst Chaos (Micah 7:12-13)
"In that day they will come to you, from Assyria and the cities of Egypt, and from Egypt to the river, from sea to sea and from mountain to mountain."
The world is filled with chaos, and our own sin brings chaos and upheaval. Yet Jesus offers us peace that passes understanding (Philippians 4:7). Where sin causes division, Jesus unites (Ephesians 2:14). He calls people from all nations to come and find peace in Him (Matthew 28:19). Jesus is our hope—He is the one who brings order to chaos, peace to unrest, and light to the darkness (John 14:27).
When darkness surrounds us, Jesus is our Hope, our Light, our Deliverer, and our Peace. Trust in Him; He will restore and redeem all that sin has broken. Don't let your circumstances dictate your condition, your Savior will vindicate and restore you, and extend your boundaries by His grace.
How does Micah 7:8-13 reveal that Jesus is our source of hope and restoration, even in the aftermath of sin?

Biblical Illustration: The Israelites in Babylon
The Babylonian exile was a time of immense devastation for Israel. Their cities were destroyed, the temple was burned, and many were taken captive. The sin of the nation, particularly the idolatry and rebellion against God, had led to this destruction (Jeremiah 25:8-11). In this dark time, the Israelites found themselves sitting in the rubble of their former glory, mourning their brokenness and captivity. They were under the weight of their sin and the consequences that followed, feeling abandoned and hopeless.
I've been right there in my own life. My sins seemed overwhelming, my life ruined, and my sin had seemingly won.
But God, in His mercy, had not abandoned them completely, nor me, nor you. Through the prophet Jeremiah, God promised restoration: “I will restore the fortunes of my people Israel, and they shall rebuild the ruined cities and inhabit them” (Jeremiah 33:10-11). This promise was ultimately fulfilled through the decree of King Cyrus of Persia, who allowed the Israelites to return to their land (Ezra 1:1-2). Despite the ruin, God brought them back to rebuild, providing them not only a return to their land but also an opportunity to rebuild their relationship with Him.
This restoration points us to Jesus Christ, the ultimate Restorer. Just as God restored Israel after their sin and devastation, Jesus brings restoration to all who trust in Him (Jeremiah 30:17-22). Jesus' death and resurrection open the door for us to experience forgiveness and be restored, no matter how deep our sins or how far we've fallen. He is the hope that leads us from darkness into light, from destruction to renewal. In Christ, all things are made new (Revelation 21:5).
How does God's restoration of Israel after exile point to the ultimate restoration all believers find in Jesus Christ?

Practical Application
If you find yourself in devastation as a result of your sin, here is what to do:

    Acknowledge the devastation of sin: Confess your sins before God and recognize the consequences (1 John 1:9).
    Repent and turn to God: Just as Israel repented and returned, we can repent and trust in Jesus for restoration (Acts 3:19).
    Seek God's restoration: Ask Jesus to rebuild what has been broken (2 Corinthians 5:17).
    Rest in Jesus’ work: Understand that Jesus is the ultimate Restorer, bringing healing, forgiveness, and peace (Colossians 1:19-20).
    Live in hope: Keep your eyes on Christ, knowing He will bring ultimate restoration and victory (Romans 8:18).

Know that you are not beyond God’s redemption—His grace is greater than your deepest failure. Even when sin leaves destruction in its wake, God specializes in bringing beauty from ashes (Isaiah 61:3). No matter how far you have fallen, He is willing and able to restore you.
In what area of your life do you need to trust God’s power to restore what sin has broken?

Day 79: Mercy Triumphs Over Judgment
God's Restoration of Sinners
Key Passage
Who is a God like you, pardoning iniquity and passing over transgression for the remnant of his inheritance? He does not retain his anger forever, because he delights in steadfast love. He will again have compassion on us; he will tread our iniquities underfoot. You will cast all our sins into the depths of the sea.
Micah 7:18-19
Teaching
Micah 7:14-20
Have you ever stood in the wreckage of your own failure, wondering if restoration is even possible? Have you felt like a ruined city, walls broken, defenses down, vulnerable and ashamed? Then hear this: God is a God of restoration. He delights in redeeming the ruined, rebuilding the broken, and reviving the weary. 
Let’s walk through Micah’s final words and see the overwhelming mercy of our God.
1. God Shepherds His People (Micah 7:14-15)
"Shepherd your people with your staff, the flock of your inheritance, who dwell alone in a forest in the midst of a garden land; let them graze in Bashan and Gilead as in the days of old. As in the days when you came out of the land of Egypt, I will show them marvelous things."
Micah prays for God to shepherd His people again. The people of Israel, ravaged by sin and judgment, are like lost sheep in a wilderness—isolated, wandering, desperate for a shepherd’s guidance (Ezekiel 34:5-6). And here is the hope: God answers this prayer in Jesus, the Good Shepherd (John 10:11). He gathers the lost (Luke 15:4-6), leads them to green pastures (Psalm 23:1-2), and restores what sin has scattered. This is the best news ever, for those who know their own depravity.
Just as God once delivered Israel from Egypt with signs and wonders (Exodus 14:21-22), He delivers His people through Jesus—the ultimate Exodus, rescuing sinners from sin and death (Colossians 1:13-14). The same God who parted the Red Sea now parts the sea of sin, making a way for us through the cross (Isaiah 43:16-17, 1 Corinthians 10:1-4).
2. God Humbles the Enemies of His People (Micah 7:16-17)
"The nations shall see and be ashamed of all their might; they shall lay their hands on their mouths; their ears shall be deaf. They shall lick the dust like a serpent, like the crawling things of the earth; they shall come trembling out of their strongholds; they shall turn in dread to the Lord our God, and they shall be in fear of you."
The enemies who once mocked God’s people will be silenced, brought low like serpents in the dust (Genesis 3:14, Isaiah 49:23). This is not just political conquest—this is the triumph of God over sin, Satan, and death (1 Corinthians 15:55-57).
The world that once opposed God will stand in awe of His power (Philippians 2:10-11). We see this in Jesus—through His cross and resurrection, He disarmed the powers of darkness, making a public spectacle of them (Colossians 2:15). Even demons tremble in His presence (James 2:19), and at the end of time, all who have stood against Him will bow in reverence (Psalm 86:9).
3. God Forgives and Restores Completely (Micah 7:18-20)
"Who is a God like you, pardoning iniquity and passing over transgression for the remnant of his inheritance? He does not retain his anger forever, because he delights in steadfast love. He will again have compassion on us; he will tread our iniquities underfoot. You will cast all our sins into the depths of the sea."
Here it is—the crescendo, the explosion of grace! Micah lifts his eyes in worship; where once he was overwhelmed by sin now he is overwhelmed by the goodness of God. Who is a God like this? Other gods demand payment, but our God made payment for us and now forgives us freely (Exodus 34:6-7). Other gods hold grudges, but our God delights in mercy (Psalm 103:8-12).
Picture it: God takes our sins and tramples them underfoot—crushing them like dust (Isaiah 43:25, Romans 16:20). Then, He casts them into the sea—not to be retrieved, not to wash up again on the shore, but to sink forever into the depths of His mercy (Psalm 103:12, Hebrews 8:12).
And how does He do this? Through Jesus! At the cross, our sins were crushed underfoot as Jesus took their weight upon Himself (Isaiah 53:5). Through His resurrection, our sins were flung into the abyss, never to condemn us again (Romans 8:1).
God keeps His promises (Micah 7:20, 2 Corinthians 1:20). He is faithful to Abraham and Jacob, just as He is faithful to us through Jesus Christ. So, let your heart rejoice! Worship the God who restores, forgives, and delights in steadfast love (Zephaniah 3:17).
Hallelujah! What a Savior!
What does Micah 7:14-20 reveal about God’s restoration, victory, and forgiveness through Jesus?

Biblical Illustration: Joseph's Brothers
Few people have stood in the ruins of their sin like Joseph’s brothers. Out of jealousy, they betrayed their own flesh and blood, selling Joseph into slavery and deceiving their father with a bloodied robe (Genesis 37:26-28). For years, they carried the guilt of their actions, watching their father grieve and fearing the consequences of what they had done. Their sin was a heavy chain around their necks, one they could never undo.
Then a famine struck. Forced to seek food in Egypt, they unknowingly stood before the very brother they had wronged—now exalted as ruler over the land (Genesis 42:6-8). Their sin had led them to ruin, but Joseph, reflecting the heart of God, offered them something astonishing: not vengeance, but restoration. He tested their hearts, then revealed himself, weeping as he declared, “You meant evil against me, but God meant it for good” (Genesis 50:20).
Their iniquities, like ours, deserved judgment. But in Joseph, they found mercy. And in Christ, we find something even greater: God trampling our sins underfoot, casting them into the sea, and welcoming us with open arms (Micah 7:18-19, Luke 15:20-24). This is the heart of the gospel—not just pardon, but full restoration.
Joseph was loved by his father but hated by his brothers. The father sent him on a mission of mercy to check on his brothers, but when they saw him coming they plotted his death. They eventually sold him into the hands of Gentiles for pieces of silver. While there he was falsely accused and placed right between two criminals; to one he brought a message of life, the other death. Finally, he was raised out of the dungeon and exalted to the right hand of Pharaoh where he became lord and savior to all who came to him.
What does the story of Joseph and his brothers teach us about God’s mercy, restoration, and sovereignty over sin?

Practical Application
Practical Application

    Confess your sins to God – He is faithful and just to forgive and cleanse us (1 John 1:9). Don’t hide in guilt; bring everything into the light. When we confess, we are not met with condemnation but with the loving embrace of a Father who longs to restore us.
    Trust in Christ’s full restoration – Just as Joseph’s brothers found mercy, we too find grace in Jesus, who forgives completely (Colossians 1:13-14). His restoration is not partial or temporary—it is complete, giving us a new identity in Him.
    Walk in newness of life – Since our sins are cast into the sea, we no longer live in shame but in the freedom of God’s love (Romans 6:4).
    Forgive others as God forgave you – Show the mercy you’ve received (Ephesians 4:32).
    Rejoice in God’s steadfast love – Worship the God who delights in saving sinners (Zephaniah 3:17).

How can you live in the freedom of God’s forgiveness today instead of carrying the weight of past mistakes?

Day 80: Run To Win, Fight To Dominate
Run Hard, Fight Hard
Key Passage
No, I strike a blow to my body and make it my slave so that after I have preached to others, I myself will not be disqualified for the prize.
1 Corinthians 9:27
Teaching
Run and Fight
1 Corinthians 9:24-27
At Setting Captives Free, we have taught the need to seek the Lord for a personalized battle plan—a God-given, tailor-made solution to our personal sin problems. Today, we will see that this personalized solution must include running to win and fighting to dominate.
Early in my Christian life I had no battle plan and I fought no fights, I lingered around temptation and I lounged in my sin. God had to teach me how to run from sin and fight to win. 
1 Corinthians 9:24 – “Do you not know that in a race all the runners run, but only one receives the prize? So run that you may obtain it.”
The Christian life is not a casual jog through the park; it is a marathon demanding full effort (Hebrews 12:1). We must run, not as those meandering aimlessly, but with intensity, focus, endurance, and a clear goal: to win! What is this prize? It is victory over sin (Romans 6:14), a life of holiness (1 Peter 1:15-16), and ultimately, Christ Himself (Philippians 3:8-14). Just as Olympic athletes push their bodies to the limit to receive a gold medal, we must strain forward in faith, pursuing Christ with all our might.
This is how we win the race. We do whatever it takes, whenever it requires, and use all means necessary to overcome. Be serious, be focused, be self-disciplined.
1 Corinthians 9:25 – “Every athlete exercises self-control in all things. They do it to receive a perishable wreath, but we an imperishable.”
Olympians deny themselves comfort, submit to strict training, control their appetites, and compete according to the rules, all for a crown that withers (2 Timothy 2:5). How much more should we, who run for an eternal reward (James 1:12; Revelation 2:10)? 
Through the gospel, Jesus has given us the Holy Spirit, who empowers us to say no to sin and yes to righteousness (Titus 2:11-12). Like a runner disciplines his diet and training, we must discipline ourselves in prayer (Colossians 4:2), Scripture (Psalm 119:11), and fellowship (Hebrews 10:24-25), fixing our eyes on Christ, the author and finisher of our faith (Hebrews 12:2).
1 Corinthians 9:26 – “So I do not run aimlessly; I do not box as one beating the air.”
Paul shifts metaphors to a boxing match. Imagine a fighter wildly swinging but never landing a punch—what a wasted effort! We must not merely flail against sin; we must land decisive, crushing blows. We do this by applying the gospel: believing ourselves dead to sin and alive to God (Romans 6:11), wielding the sword of the Spirit (Ephesians 6:17), and clinging to the victory of Christ’s cross (Colossians 2:14-15).
1 Corinthians 9:27 – “But I discipline my body and keep it under control, lest after preaching to others I myself should be disqualified.”
Paul “beats” his body into submission—not physically, but spiritually. Like a slave master ruling over his servants, he takes dominion over his fleshly desires, refusing to let them control him (Romans 8:13). We, too, must crucify the flesh with its passions (Galatians 5:24), choosing daily to walk in step with the Spirit (Galatians 5:16-17). Jesus has already conquered sin and death (1 Corinthians 15:55-57), and through Him, we are more than conquerors (Romans 8:37).
Maybe up to this point, you didn't know you had to run and fight. You thought "deliverance" meant Jesus did it all. Maybe you have clung to the truth that Jesus said, "It is finished" (John 19:30) but failed to see the other side of the coin that we are to run and fight. Both are true. 
Now is your chance to truly seek the Lord for a specific battle plan, so that you can run to win and fight to dominate. In Christ, the victory is yours, so run fast and fight hard.
What does 1 Corinthians 9:24-27 teach us about the discipline and effort required to run the race of faith and fight against sin?

Biblical Illustration: The Israelites and the Amalakites
In Exodus 17:8-13, the Israelites fought the Amalekites in the valley, but the victory was determined by what happened on the hill above them. Moses, Aaron, and Hur stood on the hilltop, and as long as Moses’ hands were raised, Israel prevailed. But when his arms grew weary and lowered, Amalek gained the advantage. So God made the outcome of the battle dependent on what that man in the middle did! Aaron and Hur supported Moses’ hands, ensuring they remained lifted, and Israel won the battle. 
This illustrates a profound truth: victory belongs to the Lord (Exodus 14:14), yet His people must still fight. Jesus, like Moses, stood in the gap for us. On a different hill, in the middle of two others, with arms stretched wide open on a cross, He won the decisive battle against sin and death (Colossians 2:14-15). Through excruciating (a word that means "out of the cross") pain, through bloody tears, through a broken heart, He hung under the wrath of God and declared “It is finished” (John 19:30), thereby forgiving your every sin and securing eternal victory for you.
Yet, just as the Israelites could not drop their swords, we must fight against sin and our natural weaknesses (Ephesians 6:12-13). Though Christ has already won, we are called to engage in the battle, taking up our cross daily (Luke 9:23) and putting to death the deeds of the flesh (Romans 8:13). Through Christ’s strength (Philippians 4:13), we run, we fight, and we overcome.
What can we learn from the account of Moses, Aaron, and Hur in Exodus 17:8-13 about fighting spiritual battles?

Practical Application
To run and fight effectively, we must take biblical actions that align with the gospel:

    Fix our eyes on Jesus (Hebrews 12:2), drawing strength from His finished work.
    Put on the full armor of God (Ephesians 6:10-18), standing firm in battle.
    Resist the devil (James 4:7) and actively flee from sin (2 Timothy 2:22).
    Encourage and uphold one another (Galatians 6:2), as Aaron and Hur supported Moses.
    Rely on the Spirit’s power (Romans 8:13), trusting that victory is in Christ alone.
    Stay rooted in God’s Word daily (Psalm 1:2-3), allowing His truth to equip and strengthen you.
    Walk in faith, not fear (2 Corinthians 5:7), knowing that God has already secured the ultimate victory.

The battle is already won in Jesus, yet we fight daily in His strength!
In what area of your life do you need to stand firm and fight in God’s strength today?

Day 81: Run and Fight
Run Hard Fight Hard
Run to Win, Fight to Dominate
Hebrews 12:1-4
At Setting Captives Free, we emphasize seeking the Lord for a personalized battle plan—a God-given, tailor-made solution to our personal sin struggles. But whatever our plan includes, one thing is certain: we must run like our lives depend on it and fight like warriors in the thick of battle. There is no passive Christianity. There is no room for half-measures. Today, we will see this truth in Hebrews 12:1-4.
Hebrews 12:1 – “Therefore, since we are surrounded by so great a cloud of witnesses, let us also lay aside every weight, and sin which clings so closely, and let us run with endurance the race that is set before us.”
Imagine a runner stepping onto the track wearing a heavy backpack and tangled in ropes. Ridiculous, right? Yet many Christians attempt to run the race of faith while carrying unrepented sin and worldly distractions. We must strip off everything that slows us down—whether it be secret sins (Psalm 66:18), destructive habits (Romans 13:12-14), or even seemingly harmless activities that dull our passion for Christ (Luke 21:34). Victory requires total commitment. Sin is a parasite that clings and chokes; cut it off and throw it away (Matthew 5:29-30)!
Hebrews 12:2 – “Looking to Jesus, the founder and perfecter of our faith, who for the joy that was set before him endured the cross, despising the shame, and is seated at the right hand of the throne of God.”
A runner fixes his gaze on the finish line, blocking out distractions. A soldier keeps his eyes on his commander, awaiting orders. We, too, must lock our focus onto Jesus. He is both our Savior and our example. When He carried His cross to Calvary, He was not meandering—He was marching to war (Colossians 2:15). When we struggle, we must remember: He endured the agony of the cross for the joy of rescuing us. If He pressed on through suffering, so must we. The gospel is both our motivation and our means (Philippians 1:6).
Hebrews 12:3 – “Consider him who endured from sinners such hostility against himself, so that you may not grow weary or fainthearted.”
Satan whispers, "It’s too hard. Give up." Our flesh whines, "It’s not worth it." But when we grow weak, we must consider Christ. He bore the venomous hatred of men. He endured mockery, beatings, and the wrath of God—all for our salvation (Isaiah 53:5). When the fight against sin feels brutal, remember that Jesus did not quit. And because He lives in us (Galatians 2:20), neither will we.
Hebrews 12:4 – “In your struggle against sin you have not yet resisted to the point of shedding your blood.”
Fighting sin is not a hobby—it is war. If sin is a wild beast, we must slay it (Genesis 4:7). If it is a fire, we must smother it (Romans 6:11-12). If it is an assassin, we must kill it before it kills us (Romans 8:13). The battle may be bloody, but we have not yet suffered as Christ did. So we fight on, knowing that our victory is certain in Him.
How does Hebrews 12:1-4 challenge us to run the race of faith with endurance and fight against sin with total commitment?

Biblical Illustration: Gideon's 300 Warriors
Biblical Illustration: Jehu’s Relentless Race
Jehu, the commander of Israel’s army, was a man who ran hard and fought fiercely. When God called him to rid Israel of Ahab’s wicked dynasty, he did not hesitate. He rode his chariot furiously, so much so that others recognized him by his reckless speed (2 Kings 9:20). Jehu ran with intensity, executing God’s judgment against Baal worship and Ahab’s house. He left no room for compromise, wiping out idolaters with ruthless determination (2 Kings 10:28).
Yet, for all his zeal, Jehu fell short. Though he destroyed Baal worship, he clung to Jeroboam’s golden calves (2 Kings 10:29). He ran fast but not far enough. He fought hard but not wholly for the Lord. His story warns us that religious fervor alone does not secure victory. Like Jehu, we can strive and fight yet still fall short if our eyes are not fixed on Jesus.
The gospel is the solution Jehu lacked. Christ, the true and perfect warrior, did not run only part of the race, He finished it. He fought sin to the death and secured eternal victory. In Him, we are empowered not only to run and fight but to persevere to the end (Philippians 1:6).
What does Jehu's life in 2 Kings 9 and 10 teach us about running our race?

Practical Application
To run and fight effectively, we must embrace biblical actions that are in line with the gospel:

    Throw off every hindrance (Colossians 3:5), cutting out sin without hesitation.
    Fix our eyes on Jesus (Hebrews 12:2), drawing strength from His sacrifice.
    Wield the sword of the Spirit (Ephesians 6:17), attacking sin with Scripture.
    Resist temptation fiercely (James 4:7), refusing to compromise.
    Endure suffering as discipline (Hebrews 12:11), knowing God uses trials to train us.
    Stand firm in faith (1 Corinthians 16:13), refusing to be shaken by the world’s pressures.
    Encourage one another daily (Hebrews 3:13), strengthening our brothers and sisters in the fight.

The victory is ours in Christ, but the battle rages on. So we run. So we fight. So we win!
What is one hindrance or sin you need to throw off today to run your race more effectively?

Day 82: Faith Is The Victory
Faith Overcomes
Key Passage
For everyone born of God overcomes the world. This is the victory that has overcome the world, even our faith. Who is it that overcomes the world? Only the one who believes that Jesus is the Son of God.
1 John 5:4-5
When I was young in the faith I remember hearing that faith would bring victory over sin, but I just couldn't understand it. I believed in Jesus, but I wasn't overcoming. This went on for many years. It wasn't until I learned how to cultivate and grow in my faith, that I began truly overcoming.
Then I started experiencing the reality that faith is the key that unlocks the door of victory. It is not a vague hope or a mere wish, or mere mental assent to the truth, but a living trust in Jesus Christ, the Son of God based on evidence He gives you in His Word (Hebrews 11:1). What I am discovering now is that faith is like a ship’s anchor in a storm—it holds us steady in the rough waters of temptation, trial, and spiritual warfare (Hebrews 6:19).
Faith Overcomes the World (1 John 5:4)
"for everyone born of God overcomes the world. This is the victory that has overcome the world, even our faith" (1 John 5:4).
The world is like a mighty current, pulling us toward sin, self, and empty pursuits that are. not from the Father (1 John 2:15-17). It tempts us with glittering distractions, promising fulfillment but delivering emptiness and vanity (Ecclesiastes 1:2). How do we swim against this tide? The answer is faith.
Faith is not self-generated; it is a gift from God (Ephesians 2:8). It comes through hearing the word of Christ (Romans 10:17), much like a fire is kindled by a spark that begins to burn within us for God's Word (Jeremiah 20:9). When we hear the good news of Jesus’ life, death, and resurrection, faith ignites in our hearts (Acts 16:31). And this faith, placed in Christ, is what overcomes the world (John 16:33).
Consider a soldier in battle. Without armor or weapons, he is defenseless. But faith arms us with the shield of God’s promises, the sword of His truth, and the helmet of salvation (Ephesians 6:16-17). We overcome the world, not by our own strength, but by holding fast to the One who has already conquered it (Zechariah 4:6), even as we come to understand that it is He who is holding fast to us (John 3:35, John 10:28,29).
Faith Overcomes Through Christ (1 John 5:5)
"Who is it that overcomes the world? Only the one who believes that Jesus is the Son of God" (1 John 5:5).
John asks, “Who is it that overcomes the world?” The answer is clear: only those who believe that Jesus is the Son of God (John 3:18). This belief is not mere head knowledge but a heart-trust, confidence that Jesus is our victorious King (Colossians 2:15) who has won the battle for "Mansoul," and has crucified our past life (Galatians 2:20) and raised us to new life (Colossians 3:1). 
Here's a free copy of "Holy War" by John Bunyan, showing how Jesus conquered "Mansoul."
The Holy Warpdf
Faith in Jesus lifts us above the world’s temptations, just as an eagle soars above a storm (Isaiah 40:31). It also enables us to overcome the flesh—the sinful desires that war against our souls (Galatians 5:16-17). Like a drowning man who stops struggling and grabs hold of a lifeline, faith clings to Christ, who gives us His Spirit to resist sin (Romans 8:13).
And faith defeats the devil, our adversary (1 Peter 5:8-9). When he accuses us and points out our failures, faith points to the cross, where Jesus paid for every sin (Colossians 2:13-14). Faith proclaims, “It is finished” (John 19:30).
The battle is real, but the victory is sure (2 Corinthians 2:14). Faith in Christ is the victory that overcomes the world, the flesh, and the devil. Let us trust in Him today and walk in triumph (Romans 8:37).
How does faith in Jesus Christ enable us to overcome the world, the flesh, and the devil, according to 1 John 5:4-5?

Biblical Illustration: Abraham
To experience spiritual power to overcome by faith, we simply find a promise of God and believe it! We hold to that promise, praying it back to God asking that He would keep His Word to us, and we move forward in faith. Abraham is an example of this very thing:
Abraham was an old man, well past the age of fathering children, and Sarah, his wife, was barren (Genesis 18:11). The world around him, and even his own body, screamed that God’s promise was impossible. Yet, against all hope, Abraham believed (Romans 4:18). His faith was not a blind leap, but a steady trust in the character of God and the promise of God—like a sailor trusting the stars to guide his path.
Years passed with no child. Doubts must have whispered in his ear, just as the world, the flesh, and the devil whisper to us today: “Did God really say?” (Genesis 3:1). But Abraham did not waver in unbelief; instead, he grew strong in faith, giving glory to God (Romans 4:20). He trusted that the God who promised was also able to perform it (Hebrews 11:11).
And God did. Sarah conceived, and Isaac was born—the child of promise (Genesis 21:1-3). But Abraham’s faith pointed beyond Isaac to Jesus Christ, the true Son of promise, who came so that all who believe might become children of God (Galatians 3:29). Like Abraham, we overcome not by sight, but by faith in Christ, the fulfillment of every promise (2 Corinthians 1:20).
What does Abraham's example teach us about overcoming challenges and doubts through faith in God's promises?

Practical Application
Just as Abraham overcame by faith, we too can live in victory by trusting in Christ. Here’s how:

    Believe God’s Word – Faith comes by hearing God’s promises (Romans 10:17). Read, meditate on, and trust Scripture daily. Let the truth of God’s Word anchor your heart and mind, especially when faced with trials and uncertainties.
    Fix Your Eyes on Jesus – The world distracts, but faith keeps its focus on Christ, the author and perfecter of our faith (Hebrews 12:2). In moments of doubt or temptation, remind yourself of Jesus' unwavering commitment to fulfill God’s plan for your life.
    Walk by Faith, Not by Sight – Trust God’s promises even when circumstances seem impossible (2 Corinthians 5:7). When things don’t make sense, take steps of obedience, knowing that God is faithful to guide you through every situation.
    Rest in Christ’s Finished Work – Victory is found not in our efforts, but in Christ’s completed salvation (John 19:30). Recognize that true peace comes from resting in what Christ has already accomplished, not from striving in your own strength.

In what area of your life do you need to shift your focus from your circumstances to God's promises, trusting Him to lead you to victory?

Day 83: The Power of Faith
Faith Makes Us Strong
Key Passage
Now faith is the assurance of things hoped for, the conviction of things not seen.
Hebrews 11:1
Teaching
Temptations are powerful, I think we would all agree. Satan knows our weaknesses and will constantly seek to exploit them. Our flesh is weak, so Satan and our flesh can work together to cast us down, cause us to live in the sin we hate, and keep us there longer than we want to stay.
So wouldn't you think that there must be a greater power that keeps us out of sin traps? That power is faith! Faith is believing that at the cross Jesus completely removed your sin, and at the resurrection He completely justified you before God. You believe this, you know you're right with God and you act on it. 
Faith is not wishful thinking or blind optimism. It is the rock-solid confidence that what God has promised will come to pass, even when we can’t yet see it (Romans 8:24-25). Faith is like a lighthouse on a stormy night—it does not remove the waves, but it gives us a sure guide through them. Hebrews 11, often called the "Hall of Faith," reminds us that faith in God has always been the key to overcoming obstacles.
Faith Moves Forward When the Path Is Unclear
"By faith Abraham obeyed when he was called to go out to a place that he was to receive as an inheritance. And he went out, not knowing where he was going."—Hebrews 11:8
Imagine stepping into the unknown, trusting only in God’s word (Genesis 12:1-4). Abraham did just that. He left his homeland without a roadmap, like a traveler who follows the North Star, trusting it will lead him the correct way. Faith moves us forward even when the path is unclear because it rests in the One who knows the way (Psalm 32:8, Proverbs 3:5-6).
Faith Overcomes Impossible Obstacles
"By faith the people crossed the Red Sea as on dry land, but the Egyptians, when they attempted to do the same, were drowned."—Hebrews 11:29
Faith is not limited by human ability (Exodus 14:13-16). When Israel stood before the Red Sea, there was no bridge, no boat, and no escape. But faith saw what human eyes could not—the power of God to make a way where there was none (Isaiah 43:16). Faith is like a key that unlocks doors we thought were permanently shut. It overcomes the impossible because it trusts in the God who makes all things possible (Matthew 19:26, Luke 1:37).
Faith Endures Hardships and Suffers for Christ
"Others suffered mocking and flogging, and even chains and imprisonment. … They were stoned, they were sawn in two, they were killed with the sword."—Hebrews 11:36-37
Faith does not guarantee an easy life (2 Timothy 3:12); rather, it gives us strength to endure. Like a tree bending but not breaking in the wind, faith holds firm even in suffering (James 1:2-4, 1 Peter 1:6-7). Many in Hebrews 11 suffered, yet they looked beyond this life to a greater reward—eternal life in Christ (2 Corinthians 4:17-18).
Faith Finds Its Fulfillment in Jesus
"Looking to Jesus, the founder and perfecter of our faith."—Hebrews 12:2
All the faith in Hebrews 11 pointed forward to Jesus (John 8:56, Galatians 3:8). He is the ultimate fulfillment of God’s promises (2 Corinthians 1:20). By faith in Him, we overcome every obstacle—sin, death, and Satan's power  (1 John 5:4-5, Colossians 2:15).
