Faith in Christ is the victory that carries us through every trial until we see Him face to face (1 Corinthians 13:12, Revelation 22:4).
What role does faith play in overcoming temptation, obstacles, and hardships, according to Hebrews 11?

Biblical Illustration: Joshua And The Walls of Jericho
Joshua stood before the towering walls of Jericho, an impenetrable fortress barring Israel’s way into the Promised Land (Joshua 6:1). Humanly speaking, victory was impossible. But God had given His word: “I have given Jericho into your hand” (Joshua 6:2). Faith had to act.
God’s battle plan seemed absurd; just march around the city for six days, then seven times on the seventh day, and shout (Joshua 6:3-5). No weapons, no siege engines, just faith in God's promise. Like stepping onto an unseen bridge, Israel obeyed, trusting that God would do what He said. And He did. At their shout, the walls collapsed, and Israel took the city (Joshua 6:20).
Sin is like the walls of Jericho and the gospel is our victory at Jericho. Sin, death, and the devil stand like mighty walls against us. We cannot break them down by effort or strength. But faith in Jesus brings victory. On the cross, Christ shattered sin’s power (Colossians 2:14-15). By faith, we walk in His triumph (1 Corinthians 15:57). Just as Israel marched by faith and saw the impossible happen, we trust in Christ, who alone brings us into the Promised Land of heaven and eternal life (John 14:6, Hebrews 4:9-10).
How does faith in God's promises lead to victory over obstacles and sin, as illustrated by the story of Jericho?

Practical Application
Faith is the key to victory. Like Joshua at Jericho, we must trust God's promises and act in faith. Here’s how:

    Find a promise of God in His Word and believe it! Then you’ll see the miraculous happen (Romans 4:20-21). God’s Word is filled with promises that are as powerful today as they were thousands of years ago. When we anchor our hearts in His truth, it gives us the courage to face any challenge. 
    Pray with faith, not fear. Bring your obstacles to God, trusting Him to move (Mark 11:22-24). When we pray in faith, we align our hearts with God’s power and open the door for Him to work in ways we can’t yet see.
    Obey even when it doesn’t make sense. Faith walks forward when sight says stop (James 2:17). Following God’s direction might feel counterintuitive at times, but He knows the best path for us and will lead us to victory.
    Fix your eyes on Jesus. He is the One who brings the victory (Hebrews 12:2, 1 Corinthians 15:57). When we focus on Christ, we are reminded that He has already won the ultimate victory and will continue to fight for us.

How can you act in faith today, trusting God's promises and following His guidance, even when the path ahead seems unclear?

Day 84: The Power of the Gospel
The Gospel Is More Than Words
Key Passage
Because our gospel came to you not simply with words, but also with power, with the Holy Spirit and deep conviction.
1 Thessalonians 1:5
Teaching
1 Thessalonians 1:4-7
Verse 4 – Chosen by God
"For we know, brothers and sisters loved by God, that He has chosen you."
Imagine a child lost in a vast, dark forest. He wanders, unsure of his way, until a loving father calls his name, takes his hand, and leads him home. This is our story. We did not find our way to God, He found us (John 15:16). His choosing is not based on merit but on His deep, unwavering love (Deuteronomy 7:7-8). The gospel is not an invitation we accept out of curiosity, it is a rescue mission initiated by God Himself (Ephesians 1:4-5). And to those whom God chooses, the gospel comes very powerfully and transforms us thoroughly. 
How did Paul know that God had chosen the Thessalonians? 
Verse 5 – The Gospel in Power
"Because our gospel came to you not simply with words, but also with power, with the Holy Spirit and deep conviction."
A man dying of thirst doesn’t just admire a glass of water—he drinks it (John 7:37-38). The gospel is not just words; it is life-giving power (Romans 1:16). Many hear it like background noise on the radio (James 1:22) or like a sweet story or a precious poem, but those who receive it with deep conviction experience its transforming force (2 Corinthians 5:17). The Holy Spirit awakens dead hearts (Ezekiel 36:26), giving faith, repentance, and a new life. It is the difference between admiring a lifeboat from the sinking ship and climbing in to be saved (Acts 2:37-38). Many people admire the gospel, can quote much Scripture, but have not received the gospel in power; there is no real life change, they have received the gospel in word only. 
How did Paul know the Thessalonians received the gospel in power?
Verse 6 – Imitators of Christ
"You became imitators of us and of the Lord, for you welcomed the message in the midst of severe suffering with the joy given by the Holy Spirit."
A mirror reflects what it sees. When we receive the gospel with true faith, we become mirrors of Christ, reflecting His love, humility, patience, and endurance (Philippians 2:5-8). This change is so real that even in suffering, there is joy; not a fleeting happiness, but a deep-rooted confidence in Christ (James 1:2-3). 
When my wife and I suddenly lost our 23-year-old son, Joshua, in May of 2022 it was about all we could do to just keep breathing. We thought our world had crashed, our hearts had been thrown to the ground and stomped on. But the gospel alone, and the power of God's Spirit, is getting us through that suffering just like He does for every believer in all trials. I'm sure you've experienced loss or severe trials also, they are impossible to escape in this world (John 16:33). But just as gold is refined by fire, suffering proves and purifies our faith (1 Peter 1:6-7). And in the midst of severe suffering, we can have real joy given by the Holy Spirit. 
Verse 7 – A Model to Others
"And so you became a model to all the believers in Macedonia and Achaia."
A single candle in the darkness is impossible to ignore (Matthew 5:14-16). When the gospel grips us, we shine, showing the world a transformed life (Titus 2:7-8). The Thessalonians did not merely hear and agree with the gospel; they lived it, becoming the living proof of its power (2 Corinthians 3:3). This is the gospel’s effect: it turns sinners into saints, wanderers into witnesses, and rebels into reflections of Jesus (Galatians 2:20).
Have you truly received the gospel, or have you only heard the words? A powerless gospel is no gospel at all (1 Corinthians 4:20). The true gospel changes everything.
What evidence would indicate that a person has truly received the gospel?

Biblical Illustration: 1 Leper out of 10
A great biblical illustration of this teaching is the ten lepers Jesus healed, especially the one who returned to give thanks (Luke 17:11-19).
The Power of the Gospel: The One Who Truly Believed
Ten lepers stood at a distance, outcasts from society, their bodies wasting away. They cried out to Jesus, “Master, have mercy on us!” He told them to go show themselves to the priests, and as they went, they were cleansed. Their disease, which no human effort could cure, vanished at the word of Christ.
Yet, only one of the ten returned to Jesus, praising God with a loud voice and falling at His feet in gratitude. He was a Samaritan, a foreigner, yet he alone recognized that something greater had happened than just physical healing. The others received a miracle but walked away unchanged, just as many hear the gospel yet remain the same. But this one man had more than healed skin—he had a changed heart.
Jesus told him, “Your faith has saved you.” The nine had experienced outward cleansing, but only one received the deeper, eternal transformation that comes through faith in Christ. This is the difference between hearing the gospel in word only and receiving it in power—a changed life, a grateful heart, and a desire to worship at Jesus’ feet.
How does the response of the one leper who returned to Jesus illustrate true faith?

Practical Application
The gospel is not just information—it is transformation. Like the one leper who returned to Jesus, we must respond to the gospel in faith and action. Here’s how:

    Believe Fully – Don’t just hear the gospel; trust in Christ alone for salvation (Romans 10:9). Genuine faith moves beyond intellectual agreement—it leads to a heart fully surrendered to Christ.
    Walk in Obedience – Follow Jesus in daily surrender, just as the leper obeyed Christ’s word (Luke 9:23). Obedience is not a burden but a response of love, demonstrating that we truly belong to Him.
    Live with Gratitude – Give thanks for God’s grace, like the one who returned to Jesus (1 Thessalonians 5:18). Gratitude fuels our worship and strengthens our faith, keeping our focus on God’s goodness.
    Share the Gospel – Tell others of Christ’s power, as the transformed leper did (Mark 5:19).

True faith in the gospel results in a changed life!
In what ways is your life reflecting the transforming power of the gospel today?

Day 85: Jesus Saves Us From the Coming Wrath
Jesus Took Your Wrath
Key Passage
"...and to wait for his Son from heaven, whom he raised from the dead —Jesus, who rescues us from the coming wrath."
1 Thessalonians 1:10
Teaching
1 Thessalonians 1:8-10
The Sound of Salvation (Verse 8)
"The Lord’s message rang out from you not only in Macedonia and Achaia—your faith in God has become known everywhere."
The gospel is not a whisper; it is a trumpet blast (Isaiah 58:1). The Thessalonian believers’ faith was like a lighthouse cutting through the darkness (Matthew 5:14-16), a beacon of hope to all who saw it. But what was their message? It was not merely that they were "in recovery", had "gotten sober", or had stopped doing porn or overeating, or found religion but that they had been rescued from sin (Colossians 1:13-14), from judgment (Hebrews 9:27), from wrath (Romans 5:9). They had been snatched from destruction and placed safely in Christ (Psalm 107:29-30; Hebrews 6:19). The gospel that rang out from them was the good news of salvation through Jesus Christ (Acts 4:12).
Turning from Idols (Verse 9)
"They tell how you turned to God from idols to serve the living and true God."
Idols promise much but give nothing (Jeremiah 2:13). They are wells without water, clouds without rain (Jude 12-13). These Thessalonians had once drunk from broken cisterns, but now they had turned to the fountain of living water (John 7:37-38). Their conversion was a total transformation—leaving behind the empty and the dead (Psalm 115:4-8) to serve the living and true God (Deuteronomy 6:13).
But why the urgency? Why turn? Because judgment was coming (Acts 17:30-31). Like people fleeing a burning building (2 Peter 3:7), they turned from their lifeless gods to take refuge in Christ (Psalm 18:2). They saw the fire of wrath in the distance and ran to the only One who could save them (Nahum 1:6-7).
Saved From Wrath (Verse 10)
"And to wait for His Son from heaven, whom He raised from the dead—Jesus, who rescues us from the coming wrath."
Here is the heart of the gospel: there is wrath to come (John 3:36), and Jesus rescues us from it (Romans 8:1). But how does He do this? By stepping into our place (Isaiah 53:4-6).
Look at the cross. See Jesus, His body bruised and torn, His face streaked with blood, His hands and feet pierced (Psalm 22:16-18). Listen as He cries out, "My God, My God, why have You forsaken Me?" (Matthew 27:46). The sky darkens. The earth shakes (Matthew 27:51), Jesus is pummeled and punished, torn, beaten and bruised, and finally dies a horrific death. This is the wrath of God poured out (Isaiah 53:10). This is what hell looks like (Mark 9:48).
And this—this is what Jesus saved you from (1 Peter 2:24). The punishment that should have crushed you fell on Him (2 Corinthians 5:21). The fire that should have consumed you burned Him instead (Lamentations 1:12-13). He took your place so that you might be free (Galatians 3:13).
The Thessalonians turned, they trusted, and they waited. May we do the same, knowing that when Jesus returns, He comes not as our Judge but as our Savior (Philippians 3:20).
What does the Thessalonians' example teach us about the urgency of turning to Christ and the transformation that comes through the gospel?

Biblical Illustration: Noah and the Ark
In the Old Testament, we see the story of Noah and the Ark (Genesis 6-9), a powerful illustration of God's wrath and salvation. The earth had become corrupt, filled with violence, and God's judgment was impending. He warned Noah of the flood, which would destroy all flesh due to the wickedness of mankind. But Noah found favor in the eyes of God and was instructed to build an ark, a vessel of salvation, for him and his family. The ark represented a refuge from the coming wrath, much like Christ represents our refuge from the wrath to come.
As the floodwaters began to rise, Noah and his family were safely sealed in the ark, spared from the judgment that covered the earth. The ark, though a physical object, points us to Jesus Christ, who is our ultimate refuge from God's judgment. Just as Noah had to enter the ark to be saved, we must turn to Christ in faith, and be in Christ, to be rescued from the coming wrath. The flood was a picture of judgment, but the ark was a picture of God's mercy. In the same way, the cross of Jesus stands as the final refuge from the wrath of God, providing salvation to all who believe in Him and come to Him (Romans 5:9).

What does the account of Noah and the Ark teach us about the relationship between God's judgment, mercy, and the refuge we find in Christ?

Practical Application
In light of the gospel and the picture of Noah’s Ark, here’s what we can do:

    Turn from all sin—Repent from every sin, recognizing the urgency of God's judgment and seeking refuge in Christ (Acts 3:19, 1 John 1:9).
    Run to Jesus daily—Make Jesus your constant source of refuge, seeking His strength and grace for every moment (Hebrews 4:16, Matthew 11:28-30).
    Rest in His finished work—Trust fully in the completed work of Christ on the cross, knowing that your salvation is secure (John 19:30, Ephesians 2:8-9). If accusation and condemnation arise, look at Jesus who was accused of your sin and condemned in your place. Do this daily and often, for one sight of the cross is the death of sin in our lives.

How can you actively turn from sin, run to Jesus daily, and rest in His finished work to experience the refuge and security He offers?

Day 86 Renewing Our Minds
Thinking Differently
Key Passage
And to be renewed in the spirit of your minds.
Ephesians 4:23
Teaching
If we want to live our lives free from habitual sin, there is nothing more important than to renew our minds. Today I want to help us understand both the need to renew our minds and the way to do so.  Let's look at Ephesians 4:22-24:
Ephesians 4:22 - "To put off your old self, which belongs to your former manner of life and is corrupt through deceitful desires."

Imagine your old self as a tattered coat, stained with sin’s grime (Isaiah 64:6), patched with lies that whisper, “This is who you are.” It clings like damp wool, heavy with corruption and the permeating stench of vomit (Proverbs 26:11, 2 Peter 2:22). 
The gospel calls us to shed it, as Christ’s death on the cross tore that garment apart (Colossians 3:9). His blood, spilled crimson, washes the stains (1 John 1:7), declaring the old life dead and buried with Him. We don’t patch it up; we cast it off because Jesus didn’t just mend the old self, He made us new (2 Corinthians 5:17).
Ephesians 4:23 - "And to be renewed in the spirit of your minds."

Picture your mind as a garden, once overrun with weeds: thorny thoughts of lust, pride, unforgiveness, anger, revenge, etc. (Jeremiah 17:9). Renewing it in the gospel is like a spring rain, softening hard soil (Psalm 65:10), letting God’s Word take root. Jesus’ death tills up that hard ground, and His resurrection is that life-giving rain (1 Corinthians 15:20), bursting through the tomb and bringing new growth with it. His blood waters the soul (Hebrews 9:14), flushing out deceit’s drought. 
The gospel isn’t a one-time fix; it’s a daily soaking: Scripture sinking deep (Psalm 119:105), reshaping how we think, aligning us with the risen King.
Ephesians 4:24 - "And to put on the new self, created after the likeness of God in true righteousness and holiness."

Now envision a radiant robe, woven from Christ’s righteousness (Isaiah 61:10), sparkling and white as snow (Isaiah 1:18). Putting on the new self isn’t play-acting; it’s stepping into who we are, remade in God’s image (Genesis 1:27). Jesus’ burial stripped away sin’s shroud; His resurrection clothed us in holiness (Romans 13:14), bought by His blood. The gospel stitches this garment: we wear His victory, not ours (Galatians 2:20). It’s not about earning it but embracing it; living bold and pure because the One who conquered death dresses us in His likeness (1 Peter 1:15-16).
Reflection: Our minds are battlegrounds; old habits claw back, but Christ’s gospel wins (1 John 5:4). His death buries the old, His blood cleanses, His resurrection renews. Soak in His Word daily. Shed the rags, wear the robe; renewed minds reflect the Savior who triumphed.
What does Ephesians 4:22-24 reveal about the process of shedding the old self, renewing the mind, and putting on the new self in Christ?

Biblical Illustration: The Ephesians
Consider the Ephesian believers in Acts 19:18-20. Many had come from a world draped in sorcery’s dark cloak, their minds tangled with incantations and idol worship. Their old selves were like moth-eaten scrolls, corrupt with deceitful desires, shaping lives of superstition and sin. But when the gospel pierced their hearts they couldn’t cling to the past. They confessed, shedding their old life, and burned their magic books, a pile of ashes marking their break from the lies.
Their minds, once a choked garden, began to bloom anew. The Holy Spirit, through Paul’s preaching of Christ’s blood-shedding sacrifice, renewed their thinking: truth replacing spells, faith uprooting fear. They put on a new self, reflecting God’s righteousness, as the gospel’s power reshaped them. The cost of their scrolls was fifty thousand silver pieces, but that cost paled next to the treasure of Jesus' love for them and the power of the gospel. Jesus’ death buried their old ways; His resurrection clothed them in holiness. What they couldn’t scrub clean themselves, His blood did, and transformed their minds in the process.
Like the Ephesians, we shed sin’s rags and renew our minds through God's Word, Christ’s gospel, God's Word and the Holy Spirit, wearing His righteousness like a bride adorned in jewels and loved by her husband. "I delight greatly in the LORD; my soul rejoices in my God. For he has clothed me with garments of salvation and arrayed me in a robe of his righteousness, as a bridegroom adorns his head like a priest, and as a bride adorns herself with her jewels" (Isaiah 61:10).
What does the response of the Ephesian believers in Acts 19:18-20 teach us about true repentance and the transformative power of the gospel?

Practical Application
Here are some ways we can practically apply the truths in this lesson today:

    Put Off the Old Self: Reject past identities and sinful thoughts and habits, refusing to let them define you (Romans 6:11-12). This requires a conscious decision every day to recognize and reject old patterns, recognizing that you are no longer bound by them in Christ.
    Renew Your Mind: Saturate yourself in Scripture (Psalm 119:11). Meditate on the gospel—Christ’s death and resurrection—as your source of renewal (Romans 12:2). The Word of God transforms your thinking, aligning it with God’s truth rather than worldly influences.
    Put On the New Self: Walk in holiness by the Spirit’s power (Galatians 5:16). Actively live in the identity Christ has given you, clothed in His righteousness (Colossians 3:10). Remember, this new identity is not something you earn, but something you receive through Christ’s work.
    Stay Rooted in Christ: Pray for transformation (Philippians 4:6-7). Surround yourself with believers who encourage gospel renewal (Hebrews 10:24-25). This community and prayerful dependence on Christ are key to sustaining your growth and transformation in Him.

How can you actively put off the old self, renew your mind, and live out your new identity in Christ today?

Day 87: The Glory of God in Jacob's Stairway
Freedom Through Jacob's Ladder
Key Passage
“How awesome is this place! This is none other than the house of God; this is the gate of heaven.”
Genesis 28:17 
Teaching
I wanted to remind us of something we looked at earlier in this devotional series, which is the need to see and meditate on the glory of God in Jesus Christ, in order to find freedom (2 Corinthians 3:17-18). Seeing Jesus in Scripture is not a mere intellectual exercise, it's that which has the power to free us from our sins. 
Let's take the example of Jacob's ladder as recorded in Genesis 28:10-17:
The Glory of God in Jacob’s Ladder
(Genesis 28:10-17)
Jacob was alone, a fugitive fleeing from Esau’s wrath, when night fell and he lay down with a rock for a pillow. But as he slept, heaven opened, and God gave him a vision—a stairway reaching from earth to heaven, with angels ascending and descending on it (Genesis 28:12). This vision was no mere dream; it was a divine revelation of Jesus Christ, who later declared to Nathaniel, "You will see heaven open, and the angels of God ascending and descending on the Son of Man" (John 1:51). Christ is Jacob’s ladder, the bridge between heaven and earth, connecting sinful man to a holy God.
1. The Stairway Connected Heaven and Earth
Jacob saw a stairway stretching between two realms: earth below, heaven above. This was no ordinary ladder; it symbolized the way to God. Sin had created a vast gulf between man and his Creator, but Jesus became the bridge through His death and resurrection. As the only Mediator (1 Timothy 2:5), He spans the divide, reconciling us to the Father. The cross, like Jacob’s ladder, reaches from earth’s sorrow to heaven’s glory, from sinful man to holy God, and through Jesus the Son we have access to God the Father (Hebrews 10:19-20).
2. The Stairway is Where God’s Promises Were Stated
At the top of the ladder, the Lord stood and spoke, reaffirming His covenant to Jacob (Genesis 28:13-15). This echoes the truth that in Christ, every promise of God finds its fulfillment: "For all the promises of God find their Yes in Him" (2 Corinthians 1:20). Jesus is the living Word, the embodiment of every divine promise.
3. The Stairway Was the Place of Blessing
God promised that through Jacob’s offspring, all nations would be blessed (Genesis 28:14). This points to Christ, who became a curse for us so that we might receive the blessing of Abraham (Galatians 3:13-14). Through Him, we inherit eternal life and the riches of grace.
4. The Stairway Was the Place of God’s Presence
God assured Jacob, "I am with you" (Genesis 28:15). Just as Jacob met God at the stairway, so in Christ, we encounter the fullness of the Godhead (Colossians 2:9). Jesus is Emmanuel, "God with us" (Matthew 1:23), revealing the Father to us.
5. The Response to the Revelation
Jacob awoke in awe, exclaiming, "Surely the Lord is in this place!" and "How awesome is this place! This is none other than the house of God, and this is the gate of heaven" (Genesis 28:16-17). Likewise, when we behold Christ, our response is worship! Jesus is the true gateway to heaven (John 10:9), the presence of God among us, and the stairway on whom our salvation rests.
Through Jacob’s ladder, we see the glory of Christ, the only way to the Father. Let us, like Jacob, stand in awe and worship Him who has bridged the gap between heaven and earth.
How does Jacob’s vision of the stairway help us understand Jesus’ role as the bridge between God and humanity?

Biblical Illustration: Thomas' worship
One of the most striking moments of worship in response to the revelation of Jesus Christ is found in Thomas, the disciple who doubted. After the resurrection, Jesus appeared to the disciples, but Thomas was absent. When told of Christ’s return, he refused to believe unless he could see and touch the wounds himself (John 20:25). A week later, Jesus appeared again and invited Thomas to see and touch His wounds. In that moment, all doubt melted away, and Thomas fell in worship, exclaiming, "My Lord and my God!" (John 20:28).
Thomas’ response mirrors Jacob’s in Genesis 28:16-17. When faced with the undeniable presence of God, both men were awestruck. Just as Jacob recognized that he was standing at the gate of heaven, Thomas saw the risen Christ and knew he stood before the doorway to eternal life. Jesus is the true stairway between heaven and earth, the one who reconciles sinners and calls forth worship. Like Jacob and Thomas, let us bow before Him in awe and adoration.
Worship of Jesus is the opposite of withering in sin. Worship is the simple response to the revelation that Jesus is God in the flesh, who came to right all your wrongs, remove all your sins, erase all your past, undo everything you did, and who has opened all of heaven for you.
What does Thomas' response to seeing the risen Christ teach us about true worship?


Practical Application
Since Christ is the stairway to heaven, we must see this Stairway through the eyes of faith, and daily walk in faith, seeing more and more of Jesus, and drawing near to God through Him (Hebrews 4:16). Like Jacob, let us recognize God’s presence in our lives, setting aside time for worship and prayer (1 Thessalonians 5:16-18). Each day presents an opportunity to have our worship and our prayers ascend to God, even as God descended to us on the stairway of Christ. The more we seek Him, the clearer our vision becomes, transforming our doubts into unwavering faith.
Like Thomas, let us overcome doubt by fixing our eyes on the risen Christ (Hebrews 12:2). Even when we cannot see the full picture, we can trust the One who holds it all together. Faith is not merely believing in God’s existence but actively relying on His promises, even in uncertainty. Finally, let us share this gospel bridge with others, proclaiming that Jesus alone is the way to the Father (John 14:6). Worship, trust, and evangelism should flow from our hearts as we live in the reality of Christ’s open heaven.
How can you intentionally step forward in faith today so that you are walking more fully in the reality of Christ as the only way to the Father?

Day 88: Jesus is Our Mediator
Jesus is All We Need for Freedom
Key Passage
For there is one God and one mediator between God and mankind, the man Christ Jesus, 6 who gave himself as a ransom for all people.
1 Timothy 2:5-6
Teaching
Jesus Is Our Ransom and Mediator
1 Timothy 2:3-6
1 Timothy 2:3 "This is good, and pleases God our Savior,"
Imagine a prisoner, chained in a dark dungeon, unable to break free. Every day, the iron shackles dig deeper into his skin. He has tried to escape, but the weight of his captivity is too much, his sin feels so much like slavery (John 8:34). This is how sin holds people in habitual bondage. But God, our Savior, looks upon this imprisoned soul, not with indifference, but with divine pleasure at the thought of setting him free (Luke 4:18). The gospel is God’s plan of rescue, a mission of deliverance that brings Him great joy (Ezekiel 33:11).
1 Timothy 2:4 – "Who desires all people to be saved and to come to the knowledge of the truth."
Picture a lighthouse standing firm against the storm, its beam sweeping across the waters, guiding lost sailors to safety (Psalm 119:105). God's heart is like that: He shines the light of truth, calling people to salvation (John 1:9, 2 Peter 3:9). No one is beyond His reach (Isaiah 59:1), and all the elect will be irresistibly drawn to the cross, and find salvation. Habitual sin blinds people, making them believe the chains cannot be broken. But the truth is this: there is One who paid the price to set captives free (John 8:32).
1 Timothy 2:5 – "For there is one God, and there is one mediator between God and men, the man Christ Jesus."
A mediator is like a bridge spanning a deep canyon, connecting two sides that could never meet on their own. Sin created an impassable gulf between humanity and God (Isaiah 59:2), but Jesus came to stand in the gap, even much more than Moses did in Exodus 32:9-14 (Psalm 106:23). Unlike human mediators who negotiate peace between two parties, Jesus did more: He carried our sins and our sorrows (Isaiah 53:4), suffered in our place (Isaiah 53:5), and became the path back to the Father (John 14:6). Without Him, we remain estranged, locked in a cycle of failure. But through Him, we can walk freely into God’s presence, no longer separated by guilt (Hebrews 10:19-22).
1 Timothy 2:6 – "Who gave himself as a ransom for all, which is the testimony given at the proper time."
A ransom is a price paid to secure someone's release. Imagine a slave market where the highest price is demanded for freedom—yet no man can afford it (Psalm 49:7-8). Jesus stepped forward and offered His own life as payment (Mark 10:45). His blood was the currency that redeemed us (1 Peter 1:18-19), breaking sin’s ownership over us (Romans 6:6-7). Now, those who trust in Him are no longer bound to "addiction", lust, anger, or any other chain (Romans 8:2). The cross is the receipt of our freedom (Colossians 2:13-14).
If sin still grips you, remember this: Jesus has already paid your ransom (Galatians 3:13), and as your Mediator, He is leading you into true liberty. Trust Him, walk with Him, and step into the freedom He purchased for you (John 8:36).
How does recognizing Jesus as our mediator and ransom transform the way we live and respond to temptation and sin?

Biblical Illustration: The Longing of Job
Job’s Longing for a Ransom and Mediator
Job sat in the dust, scraping his sores with a broken piece of pottery, his heart as shattered as the fragments in his hands. He had lost everything including his wealth, his children, his health; yet what weighed most heavily upon him was the crushing reality of standing before a holy God.
“If only there were someone to mediate between us, someone to bring us together,” he groaned (Job 9:33). He felt the vast distance between himself and God, like a man stranded on one side of a great canyon with no way across. He longed for a bridge, someone who could lay a hand on both him and Almighty God. He felt the heavy rod of God upon Him and needed someone to remove the rod. Little did He know that He was longing for Jesus, who would come to bridge the gap and take the rod of God's wrath upon Himself!
But Job also recognized his guilt. “He is not a mere mortal like me that I might answer him” (Job 9:32). He knew that no argument, no effort of his own, could justify him before God. What he needed was a ransom; someone to pay the price for him, to remove God’s rod of judgment (Job 9:34).
Centuries later, Job’s cry was answered by Jesus Christ. As Mediator, Jesus bridges the gap (1 Timothy 2:5). As Ransom, He pays the price (1 Timothy 2:6). What Job longed for, we now have in Jesus! He breaks the power of canceled sin, He sets the captive free. Oh Jesus, how I love you, because you first loved me.
What does Job's cry for a mediator and ransom reveal about the role of Jesus Christ in our relationship with God?

Practical Application
Living in the Freedom of Christ
Like Job, we need both a Mediator and a Ransom. Jesus has already fulfilled both roles, so how do we respond? 
First, repent and believe (Mark 1:15), meaning turn from sin and trust that Jesus' blood fully paid for your sin and bought your freedom (1 Peter 1:18-19).  Repentance is having a change of heart and mind, turning away from sin and towards God's mercy, while belief is having complete trust in Christ's sacrifice as the only means of salvation.
Second, draw near to God in prayer (Hebrews 10:22), knowing Jesus intercedes for you (Romans 8:34). Prayer is not just a request line to God, but a relationship where you connect with God through Jesus, who not only removed the rod of God from you, but also continually pleads on your behalf.
Third, walk in obedience (John 14:15), rejecting sin’s chains because you are no longer a slave (Romans 6:6-7). Obedience reflects a life transformed by the gospel, where the power of sin is broken, and we live as free people in Christ.
Finally, proclaim this freedom to others (2 Corinthians 5:20). Our testimony becomes a declaration of God's mercy, showing others that freedom in Christ is available to all who believe.
Christ’s ransom has been paid, now live in the liberty He purchased for you!
How can you begin to live out the freedom Christ purchased for you by responding in repentance, prayer, obedience, and proclamation?

Day 89: Knowing Christ
Knowing Jesus: the Key to Life and Freedom
Key Passage
What is more, I consider everything a loss because of the surpassing worth of knowing Christ Jesus my Lord, for whose sake I have lost all things. I consider them garbage, that I may gain Christ.
Philippians 3:8
Teaching
There is one thing that will set us free from sin forever: knowing Jesus Christ in an intimate, close, personal relationship. Paul compares it to the sexual intimacy of husband and wife (Ephesians 5:31-33) and it is the most exquisite experience any believer can ever have. Let's look at it together.
I Want to Know Christ
Philippians 3:7-11
Verse 7 – “But whatever were gains to me I now consider loss for the sake of Christ.”
Imagine holding a handful of diamonds one evening, only to check on them in the morning and see the sunrise reveal that they are just shattered glass. This is what Paul realized: everything he once treasured was worthless compared to Christ. The gospel flips our values upside down. What we once counted as gain; our achievements, reputation, sinful habits, even religious works, become worthless when we see Jesus as He truly is: the only One who loved us to death and back, to hell and back and to heaven and back to get us. 
Can you say that the sin that once bound you is totally a loss now, for the sake of Christ? That it is shattered glass, not diamonds? 
Verse 8 – “I consider everything a loss because of the surpassing worth of knowing Christ Jesus my Lord…”
Paul uses the word “knowing” (Greek: gnōsis), which isn’t just head knowledge—it’s a deep, intimate union, like a husband and wife (Genesis 4:1). To know Christ is to experience Him, not just study Him. Moses cried, “Show me your glory!” (Exodus 33:18). David longed for this intimacy and said, “My soul thirsts for You” (Psalm 63:1). This has always been the heart cry of God’s people: to truly know Him. And this is what salvation is: "Now this is eternal life: that they know you, the only true God, and Jesus Christ, whom you have sent" (John 17:3).
Verse 9 – “Not having a righteousness of my own…but that which is through faith in Christ…”
A drowning man cannot save himself by paddling harder; he must be rescued. How much more a dead man that cannot climb out of his own grave, but must be resurrected? So it is with righteousness. We cannot achieve it; we must receive it. The truth is this: Christ became sin for us so that we might become His righteousness (2 Corinthians 5:21). Knowing Him means resting in His finished work rather than striving in our own. Christian, you are righteous, right now, because of what the Lord has done for you.
Verse 10 – “I want to know Christ—yes, to know the power of His resurrection and participation in His sufferings…”
To know Christ is to share in both His triumph and His trials. The resurrection power that raised Him from the dead now lives in us (Romans 8:11), transforming our hearts, minds, and lives. But knowing Christ also means walking His road of suffering, surrendering our will to God as Jesus did in Gethsemane. Love and suffering go together, Jesus proved that on the cross.
Remember that when you lose a loved one and the ache is so deep it feels like you can't breathe and don't know if you'll live. Remember it when you are ridiculed for your faith, excluded from something you enjoy, or shut out from family that you love (Matthew 10:34). God gave His Son up, Jesus was excluded from the camp of Israel (Hebrews 13:12-13) and shut out from "His own" (John 1:11)
Verse 11 – “…somehow, attaining to the resurrection from the dead.”
The goal of knowing Christ isn’t just knowledge; it’s resurrection life. Just as a caterpillar dies in the cocoon to emerge as a butterfly, we die to ourselves so that Christ’s life is formed in us (Galatians 2:20).
Let this be our longing today: to know Jesus deeply, intimately, personally, and powerfully—because He is worth knowing.

Day 89: Knowing Christ

What are the things in your life that you once considered valuable but now see as loss compared to the surpassing worth of knowing Christ?

Illustration: Mike Cleveland
I was ensnared in overeating, drinking, impurity, and much more for decades. Like everyone else, I was born a sinner, but it got a strong hold on me one day when I was on a layover with the airline I worked for. I walked into the hotel room, and there on top of the TV was a black box. I turned the TV on and began pushing the buttons out of curiosity.
That turned into bondage to all types of impurity that lasted for many years and only got worse no matter what I tried to stop. And because sin begets sin and is never satisfied I began drinking the "mini" alcohol bottles that were in the room and ordering room service. When home I was increasingly mentally unavailable, harsh to my family, and would long for my next trip out of town. This bondage led to the death of my first marriage, and other unspeakable losses, ones that wrack my heart and make me hurt even today just thinking about it.
Though I professed to be a Christian during this time I had not experienced the resurrection power of Jesus Christ, did not have any intimacy with Him, and was constantly trying to fuel the insatiable cravings within my soul.
One day after a long night of sin, I began crying to the Lord. I had a Bible with me, I began reading it, came to Psalm 51, and prayed earnestly through each verse, confessing to God that I had sinned against Him, hurt my family, traded my entire life for a banquet in the grave, and plead with Him to give me a new heart, put His Spirit within me, and cause me to obey Him. I learned later that He came and sought me out, convicted me of sin (which is what I had), righteousness (which is what I needed), and judgment (which is what I was headed for). He began revealing Himself to me in His Word and by His Spirit.
I had already led the way to the death of my first marriage and was rapidly killing my marriage to Jody, as sin is not satisfied until it brings death to everything. But after my experience of crying out to God in Psalm 51, Jody and I went to a biblical counselor who was pastoring a church nearby. He showed me the gospel, and through my study of Scripture, I saw for the first time how desperately I needed Christ. I begged to know Him, to experience Him, to live in Him. I confessed my sin to Jody, and plead for forgiveness and she forgave me from her heart.
God answered me in powerful ways: over time He has given me such a godly sorrow and thorough repentance that I'm nauseated at the thought of my previous life and have no desire to return to it whatsoever. This has been a progressive change, not an instant one (though some things did drop off instantly), and through it all, I've come to this one conclusion: knowing Christ is better by far! The beloved said "Your love is better than wine" (Song 4:10) and every believer who has experienced intimacy and union with Jesus knows His love is better than wine, porn, gluttony, smoking, workaholism, religion, and everything else.
I'm living in purity, lost the 45 pounds I put on, and never get drunk or smoke anymore. Of course, I can still be tempted, yes, the world never loses its allurements, and my flesh is as bad today as it ever was (see 1 Corinthians 10:12). But Jesus! Oh, Jesus is so sweet to my soul, so precious to my heart, more captivating now than He ever was!
My wife, Jody, and I have been married for decades now, and our relationship is the most enjoyable thing in the world for us both. Yet we both long to know Christ more, to experience more of Him, and can truly say, "I consider everything a loss because of the surpassing worth of knowing Christ Jesus my Lord, for whose sake I have lost all things. I consider them garbage, that I may gain Christ 9 and be found in him, not having a righteousness of my own that comes from the law, but that which is through faith in Christ—the righteousness that comes from God on the basis of faith" (Philippians 3:8-9).
If you would like to, please share a brief testimony. How did you come to know Jesus? What intimacy do you have with Him? What changes is He making in you?

Practical Application
Putting It into Practice: Knowing Christ
To truly know Christ, we must pursue Him as the treasure of our lives. Here’s how:

    Seek Him in His Word – Read Scripture daily, not just for knowledge, but to meet Jesus (John 5:39-40). Allow the Word to become alive in your heart, transforming your thoughts and actions as you grow in your relationship with Him.
    Pray for Deeper Fellowship – Ask God to reveal Christ to your heart (Ephesians 1:17). Pray for a deep, intimate connection with Jesus, inviting the Holy Spirit to illuminate your understanding and draw you closer to His presence.
    Rest in His Righteousness – Trust that your standing with God is through faith, not works (Romans 3:22). Let go of striving and rest in the truth that Jesus' sacrifice has made you right with God, and nothing can separate you from His love.
    Embrace Suffering for Christ – View trials as a means to know Him more (1 Peter 4:13).  Recognize that suffering refines your faith, drawing you nearer to Christ and revealing His sustaining power in your life.
    Live in Resurrection Power – Walk in newness of life through the Spirit (Romans 8:11). Allow the resurrection power of Christ to transform how you live, empowering you to overcome sin and live victoriously in Him.

In what ways can you actively pursue a deeper relationship with Christ through His Word, prayer, and embracing His power in your life?

Day 90 Walking With Christ
Walking With Christ
Key Passage
And beginning with Moses and all the Prophets, he explained to them what was said in all the Scriptures concerning himself.
Luke 24:27
Teaching
To know Christ we have to walk with Him. By that I mean we must spend time with Him throughout the day, ask Him questions, and learn from Him, seeing Him in His Word, following Him in the way we live. Like you would walk with a friend. As the old hymn says:
And he walks with me and he talks with me
And he tells me I am his own
And the joy we share as we tarry there
None other has ever known
https://library.timelesstruths.org/music/In_the_Garden/
But the reality is that every believer who has had an encounter with Jesus has fully known this experience. Today we want to see the two discouraged disciples who had an encounter walking with Jesus, as this is a perfect example of how we walk and talk with Him. It could be summarized like this: 1. We tell Him our problems and burdens. 2. He listens and 3. He reveals Himself and the gospel, in the Scriptures, as the solution.
What It’s Like Walking with Jesus
Luke 24:13-35
A Journey from Blindness to Sight
Luke 24:13-16 – Two disciples walked, discussing the events of Jesus’ death, but their eyes were kept from recognizing Him.

Walking through life without knowing Christ is like hiking through thick fog—you can see shapes and shadows, but nothing clearly. The disciples, grieving and confused, were in a spiritual fog. Yet Jesus, the risen Savior, walked right beside them, unrecognized but present. He does the same today, drawing near when we least expect it and when we most need Him!
When our son, Joshua, died, Jody and I were full of grief. I went out walking by the beach and had a thought plaguing me; the thought told me that I was so unloved. Now logically I knew better, but my heart was aching with the loss of my son, and grief was doing the thinking for me. I truly felt so unloved in life, by everybody. As I was walking along a pathway, a man I'd never seen before came walking toward me, and as our eyes met briefly he spontaneously uttered three words, "Jesus loves you!" and kept walking.
Jesus used that man's few words to communicate His love to my heart just when I needed to hear it. It was as if Jesus Himself showed up and put His arm around his hurting child. My heart was comforted and stilled. Several weeks later that same man came to the church I was pastoring, we got to know each other well over time, and I shared with him what he meant to me that day as he boldly shared the love of Jesus with a stranger, and the amazing timing of our encounter.
Luke 24:17-24 – Jesus asked them what they were talking about, and they told Him of their dashed hopes.
They had hoped Jesus would redeem Israel, but their hope had died with Jesus on a cross. When we misunderstand Christ’s mission, we expect Him to fix earthly troubles while missing His greater work—saving our lives from sin and our souls for eternity. 
Luke 24:25-27 – Jesus rebuked their slowness to believe and explained how the Scriptures pointed to Him.
Imagine a masterpiece covered in dust: Jesus swept His hand across the Old Testament, revealing Himself in every stroke of the divine story. From Genesis to Malachi, the Scriptures whisper His name, bleed His blood, and reveal His love. Like a lamp lit in a darkened room, He illuminated their understanding, showing that the cross was not a detour but the destination, planned from the beginning. What love He had for them to point to the cross and resurrection as that which should comfort their hearts.
Luke 24:28-31 – They urged Him to stay, and at the table, their eyes were opened as He broke the bread.
A single moment can turn night to day, like when that stranger told me Jesus loved me. When Jesus broke the bread, they recognized Him. The One they thought was gone was alive! Likewise, when we partake of the gospel, feeding on the broken body of Jesus, drinking in His blood that speaks a better word, our spiritual eyes are opened. We recognize Jesus when we come to the cross and the empty tomb.
Luke 24:32-35 – Their hearts burned within them as He spoke, and they ran to tell the others.
A cold heart ignites when touched by Jesus Christ. Like dry wood catching fire, their sorrow was replaced with joy, and they could not keep the news to themselves. Knowing Jesus Christ absolutely transforms us, and we cannot help but run to tell others that the grave is empty, and our Savior lives! This is the very reason why I love to preach!
Walking with Jesus means seeing Him in His Word, recognizing Him in fellowship, and proclaiming Him with joy. The road of life may be long, but with Jesus, every step is full of love and grace and leads to glory.
How does the encounter of the two disciples with Jesus on the road to Emmaus illustrate what it means to walk with Him daily?

Biblical Illustration: Cleopas And His Companion
Cleopas and His Companion on the Road to Emmaus
Sticking with this same biblical story, there is something essential to learn from it. It's how to read your Bible.
Notice something about this story: 
Cleopas and his companion trudged along the road to Emmaus, their steps heavy with sorrow. The events of the past days had shattered their hopes: Jesus, the one they believed would redeem Israel, had been crucified. Their faith was like a dying ember, flickering but nearly extinguished.
As they walked, a stranger joined them, though they did not recognize Him because Jesus kept them from recognizing Him. Why? What point was He making in concealing His identity? I mean, they were looking right at Him but couldn't recognize Him. Why? They were nose to nose but missed understanding that it was Jesus. Why?
Because Jesus was illustrating a point, making an object lesson that they would never forget. See they had been reading their Bibles just like that walk: looking right at the Word of God but not recognizing Jesus in it. They had their noses in the book but missed the death of Jesus for their sins and His resurrection from the dead.
Jesus had to correct their method of Bible study, and He did so with this simple object lesson. Jesus listened to their grief, but then He opened the Scriptures, revealing how the Messiah had to suffer before entering glory (His death and resurrection). Like the first rays of dawn chasing away the darkness, the truth began to break upon their hearts.
Still, they remained blind until they urged Him to stay and break bread with them. In that simple act, Jesus opened their eyes. He had opened the Scriptures to them and taught them how to study their Bibles, now He opened their eyes to see Him! The One they had mourned was alive! Now their sorrow turned to burning joy, their confusion to clarity. They could not contain their excitement, they ran back to Jerusalem to tell the others.
Like them, we often study our Bibles in spiritual blindness, missing Christ’s work. But the gospel; His death and resurrection, opens our eyes. In His Word and fellowship, we truly come to know Him. And when we do, our hearts burn within us, and we are compelled to share the good news.
What does the story of Cleopas and his companion on the road to Emmaus teach us about recognizing Jesus in Scripture?

Practical Application
Just as Jesus opened the Scriptures to the disciples, we must seek Him in His Word. Here’s how:

    Pray for Open Eyes – Ask God to remove spiritual blindness and reveal Christ in the Bible (Psalm 119:18). Without His illumination, we may read the words but miss the life-changing truth within them.
    Read with Expectation – Approach Scripture believing that all of it points to Jesus (Luke 24:27). Every story, prophecy, and command ultimately finds its fulfillment in Him, deepening our faith as we see His presence woven throughout.
    Meditate on the Gospel – Focus on His death and resurrection as the key to understanding Scripture (1 Corinthians 2:2). When we keep the gospel at the center, the Bible transforms from a mere text into the living revelation of God's love and redemption.
    Fellowship with Believers – Discuss Scripture with others, as Jesus often reveals Himself in community (Matthew 18:20). Hearing different insights and experiences helps us grow in understanding and strengthens our walk with Him.

Share about a time when you clearly saw Christ in the Scriptures.

Please Help Us Reach People

"Two are better than one" and you can help us by partnering with us in the gospel, giving monthly to reach more people. Click here to set up a monthly recurring donation of any amount.

Day 91: The Ongoing Need For Repentance
Repenting Repenters and Believing Believers
Key Passage
“Repent and believe the good news!”
Mark 1:15
Teaching
Repenting Repenters: A Life of Ongoing Turning
Repentance is not a one-time event but a lifelong posture. Just as faith is not something we exercise once but continue in, so also we are "repenting repenters." Jesus' first recorded words in Mark’s Gospel were, “Repent and believe the good news!” (Mark 1:15). These two actions, repenting and believing, define the entire Christian life.
Repentance consists of four essential elements: illumination, humiliation, detestation, and transformation. If any of these are absent, our repentance is incomplete, and we are in danger. Let's check our repentance by these four truths:
1. Illumination: Seeing Sin as Sin
Repentance begins when God’s light shines into our hearts, revealing sin as it truly is. Just as a candle flickers dimly in a dark cave, and you don't have much light until the morning sun floods in, so our understanding of sin is murky until God exposes it.
Scripture: "Your word is a lamp to my feet and a light for my path." (Psalm 119:105)
Danger of Neglecting Illumination: Without seeing sin, we remain blind, stumbling deeper into darkness. “If we claim to be without sin, we deceive ourselves” (1 John 1:8).
2. Humiliation: Brokenness Over Sin
Once illuminated, we are humbled because seeing our sin rightly causes grief and humility. Like Peter weeping bitterly after denying Christ (Luke 22:62), true repentance melts pride and brings contrition.
Scripture: "The sacrifices of God are a broken spirit; a broken and contrite heart, O God, you will not despise." (Psalm 51:17)
Danger of Neglecting Humiliation: Without humility, we rationalize and excuse sin, preventing true and lasting change, and at this stage God is opposed to us. “God opposes the proud but gives grace to the humble” (James 4:6).
3. Detestation: Hatred for Sin
Seeing and feeling sin’s weight leads to loathing it. A man who narrowly escapes a house fire does not return to retrieve his belongings, he flees! So must we hate sin and turn from it. This is one of the greatest gifts God has given me, to hate my previous sins with a passion. Has He given this to you as well? If not, ask Him for it.
Scripture: "Hate what is evil; cling to what is good." (Romans 12:9)
Danger of Neglecting Detestation: Without hatred for sin, we return to it like a dog to its vomit (2 Peter 2:22). True repentance means leaving sin behind.
4. Transformation: A Changed Life
Repentance is incomplete without transformation. The prodigal son didn’t just regret his choices, he got up and returned to his father (Luke 15:20). True repentance results in a changed life, a new course, and a new obedience.
Scripture: "Produce fruit in keeping with repentance." (Matthew 3:8)
Danger of Neglecting Transformation: If sin remains unchallenged, repentance is mere sentiment, feeling bad for being caught or feeling bad because of the consequences. “Faith by itself, if it is not accompanied by action, is dead” (James 2:17).
Repentance is a gift from Christ, who carried our sins on the cross so that we might turn from them. As we keep repenting, we keep believing, clinging to Jesus our righteousness, holiness, and redemption (1 Corinthians 1:30). May we walk as believing believers and repenting repenters until the day we see Him face to face.
What role do illumination, humiliation, detestation, and transformation play in ensuring true repentance?


Biblical Illustration: The Ninevites
One of the clearest pictures of full repentance is found in the people of Nineveh (Jonah 3). When Jonah preached God’s coming judgment, the entire city was illuminated—their eyes were opened to their sin. They did not ignore or dismiss the warning but recognized their guilt before God.
Their response led to humiliation—from the king to the lowest servant, all put on sackcloth and fasted, showing true sorrow over their sin (Jonah 3:5-6). This was not mere regret but deep contrition.
Next, they showed detestation—they didn’t just acknowledge sin but turned from it with passion. The king decreed that everyone should “give up their evil ways and their violence” (Jonah 3:8). There was a decisive rejection of wickedness.
Finally, their repentance resulted in transformation—they changed their behavior, and God, in His mercy, relented from sending disaster (Jonah 3:10).
This story points us to the greater truth of the gospel: just as God had mercy on Nineveh, He has mercy on us through Christ. Jesus is the true and better Jonah (Matthew 12:41), who not only warns of judgment but provides salvation through His death and resurrection. As we repent, we find grace in Him, and our lives are forever changed.
How did the repentance of Nineveh lead to transformation and God's mercy?

Practical Application
Living as Repenting Believers
To practice ongoing repentance, we must actively engage with the gospel and apply God’s truth to our lives:

    Seek Illumination: Regularly read Scripture and ask the Holy Spirit to reveal both our sin (Psalm 139:23-24) and our Savior (Matthew 11:27). 
    Embrace Humility: Confess sin openly to God and trusted believers (James 5:16). Look to the cross and see the extent God had to go because of your sin, and see His love for you in going that far. This humbles our hearts.
    Cultivate Detestation: Pray for a deeper hatred of sin and love for righteousness (Psalm 97:10).
    Pursue Transformation: Actively turn from sin and walk in new obedience through Christ’s power (Romans 6:11-12).

As we daily repent, we daily believe, clinging to the cross where grace abounds.
For further reading on this subject, especially the illumination, humiliation, detestation, and transformation of repentance, delight your heart in a sermon by Charles Spurgeon here.
How can you incorporate ongoing repentance into your daily life?

Day 92: Scripture on Repentance
What The Bible Says
Key Passage
Those whom I love I rebuke and discipline. So be earnest and repent.
Revelation 3:19 
Teaching
Repent! A Call to Turn and Live
There is a sickness that spreads unseen, a darkness that cloaks the soul. Sin is not just a mistake or a misstep, it is rebellion against God, a poison that seeps into our hearts, separating all humanity from our Maker. But there is hope! There is cleansing at the foot of the cross, where Jesus’ blood washes away every stain. We need to repent to find that cleansing. 
Today, let’s look at what the Bible says about repentance. Please meditate on each verse, letting God's Word work in your heart.
Acts 3:19 – “Repent, then, and turn to God, so that your sins may be wiped out, that times of refreshing may come from the Lord.”
Sin is a heavy burden and it makes our souls parched and dehydrated. But repentance is the oasis. Turn to God, come to the cross, run to Jesus, and He will refresh your soul with the cool waters of His mercy.
Proverbs 28:13 – “Whoever conceals their sins does not prosper, but the one who confesses and renounces them finds mercy.”
Covering sin is like painting over mold; the decay remains. But confession brings it into the light where God’s mercy cleanses completely. Do you see the cross? If so, you will see streams of mercy pouring down, and grace running freely, like Jesus' blood, is available at the cross. Yes, God has a flood of mercy for you, and like the flood in Noah's day which covered the mountains to a depth of more than 20 feet, God's flood of grace covers your sin completely (Micah 7:18-19).
2 Chronicles 30:9b – “For the Lord your God is gracious and compassionate. He will not turn his face from you if you return to him.”
A father never refuses his weeping child. Run to Him. His arms are open.
Matthew 3:8 – “Produce fruit in keeping with repentance.”
True repentance is not empty words; it is a tree bending under the weight of good fruit. A changed heart bears visible evidence. The smoker stops smoking. The porn viewer turns away from it. The drunkard makes a hard about-face to leave it behind. The overeater learns moderation. The liar begins speaking the truth. The grudge bearer freely forgives. The harsh husband learns to love, and live in meekness and understanding with his wife. The headstrong wife turns to submitting to her husband. 
2 Peter 3:9 – “The Lord is not slow in keeping his promise, as some understand slowness. Instead he is patient with you, not wanting anyone to perish, but everyone to come to repentance.”
God delays judgment because He longs to save. It is not too late for anyone who is still breathing.
Matthew 4:17 – “From that time on Jesus began to preach, ‘Repent, for the kingdom of heaven has come near.’”
The King has come. The time is now. Will you turn to Him?
James 4:8 – “Come near to God and he will come near to you. Wash your hands, you sinners, and purify your hearts, you double-minded.”
Sin leaves stains, but the blood of Christ makes us clean. Draw near; He is waiting.
Revelation 3:19 – “Those whom I love I rebuke and discipline. So be earnest and repent.”
God’s rebuke is love’s sharp edge, cutting away what destroys. Yield to Him, and in earnestness turn from your sin, stop believing lies and making excuses, and repent with all seriousness of mind with a desire to obey God.
Ezekiel 18:32 – “For I take no pleasure in the death of anyone, declares the Sovereign Lord. Repent and live!”
A lifeboat is beside the sinking ship, even as the cross stands beside a captive sinner. This is because God does not delight in our death so He put His Son to death in our place. Now get in the boat. Kneel at the cross. Open your hands and let the idols fall to the ground. Lift your hands high in worship to the One who sunk so low to rescue you.
Joel 2:13 – “Rend your heart and not your garments. Return to the Lord your God, for he is gracious and compassionate, slow to anger and abounding in love, and he relents from sending calamity.”
God does not want a show of only sorrow; He wants a broken heart that He can mend.
Acts 17:30 – “In the past God overlooked such ignorance, but now he commands all people everywhere to repent.”
This is not a suggestion. It is a command from Almighty God.
Luke 13:3 – “I tell you, no! But unless you repent, you too will all perish.”
There is no other way. Repent or perish. Jesus saves all who come to Him.
Luke 15:10 – “In the same way, I tell you, there is rejoicing in the presence of the angels of God over one sinner who repents.”
All of heaven sings when a sinner kneels at the cross. Is there something you need to turn away from today? Will the angels rejoice over you today?
Proverbs 1:23 – “Repent at my rebuke! Then I will pour out my thoughts to you, I will make known to you my teachings.”
The path to wisdom begins with repentance. Today, hear God’s call. Repent! Turn from sin, run to the cross, and live. His mercy waits, His grace is ready, His love will never fail, and you will discover Him pouring out His thoughts to you so that you will have the mind of Christ (1 Corinthians 2:16). The time is now.
How does genuine repentance lead to transformation in a believer’s life according to Scripture?


Biblical Illustration: King Rehoboam
King Rehoboam, son of Solomon, had every opportunity to lead Israel in the ways of righteousness. Yet, he hardened his heart and refused to listen to wise counsel, choosing instead to oppress the people of God. As a result, ten of the twelve tribes rebelled, and the kingdom was divided (1 Kings 12:1-20). Rehoboam’s pride led to ruin, yet God was not done calling him to repentance.
Later, when Egypt attacked Jerusalem, Rehoboam and the leaders of Judah humbled themselves before God. Because of their repentance, God granted them some measure of deliverance, saying, “Since they have humbled themselves, I will not destroy them but will soon give them deliverance” (2 Chronicles 12:7). However, Rehoboam’s repentance was short-lived, and he turned again to evil, leading to his downfall.
Rehoboam’s story is a warning: repentance must be sincere and lasting. Temporary humility does not save, but true and ongoing surrender does. Jesus Christ, the perfect King, invites us to repentance that leads to life, not destruction. Unlike Rehoboam, we must fully turn to God, and embrace our forgiveness purchased at the cross, so we can live.
What can we learn from Rehoboam’s story about the importance of sincere and lasting repentance?

Practical Application
True repentance is more than just feeling remorse—it is a complete turning away from sin and a wholehearted pursuit of God. The Bible makes it clear that genuine repentance leads to transformation, bringing us into a restored relationship with Him. But how do we live out true repentance in our daily lives? Scripture provides clear guidance on turning from sin, seeking God’s mercy, trusting in Christ, and bearing fruit that reflects a changed heart. When we walk in repentance, we experience the fullness of God’s grace and become a light to others, pointing them to the salvation found in Jesus.

    Turn away from sin – Repentance means forsaking sin, not just feeling sorry (Isaiah 55:7). True repentance requires action. It is a deliberate choice to turn from sinful ways and walk in obedience to God. 
    Seek God's mercy – Cry out to Him, and He will forgive (Psalm 51:1-2). God’s mercy is endless, and He eagerly welcomes those who turn to Him with sincere hearts, offering complete forgiveness and restoration to all who come to Him for pardon.
    Trust in Jesus Christ – Only His sacrifice cleanses sin (Romans 3:24-25).  No amount of good deeds can erase sin; only the blood of Jesus has the power to cleanse. True faith in Christ means relying completely on His finished work on the cross and living in the freedom of His grace.
    Bear fruit in keeping with repentance – Live in a way that reflects true change (Galatians 5:22-23). A life transformed by Christ is evident through the fruit of the Spirit—love, joy, peace, patience, kindness, goodness, faithfulness, gentleness, and self-control. True repentance produces visible, lasting change that glorifies God.
    Draw near to God daily – Through prayer and His Word, remain close to Him (James 4:8). A repentant heart continues seeking God daily, growing in faith and obedience. Through prayer, Scripture, and worship, we align our hearts with His will and find strength to walk in righteousness.
    Encourage others to repent – Share the good news of Christ’s salvation (2 Corinthians 5:20). As ambassadors of Christ, we are called to share the hope of the gospel. Urging others to turn to God is an act of love, pointing them to the grace and forgiveness we have found in Him.

How can you actively demonstrate true repentance in your daily life?

Day 93: Our Bodies Are God's Temple
Your Body is the Temple of The Holy Spirit
Key Passage
Do you not know that your bodies are temples of the Holy Spirit, who is in you, whom you have received from God? You are not your own; you were bought at a price. Therefore honor God with your bodies.
1 Corinthians 6:19-20
On our Setting Captives Free - 365 Marco Polo group, the request came in to discuss what it means that our bodies are the temple of the Holy Spirit. I love requests like this and offer the following thoughts on this subject.
The temple of God was a sacred place, set apart for His presence. It was not an ordinary building, it was where heaven touched earth, where God’s glory dwelled. The New Testament reveals a profound truth: our bodies are now that temple. We are not our own; we belong to God, purchased at the highest cost—the blood of Jesus.
To grasp what this means, let’s consider five key truths about God’s temple in the Old Testament and how they apply to our bodies today:

    The Temple Was Built for God's Glory – Solomon’s temple was magnificent, crafted with the finest materials to reflect God's splendor (1 Kings 8:10-11). Likewise, our bodies are designed to display His glory; not through gold and stone, but through lives of holiness and worship (Romans 12:1).
    The Temple Was a Place of Worship – Sacrifices and offerings were made daily in the temple as acts of devotion (2 Chronicles 7:12). Today, our bodies are living sacrifices, called to worship God through obedience, purity, and surrender to Christ (Romans 6:13).
    The Temple Was Kept Pure – God commanded that the temple remain undefiled; anything unclean was driven out (2 Chronicles 29:15-16). Our bodies, as temples, must also be kept holy, avoiding sin and resisting the desires of the flesh (1 Thessalonians 4:3-4). Whereas when we were unbelievers we were at war with God and had simply made peace with our sin, not able to fight it so we just gave in, now in Christ we are peace with God and at war with sin. Jesus' death on the cross was to purify the temple of our bodies.
    The Temple Was the Dwelling Place of God – The Holy of Holies (or Most Holy Place) housed the very presence of God (Exodus 25:8). Now, through Christ, the Holy Spirit dwells within believers, making our bodies the sacred dwelling place of God Himself (Ephesians 2:22).
    The Temple Was Bought at a Cost – David wanted to build the temple but was told his son Solomon would do it. However, David gathered materials, saying, “I will not sacrifice to the Lord my God burnt offerings that cost me nothing” (2 Samuel 24:24). In a far greater way, Jesus gave His own life to make us His temple. We were bought with His blood, redeemed for His purpose (1 Peter 1:18-19).

Because we belong to Christ, we must overcome sin, put away bad habits, and walk in the new life He has given us. The world tempts us to defile our bodies, but the cross crucifies our sins and calls us to holiness. Let us honor God with our bodies, remembering that we are His sacred temple, set apart for His glory.
Tomorrow we will consider how Jesus Christ on earth was God's Temple, and how that helps us overcome sin. 
How can we honor God with our bodies as His temple, based on the truths of the Old Testament temple?

Biblical Illustration: Daniel
Daniel was a young man taken from Jerusalem and forced to live in Babylon, a land filled with idolatry and immorality. Though surrounded by a culture that indulged in sin, Daniel refused to defile himself. When offered the king’s food and wine—things likely sacrificed to idols—he “resolved not to defile himself” (Daniel 1:8). He treated his body as a holy temple, set apart for God.
But Daniel’s holiness wasn’t just about diet. His entire life was one of purity and devotion. When a law was passed forbidding prayer to anyone but the king, Daniel continued to pray to God three times a day (Daniel 6:10). His body was not his own; it belonged to the Lord.
The gospel shines brightly in Daniel’s story. Just as he was thrown into the lions’ den for his faithfulness, Jesus was handed over to death for His obedience. But just as God shut the mouths of the lions and delivered Daniel, Jesus rose from the grave, conquering sin and death.
Like Daniel, we are called to honor God with our bodies. We must resist the temptations of the world, knowing that we belong to Christ, who purchased us with His blood.
What can we learn from Daniel’s example of honoring God with his body and resisting worldly temptations?

Practical Application
Practically speaking, what does it mean that our bodies are temples of the Holy Spirit? What can we do daily to show this truth to those around us? Here are some truths from God's Word.

    Offer your body as a living sacrifice – Surrender every part of yourself to God in worship and obedience (Romans 12:1).
    Flee from sin – Just as impurity was removed from the temple, we must turn from habits that defile our bodies (1 Corinthians 6:18). No more just laying down in sin, stand up and run! Fight! Learn how to overcome!
    Acknowledge that you are filled with the Holy Spirit – God’s presence rules in your life through prayer and the Word (Ephesians 5:18).
    Glorify God in daily actions – Honor Him in what you eat, drink, and do (1 Corinthians 10:31).
    Remember you were bought with a price – Live in gratitude for Christ’s sacrifice, walking in holiness (1 Peter 1:18-19).

How can you honor God with your body as a temple of the Holy Spirit today?

Day 94: Jesus Tabernacled Among Us
Freedom At The Tabernacle
Key Passage
And the Word became flesh and dwelt among us, and we have seen His glory, glory as of the only Son from the Father, full of grace and truth.
John 1:14
Teaching
Knowing all of Scripture is essential for helping us overcome sin. Yesterday we looked at how our bodies are the temple of the Holy Spirit because Jesus bought us to live in us. But there is some more we can learn about the Tabernacle, which will help us as well.
Jesus: The True Tabernacle
The phrase in John 1:14 “dwelt among us” literally means “tabernacled”—Jesus pitched His tent in our midst, just as the tabernacle stood in the center of Israel’s camp. The Old Testament tabernacle was where God’s presence dwelled, the meeting place between God and man, where sacrifices were offered, and where sins were atoned for. Now, Jesus Himself is that place—God with us, dwelling not in a tent but in a body.
John 2:19-21 – “Jesus answered them, ‘Destroy this temple, and in three days I will raise it up.’ The Jews then said, ‘It has taken forty-six years to build this temple, and will You raise it up in three days?’ But He was speaking about the temple of His body.”
Jesus was saying that His body, not the stone temple, was the true meeting place between God and man. Just as the tabernacle housed God’s presence, so did Jesus. And just as sacrifices were slain in the Tabernacle, so Jesus came to offer Himself—once and for all. Jesus prophesied the gospel, that His body would be destroyed on the cross but raised up three days later.
Three Comparisons Between the Tabernacle and Jesus

    The Daily Sacrifices (Exodus 29:38-39) vs. Jesus’ One Time Sacrifice
    Every morning and evening, a lamb was slain in the Tabernacle, a never-ending reminder of sin. But Jesus, the Lamb of God, was sacrificed once and for all (Hebrews 10:10). His sacrifice was not repeated daily—it was sufficient forever. When we struggle with sin, we don’t need repeated sacrifices; we need to look to Christ, whose single offering has cleansed us permanently.
    The Day of Atonement (Leviticus 16) vs. Jesus’ Death on the Cross
    Once a year, the high priest entered the Most Holy Place with blood for the nation’s sins. But Jesus, our Great High Priest, entered heaven itself with His own blood (Hebrews 9:12). His atonement was not temporary—it was eternal. When guilt weighs us down, we don’t need another sacrifice; the cross removed our guilt and lifts us clear up to heaven.
    The Tabernacle’s Separation vs. Jesus’ Open Invitation
    In the Tabernacle, a thick veil separated the people from God’s presence. But at Jesus’ death, the temple veil was torn in two (Matthew 27:51) from top to bottom. This was God's "object lesson" teaching that sin no longer blocks our access to God—we are invited into His presence, washed clean.

Victory Over Sin
If Jesus is the true Tabernacle, then our hope in overcoming sin is found in Him. We don’t need repeated sacrifices, new rituals, or self-effort to cleanse us. We come to Jesus, our Tabernacle, who has already made the sacrifice, removed the separation, and now dwells in us by His Spirit. When sin tempts us, we don’t look to ourselves—we run to Him, who “tabernacled” among us to set us free. 
How does understanding Jesus as the true Tabernacle help us overcome sin and find victory in His finished work?

Biblical Illustration: Isaiah
Isaiah’s Encounter: From Unclean to Cleansed
Isaiah stood in the temple, and suddenly, the veil between heaven and earth seemed to vanish. He saw the Lord, high and exalted, His robe filling the temple (Isaiah 6:1). The scene was overwhelming—angels cried, “Holy, holy, holy,” and the very thresholds shook. Isaiah felt like a man standing in the Tabernacle’s Most Holy Place without a sacrifice.
“Woe is me! For I am lost; for I am a man of unclean lips, and I dwell in the midst of a people of unclean lips!” (Isaiah 6:5).
Isaiah knew the Tabernacle’s system: sin required sacrifice. But here he was, unclean, undone, standing before perfect holiness. No lamb, no priest, no offering in hand. But then, grace.
A seraph flew to him, carrying a burning coal from the altar. “Your guilt is taken away, and your sin atoned for.” (Isaiah 6:7). Let those words sin into your heart today. There was an altar of sacrifice 2,000 years ago called a cross, and Jesus died there to take your guilt away and atone for your sin out of love.
What Isaiah received in a vision, Jesus accomplished in reality. He is our atonement, the true Tabernacle who takes away sin not with burning coals, but with a bleeding body. When we feel overwhelmed by sin, we don’t need another sacrifice—we need Jesus, who has already made the offering. Like Isaiah, we are undone before God, but through Christ, we are made clean. 
What does Isaiah’s encounter in the temple teach us about how Jesus, as the true Tabernacle, cleanses us from sin?

Practical Application
It’s easy to fall into the trap of trying to overcome sin and shame on our own, but the truth is, that Jesus, our true Tabernacle, has already made the ultimate sacrifice for us. Just as Isaiah experienced grace and atonement when he acknowledged his sin, we too can approach God with confidence, knowing that Jesus has paid the price for our sins once and for all. This invitation to come before God and live in His presence boldly is not just for a moment of salvation, but a daily reality. By confessing our sins, resting in Christ’s sacrifice, preaching the gospel to ourselves, and living in worship, we reflect the transformative power of God’s grace in our lives. 
Some practical ways to implement this truth:

    Confess Your Sin – Like Isaiah, acknowledge your sin before God (1 John 1:9). Don’t hide—Jesus, our Tabernacle, has already made the atonement.
    Come to Christ Daily – Instead of trying to "fix" yourself, rest in the once-for-all sacrifice of Jesus (Hebrews 10:14).
    Enter God’s Presence Boldly – The veil is torn! Through Christ, we have direct access to God (Hebrews 4:16).
    Preach the Gospel to Yourself – When guilt feelings arise, remind yourself: "It is finished." (John 19:30). Remember the words told to Isaiah, “Your guilt is taken away, and your sin atoned for.” (Isaiah 6:7).
    Live in Worship – Like Isaiah, respond with, "Here am I! Send me." (Isaiah 6:8).

What are some ways you apply Jesus' sacrifice in your daily life?

Day 95: How To Overcome Sin
Walking By The Spirit
Because our battle with sin is life and death, because it lasts our entire life, Scripture is replete with how to overcome it. So many passages deal with this very subject. But instead of a checklist of behaviors to follow, the Bible's focus is on what Jesus did, and how we can overcome by seeing Him and walking with Him.
Today we are reminded that not only did Jesus remove our sin from us at the cross, but He purchased the Holy Spirit to live in us. The Bible teaches us how to walk by the Spirit, and in doing so we don't sin, we don't gratify our flesh. 
How do we overcome sin through the gospel of Jesus Christ? Let's look at Galatians 5:16-25 today and see.
Walking By the Spirit: The Path to Victory Over Sin
Galatians 5:16-25
Galatians 5:16 – “But I say, walk by the Spirit, and you will not gratify the desires of the flesh.”
Imagine walking on a narrow mountain trail with a steep drop on either side (Matthew 7:13-14). That's what this life is truly like, with sin drop-offs on both sides (Proverbs 4:14-15). If you follow your guide, step for step, you won’t fall away, even if you fall (Psalm 37:23-24). The Holy Spirit is our guide, leading us along the path of righteousness (Psalm 23:3). As we keep in step with Him, we leave no room for sin’s slippery footing (Galatians 5:16). The gospel assures us that Jesus walked this path perfectly (Hebrews 4:15), and through faith in Him, we receive the same Spirit who empowered His sinless life (Romans 8:11). This plan of God for making sinners righteous, and empowering their obedience, is beyond genius. It deals with the root problem and the daily problem. Wow!
Galatians 5:17 – “For the desires of the flesh are against the Spirit, and the desires of the Spirit are against the flesh, for these are opposed to each other, to keep you from doing the things you want to do.”
Picture two armies locked in battle; one fighting to enslave, the other to set free (Romans 7:23).
