Notice that what we must pay more careful attention to is the message of salvation, the message of the cross, the message of the resurrection of Jesus Christ from the dead, the message of putting faith in Jesus' bloody death for sins, and our forgiveness and eternal life through believing!

And God has not whispered salvation from a distant mountain; He has declared it from heaven’s throne through the life, death, and resurrection of Jesus (Luke 3:22; Acts 2:22-24). The apostles bore witness, miracles confirmed it, and the Holy Spirit still applies it to believing hearts (Ephesians 1:13-14). 
This message didn’t come through fog or fable; it is a blazing torch cutting through the night of our sin. 
Application

We must fix our eyes on Jesus (Hebrews 12:2), steer daily by His Word (Psalm 119:105), and drop the anchor of hope deep into the harbor of His finished work (Hebrews 6:19). To ignore such a salvation is to cast ourselves adrift; but to cling to it is to find safe harbor in the storm. Let us listen with holy attentiveness, for in the gospel, we hear the very heart of God.

In the 20th and 21st century church, it has become popular to limit the message of the cross to salvation only. This is a ploy of Satan, for if believers can become convinced the message of Jesus' shed blood is only for entering into salvation, then what more do we need with that message once we're saved? It's useless to us who are believers, since it is "the message of salvation." We don't need to pay attention to it any longer. 
How wrong this is! The message of the cross is indeed the message of salvation, but it is also the message of sanctification, the message of Christian growth, the message freeing us from sin, and the message that will keep us safe to the end.
"What does Hebrews 2:1–4 teach us about the importance of staying anchored in the gospel message?"

Biblical Illustration: Demas
The Drifting Heart of Demas
Demas once stood shoulder-to-shoulder with the apostle Paul, a fellow laborer in the gospel (Philemon 1:24). He had seen the power of Christ, heard the Word preached with fire, and tasted the fellowship of the saints. 
But somewhere along the way, the anchor lifted. The tides of the world tugged at his heart, and he began to drift. Paul’s final letter contains a sobering epitaph: “Demas, having loved this present world, has deserted me” (2 Timothy 4:10).
Demas didn’t run toward evil with fists raised; he simply loosened his grip on truth. The slow pull of worldly comfort, the ease of compromise, and the glitter of temporary gain led him away from the message of salvation. Like a ship that quietly slips from harbor, he drifted into danger.
But the gospel of Jesus Christ is the anchor that holds. Where Demas drifted, Christ died. Where we grow weary, Christ intercedes (Hebrews 7:25). In the cross, we see the peril of neglect and the price of rescue. We are called not merely to hear, but to hold fast (Hebrews 3:14). If Demas warns us, Jesus welcomes us; to return, to repent, and to be re-anchored in His redeeming grace.
"What does the story of Demas teach us about the importance of holding fast to the gospel?"

Practical Application
Practical Application: Staying Anchored in the Gospel
In a world filled with distractions and temptations, it is easy for our hearts to begin to drift away from the steadfast anchor of Christ. The following steps offer a practical guide for staying anchored in Him, ensuring our focus remains on His Word, His love, and His grace. By intentionally nurturing these spiritual practices, we can avoid drifting and remain secure in the truth of the gospel.

    Read and reflect daily on the Word of Christ to keep your heart tethered to truth (Colossians 3:16).
    Pray earnestly for a love that treasures Christ above the world (Psalm 73:25-26).
    Gather regularly with believers to stir one another to stay the course (Hebrews 10:24-25).
    Confess quickly when drifting begins, and return to the cross for fresh grace (1 John 1:9).
    Fix your eyes on Jesus, the Anchor of your soul and Captain of your salvation (Hebrews 6:19; 12:2).

Cling to Christ—and you won’t drift.
Which action listed above will you prioritize today to stay anchored in Christ and why?

Day 122: Troubled But Not Trampled
Weaknesses Are Not Worthlessness
Key Passage
But we have this treasure in jars of clay to show that this all-surpassing power is from God and not from us. We are hard pressed on every side, but not crushed; perplexed, but not in despair; persecuted, but not abandoned; struck down, but not destroyed.
2 Corinthians 4:7-9
Teaching
For much of my life, thoughts of guilt and condemnation could throw me to the ground and trample me. You know what I mean, the darkness can overwhelm us, our own thoughts accusing and condemning us, using real evidence of our lives against us. These thoughts used to be able to trample me and leave me spiritually disabled, nearly catatonic. 
Now, because the gospel of Jesus Christ has taken such a hold of me, I have weapons to fight the thoughts. I look at the cross and see that Jesus Christ was trampled to death by my guilt and shame so that I won't be, that He was condemned instead of me, and so now all the thoughts can do is trouble me, but not trample me.
Today, let's notice some lessons from 2 Corinthians 4:7-9:
Precious in Pottery (v. 7)
"Now we have this treasure in jars of clay to show that this surpassingly great power is from God and not from us" (2 Corinthians 4:7).
Like a priceless diamond housed in a simple clay pot, the glory of the gospel resides in our fragile human forms. We are mere vessels; cracked, imperfect, easily broken, and yet entrusted with the magnificent light of Christ's salvation. Our weakness doesn't diminish the treasure; rather, it magnifies the source of our strength, the glory of the gospel!
When the potter's hands shape common clay into a vessel for noble purposes (Jeremiah 18:6), the glory belongs to the craftsman, not the clay. Similarly, when God's power flows through our limitations, the praise returns to Him. Our very frailty becomes the canvas upon which God's grace is displayed most vividly (2 Corinthians 12:9).
The gospel shines brightest through the cracks of our humanity. Like a lantern with perforations that allow light to escape, our weaknesses become the very windows through which Christ's light reaches a darkened world. This divine exchange, His strength for our weakness, stands at the heart of the gospel (Isaiah 40:29-31).
Pressed but Preserved (v. 8a)
"We are hard pressed on all sides, but not crushed..." (2 Corinthians 4:8a).
Life presses in like an olive in the crushing mill, squeezed from every direction. Financial strains, relationship fractures, health concerns, not to mention the ever present temptation toward guilt and condemnation; these pressures are real and relentless. Yet like a sponge under pressure that maintains its essential nature, we are not annihilated by these forces. Christ is in us (Colossians 1:27)! 
Perplexed but Purposeful (v. 8 b- 9)
"...perplexed, but not in despair; 9 persecuted, but not forsaken; struck down, but not destroyed" (2 Corinthians 4:8b-9).
Questions swirl like autumn leaves in a whirlwind. Why this suffering? Where is God in this moment? Am I guilty still? How will I endure? We stand perplexed at life's twisted paths and relentless poundings, yet we are never utterly lost. Troubled but not trampled.
The cross itself appeared as history's greatest perplexity; God's Son apparently defeated, yet revealed itself as divine strategy (1 Corinthians 1:18-25). Our confusion doesn't nullify God's direction. Like Joseph in an Egyptian prison, what seems senseless to us fits perfectly in God's narrative (Genesis 50:20).
We may be struck down like trees in a violent storm, yet our roots, anchored in Christ's finished work, hold fast. The gospel promises not that we won't fall, but that we will rise again (Proverbs 24:16). Our perseverance doesn't stem from personal resilience but from the resurrection power that flows from our union with the One who conquered death itself (Philippians 3:10-11).
What does 2 Corinthians 4:7-9 teach us about the relationship between our weaknesses and God's power in our lives?

Illustration: Jody Cleveland
On the previous page, I wrote, "The gospel promises preservation, not exemption. Jesus himself was 'a man of sorrows, familiar with suffering' (Isaiah 53:3), yet remained unbroken in his divine purpose. When anxieties weigh heavy like storm clouds gathering overhead, the gospel provides not always calm skies but an unshakable anchor (Hebrews 6:19).
The pressing produces precious oil. Just as crushed grapes yield sweet wine, our trials extract the fragrance of Christ from our lives (Romans 8:28-29). The gospel transforms pressure from destructive to productive."
A good example of this is my wife, Jody. Two years ago, when we lost our 23-year-old son, Joshua, in a sudden and unexpected death, Jody about died, too. Literally. For months, I watched her grieve, her body convulse with the agony of losing one she gave birth to, whom she loved with her entire being. She describes it, "I felt like I couldn't breathe!". Such is the hurting heart of a mom who has lost her dear son.
And yet slowly, over time, through her seeking of the Lord, she began to have the desire to minister to other people who are hurting, facing death, and to their relatives. She had been a nurse before becoming a stay-at-home mom to raise our kids, so we prayed about it together and, since all our other children are grown and out of the house, she went back to nursing. Specifically to hospice nursing. Here she pours out the love of Jesus to those about to meet Him, and to their relatives who are hurting both before and after the death.
I have watched her minister so powerfully to patients, not only caring for their physical bodies, but for their hearts and souls. She ministers the gospel unashamedly, through tears, and has so much understanding and love for these hurting people. We have seen conversions to Christ and baptisms shortly before death; all because God is using Jody's pain to be a light to others who desperately need Jesus. She is greatly troubled, but not trampled. 
Have you had a similar experience of God using the painful things of your life to guide you into helping others? Please share.

Practical Application
When hardships press in, God doesn't call us to deny our struggles, but to respond through them with Christ-centered action. The following practices help us live as vessels of His strength, anchored in His truth, and useful for His glory even in our weakness.
When troubled but not trampled, respond with gospel-centered action:
• Remember your identity as Christ's vessel, intentionally presenting your weakness to God as an opportunity for His strength (2 Corinthians 12:9-10).
• Meditate daily on the finished work of Christ, anchoring your hope beyond present pressures (Colossians 3:1-3).
• Share your struggles with trusted believers, allowing the body of Christ to function as designed (Galatians 6:2).
• Practice thanksgiving in trials, recognizing God's refining purpose (1 Thessalonians 5:18).
• Speak gospel truth to your own heart when doubts arise, countering lies with Scripture (Psalm 42:5-6).
• Serve others despite your struggles, finding purpose in pointing to Christ amid your perplexities (2 Corinthians 1:3-4).
We can be troubled greatly by life, by pressing guilt, by thoughts of failure, by remembrances of past sins, but believers can never be trampled. What a powerful gospel we have, to remind us of why we can't be trampled!
Which gospel-centered practice do you most need to strengthen in this season of your life?

Day 123: Grieving With Gospel Hope
Loss and Pain
Key Passage
And so we will always be with the Lord.
1 Thessalonians 4:17
I'm sitting here in tears as I write today: one of our gospel sisters on the Setting Captives Free-365 Marco Polo group, Mary, just lost her husband in a tragic accident. 
The hearts of many people in our group are wounded and bleeding along with Mary. Though we've tried to offer words of comfort, words are sometimes the worst thing we can do for someone grieving. Jesus went to Lazarus' grave and cried with the hurting. This shows His comforting presence. Then He raised Lazarus from the dead. This shows His comforting power. 
Today, we will see from 1 Thessalonians 4:13-18 that He still does, and will do, those two things: 
1. Clarity in Our Sorrow (v.13)
"Brothers, we do not want you to be uninformed about those who sleep in death, so that you will not grieve like the rest, who are without hope" (1 Thessalonians 4:13). 
Paul begins with a tender pastoral tone, like a shepherd gently leading grieving sheep away from despair. He says we are not to grieve as others do who have no hope. We can see here that grief is not denied, but defined. For the believer, grief is not a bottomless pit that we can never get out of, but rather it's more like a dark valley with a sunrise coming. 
Our sorrow is real, it is relentless, coming at us in wave after unexpected wave, but it is also reframed by the resurrection. Unlike every other dead religious leader, Jesus rose from the dead, and His resurrection was one of the most-witnessed events in history. And Jesus will raise our loved ones. 
Like a traveler who mourns in the night yet walks toward the dawn, we sorrow deeply, but with eyes fixed on Christ’s return (John 14:1-3), our hope being anchored by Jesus' empty tomb.
2. Confidence in the Savior (v.14-16)
"For since we believe that Jesus died and rose again, we also believe that God will bring with Jesus those who have fallen asleep in Him. 15 By the word of the Lord, we declare to you that we who are alive and remain until the coming of the Lord will by no means precede those who have fallen asleep. 16 For the Lord Himself will descend from heaven with a loud command, with the voice of an archangel, and with the trumpet of God, and the dead in Christ will be the first to rise" (1 Thessalonians 4:14-16).

In this section of Scripture, Paul moves from clarity to confidence. Jesus died and rose again, and so too God will bring with Him those who have died in Christ. The gospel is not just a story; it is a structure—a bridge over the abyss of death. Jesus, having died for all (2 Corinthians 5:15), carries all people who have died in Him over this bridge to eternal life.
Just as Noah’s ark carried his family above the flood (Genesis 7), Jesus Christ carries His people above judgment through His resurrection. The shout, the trumpet, and the voice of the archangel (v.16) are not chaotic sounds; they are the symphony of salvation. The dead in Christ rise first, not last, because death has lost its sting (1 Corinthians 15:52-55). Death is not the end of the story, but the unveiling of a new chapter: eternal life for all who believe.
3. Comfort in the Second Coming (v.17-18)
"After that, we who are alive and remain will be caught up together with them in the clouds to meet the Lord in the air. And so we will always be with the Lord. Therefore encourage one another with these words" (1 Thessalonians 4:17-18). 
Finally, Paul turns to comfort. We will be caught up together with them: reunited, not only with our loved ones, but with our Lord. Like a bride longing for her groom (Revelation 19:7), the church waits for Christ not with dread, but with desire. We will be with the Lord forever, not for a moment, not for a millennium only, but for eternity. 
Therefore, we comfort one another with these words (v.18). The gospel gives us a vocabulary of victory even in the valley of death. We comfort one another with the gospel: Jesus died and rose again, and Jesus is coming again.
Let us grieve, yes, but let our grief be soaked in gospel hope. For every tear that falls at a graveside, from Adam and Eve at Abel's grave, to you and I who lose loved ones, and to Mary who lost her husband, there is a promise written in heaven: "Behold, I make all things new" (Revelation 21:4-5).
And lastly, what about those who lose those they love who are not believers, or who may not have made clear confessions of faith in Jesus? Are they in heaven or hell? Well, we don't know for sure, but this much I do know: the God I serve stops everything He is doing to come to the side of the one who is dying. How do we know this? Because the Bible tells us He leaves the ninety-nine for the one (Luke 15:4), and at the moment our loved one is dying, he or she is the one. 
This is why my wife, Jody, whenever she is ministering to one who is about to die, always says, "When Jesus comes to you, say yes."
A simple summary of this writing in 1 Thessalonians 4: "Jesus died and rose again...so we will always be with the Lord." 
How does understanding 1 Thessalonians 4:13-18 reshape the way believers grieve the loss of loved ones?

Day 123: Grieving With Gospel Hope
Biblical Illustration: Martha and Mary
When Lazarus died, his sisters, Mary and Martha, were plunged into sorrow (John 11:17-37). Their grief was real and raw. Martha confessed her faith, yet her heart wrestled with the sting of loss. Mary wept openly, her tears pooling at the feet of Jesus. They grieved, but not without hope, for standing before them was the Resurrection and the Life. 
Jesus wept with them, showing that divine compassion does not erase grief but transforms it. Then, like a sunrise breaking through heavy fog, Jesus called Lazarus out of the tomb. Death obeyed the voice of Life. Lazarus walked out, bound but breathing; a living testimony that death does not have the final word. 
This moment foreshadowed Christ’s own resurrection, where He would rise not only for one man but for all who trust in Him. The sorrow of Mary and Martha reminds us that grief is inevitable, but Jesus' power reminds us that resurrection is invincible. 
In Christ, every grave is a temporary resting place, every tear has an expiration date, and every heartache will one day be swallowed up in victory (1 Corinthians 15:54). The gospel assures us that our grieving is real, but our hope is far more real.
What does the story of Lazarus teach us about the relationship between grief and hope in Christ?


Practical Application
In seasons of grief, we are not left to carry our sorrow alone. Scripture gives us clear, hope-filled steps to walk through heartache with Christ at our side. Here are some ways to anchor your soul in His promises as you journey through the valley.

    Bring your grief to Jesus in prayer, trusting that He weeps with you and walks beside you (Hebrews 4:15-16).
    Anchor your heart daily in the gospel, remembering Christ’s death and resurrection as your sure hope (Romans 6:4-5).
    Comfort one another with Scripture, gently reminding fellow believers that we will be reunited in Christ (1 Thessalonians 4:18).
    Look for glimpses of resurrection even now, as God brings life out of loss and hope out of sorrow (Romans 8:28).
    Dwell on the promise of forever, knowing that one day, Jesus will wipe away every tear (Revelation 21:4).

You are not alone in this valley; He walks with you.
Which practice listed can you intentionally incorporate into your daily life this week to draw closer to Christ in your grief?

Day 124: Jesus Is Near To The Hurting
Jesus Is Near To The Broken
Key Passage
The Lord is close to the brokenhearted and saves those who are crushed in spirit.
Psalm 34:18
This day is dedicated to Mary.
Teaching
Jesus Is Close When We Are Hurting
 (Psalm 34:17 - 20)
When storms rage and hearts break like fragile glass, when the agony of our loss overwhelms us many times throughout the day, Psalm 34 reminds us that Jesus draws near. He is not the distant judge but the present Shepherd, bending low to lift His hurting sheep.
Psalm 34:17 – "The righteous cry out, and the Lord hears them; he delivers them from all their troubles."

When the soul cries, not a polished prayer but a raw, broken sob, the Lord bends His ear to listen, getting closer to us than we had previously known. Like a loving Father kneeling beside His injured child, God hears even the whisper of our wounds, as He cherishes our broken hearts. 
Our deliverance from this pain, and the brokenness is not always immediate escape, but rather His presence in the trouble (Isaiah 43:2). Psalms 23:4 reminds us "Even though I walk through the valley of the shadow of death, I will fear no evil, for You are with me; Your rod and Your staff, they comfort me."
Look to the cross and see that Christ, our Deliverer, entered the deepest trouble to guarantee our final rescue from sin and sorrow (2 Corinthians 1:10).
Psalm 34:18 – "The Lord is close to the brokenhearted and saves those who are crushed in spirit."
Broken hearts are not discarded in God’s economy; they are invitations for His nearness. Like a potter tenderly gathering broken clay, Christ comes close to remake us (Jeremiah 18:4). His salvation is not reserved for the strong, but for those crushed and emptied. At the cross, Jesus Himself was crushed for our iniquities (Isaiah 53:5), proving forever that God will meet the broken with mercy, since He met His Son in judgment for our sakes.
Psalm 34:19 – "The righteous person may have many troubles, but the Lord delivers him from them all."

Trouble clings like burrs to the righteous, but the Lord promises full and final deliverance. Even when troubles multiply like storm clouds, they cannot outnumber God's mercies (Lamentations 3:22-23). In Christ, every sorrow we endure becomes a seed for future joy, a bridge to more intimate fellowship with Him who “bore our griefs and carried our sorrows” (Isaiah 53:4).
Psalm 34:20 – "He protects all his bones, not one of them will be broken."
This verse points us straight to Jesus. As He hung on the cross, the soldiers did not break His legs, fulfilling this prophecy exactly (John 19:36). His unbroken bones symbolize His perfect, unbroken sacrifice; securing unbreakable promises for us. Even when our hearts feel shattered, the body of Christ reminds us: God’s love and salvation for us remain whole, unbroken, and sure.
Final Thought:
When we are hurting, we often seek sinful comforts to numb the pain; old habits, false refuges. But Jesus, the Man of Sorrows, stands nearer than the shadows. His gospel invites us to run to His wounds for healing, His heart for comfort, His cross for love, and His empty tomb for power. When we cling to Him in our brokenness, we are not overcome; we are held.
How does Psalm 34 teach us that God responds to our brokenness, and what does this reveal about His character in times of suffering?

Biblical Illustration: Hagar in the Wilderness
Hagar knew the agony of being brokenhearted and alone. Cast out by Abraham and Sarah, she wandered into the desert with her young son, her water gone, her hope evaporating like mist under the burning sun (Genesis 21:14-16). Sitting at a distance, she lifted up her voice and wept, believing death was certain. She was like a single dying leaf blown into a barren land; unseen, unheard, unloved, forgotten.
But God heard. God saw. God loved. The Angel of the Lord called to her from heaven, opening her eyes to a well of water nearby and promising that her son would become a great nation (Genesis 21:17-19). God was not far off; He was near; the God who sees (Genesis 16:13).
This beautiful moment points forward to the gospel of Jesus Christ. Just as Hagar was seen, heard, and rescued, so too are we when we cry out in our brokenness. Jesus Christ came into the "wilderness" of our sin-sick world to redeem us. He is the Living Water (John 4:14), the Well that never runs dry, offering hope to those who think all hope is lost. In Him, the abandoned are adopted, the broken are blessed, and the outcasts are brought in.
What does Hagar's story teach us about God's presence and care in moments of abandonment and despair, and how does it point us to Jesus?

Practical Application
When life leaves us feeling abandoned, overwhelmed, or forgotten, the story of Hagar reminds us that God sees, hears, and draws near. In our moments of deepest sorrow, we are not alone. Jesus calls us to turn from empty comforts and run to Jesus—our Living Water, our Redeemer, and our ever-present help in trouble. When sorrow breaks over you like a crashing wave, run to Jesus, not to sinful escapes. When life feels like a desert and hope seems lost, run to Jesus, the Living Water.

    Cry out honestly to Him, as Hagar did (Genesis 21:16-17).
    Trust that He sees you even when no one else does (Genesis 16:13).
    Drink deeply from His promises of life and hope (John 4:14).
    Rest in His nearness, not in sinful escapes (Psalm 34:18).
    Fix your heart on the cross, where Christ secured your adoption forever (Galatians 4:4-7).

Even in the wilderness of pain, Christ is the Well who will never run dry.
When you are feeling overwhelmed or alone, what specific actions will you take to turn to Jesus the Living Water instead of falling back on old patterns or false comforts?

Day 125: A Sheep or a Snake?
Do You Listen to God's Word?
Key Passage
I have other sheep that are not of this fold. I must bring them in as well, and they will listen to My voice. Then there will be one flock and one shepherd.
John 10:16
Teaching
"My Sheep Will Listen to My Voice"
(John 10:14-16)
1. The Shepherd’s Personal Knowledge (John 10:14)
"I am the good shepherd; I know my sheep and my sheep know me."
Jesus is not a distant, disinterested deity, He is a devoted Shepherd. He knows each of His sheep intimately: their weaknesses, wounds, and wandering ways. Like a shepherd who calls each sheep by name (John 10:3), Jesus calls His followers individually, tenderly, and personally. Sheep are relational; they listen for the familiar voice that has fed, led, and rescued them.

Snakes, however, are cold, solitary creatures. They have no ears; instead, they flick their tongues to "taste" the air, led by appetite and instinct (Genesis 3:1). What a picture of fallen humanity: led not by the voice of God but by the cravings of the flesh (James 1:14)! This was my life, day after day being led around by my appetites, tongue a-wagging, while refusing to listen to God and godly people. Unfortunately it can still be me, if I give in to my flesh.
But the gospel announces a better way: the Shepherd came to seek and save the lost (Luke 19:10), to transform snakes into sheep through His cross and resurrection, and by the power of the Holy Spirit.
2. The Shepherd’s Perfect Union (John 10:15)
"Just as the Father knows me and I know the Father—and I lay down my life for the sheep."
The unity between Jesus and the Father is deep, divine, and eternal (John 17:21). This same perfect love and fellowship is now extended to the sheep. At the cross, Jesus laid down His life to rescue us from being deceived serpents, giving us new hearts that long to listen (Ezekiel 36:26).

While snakes hiss and coil in rebellion, sheep gather close, leaning into the sound of the Shepherd's voice, the sound of grace and truth. They know the truth that by His death, Jesus didn’t merely give sheep better habits; He gave them a new nature (2 Peter 1:4). 
Through Christ’s sacrifice, His loving death for us (John 15:13), we are drawn into the everlasting embrace of Father and Son (John 14:23, Romans 8:39).
3. The Shepherd’s Pursuing Call (John 10:16)
"I have other sheep that are not of this sheep pen. I must bring them also. They too will listen to my voice."
The voice of Jesus crosses deserts and oceans, calling the nations to Himself (Isaiah 49:6; Revelation 7:9). His mission is magnetic: His sheep will listen, will follow, will come home. His grace is literally irresistible and His call to His sheep is effectual.

Snakes slither away at the sound of the gospel; sheep are stirred to life. Like Lazarus at the tomb, Jesus’ sheep rise at His voice (John 11:43-44). This is because the gospel is not a mere suggestion; it is a resurrection command, and the sheep respond, but only to the Shepherd. It’s as if they would sing a slightly modified version of the song, saying, "I only have ears for You"—listening intently to the voice of the Shepherd alone.
The reality is, snakes have no ears; they are led around by their tongues. Ever met people who only talk? Who can't seem to hear a word you say? Who are more interested in talking than in listening and understanding (Proverbs 18:2; James 1:19)? This is the snake lifestyle, and that of the Pharisees (Matthew 3:7; Matthew 23:27–28) and merely religious people (2 Timothy 3:5); and it is the lifestyle of bondage (John 8:34).
Sheep, on the other hand, listen carefully (Hebrews 2:1), hear and respond, follow Jesus, and are free. Which are you?
What does it mean to truly listen to the voice of Jesus?

Biblical Illustration: Potiphar's Wife
Potiphar’s wife was like a serpent: deaf to truth, led only by her appetites (Genesis 39:7-20). When she tempted Joseph, he answered with three piercing arguments that should have stopped her cold and made her consider her ways. 
First, Joseph spoke of his master: “My master trusts me and has withheld nothing from me except you” (Genesis 39:8-9). Loyalty should have silenced her lust. 
Second, he reminded her of her marriage: “You are his wife.” Her very identity demanded faithfulness. 
Third, Joseph lifted her eyes to her Maker: “How then could I do this great wickedness and sin against God?” (Genesis 39:9).
Each word was like a staff striking the serpent, but Potiphar’s wife had no ears to hear. Immediately after Joseph made those three striking points, the Bible says, "Although Potiphar’s wife spoke to Joseph day after day, he refused to go to bed with her or even be near her" (Genesis 39:10). It's clear, Potiphar's wife didn't hear a thing Joseph said, she continued to speak to him day after day.
Like a snake flicking its tongue but never listening, she slithered deeper into deception, ignoring honor, marriage, and the fear of the Lord. Her heart was hardened, her mind clouded, her path set. She did not repent, but falsely accused Joseph, wrapping her lies around him like coils.
Joseph, by contrast, listened to the voice of his Shepherd and fled (Genesis 39:12). He chose faithfulness over fleeting pleasure (Hebrews 11:24–25), loyalty over lust (1 Corinthians 6:18), the voice of God over the hiss of sin (John 10:27). Where snakes are deaf, sheep listen and live (John 10:4; Deuteronomy 30:19–20).
What does Joseph’s response to temptation teach us about recognizing and resisting the voice of sin?

Practical Application
In a world filled with distractions and deceit, it is vital to train our hearts to recognize and respond to the voice of our Shepherd. His Word lights our path, His Spirit strengthens our resolve, and His love draws us near. These practical actions will help you walk closely with Christ and resist the hiss of sin.

    Listen daily to the Shepherd’s voice by reading and meditating on Scripture (John 10:27; Psalm 119:105).
    Flee from sin swiftly like Joseph did, trusting God to provide a way of escape (1 Corinthians 10:13).
    Honor Christ in every relationship, remembering you are first accountable to Him (Colossians 3:23-24).
    Preach the gospel to your heart, reminding yourself that Jesus laid down His life to make you His own (John 10:11).
    Pray for a soft, listening heart, asking God to keep you from the serpent’s deceit (Hebrews 3:13).

The Shepherd’s voice leads to life; follow it closely today!
What is one specific way you can tune your heart to the Shepherd’s voice today and turn away from a distraction or temptation that pulls you from Him?

Day 126: Forget the Past
Don't Dwell on the Past
Key Passage
Do not call to mind the former things; pay no attention to the things of old. 19 Behold, I am about to do something new; even now it is coming. Do you not see it? Indeed, I will make a way in the wilderness and streams in the desert.
Isaiah 43:18-19
Teaching
When we have sinned in life-altering ways, we always remember it. It's hard to shake the plaguing thoughts, the vivid memories, the pain caused by our sin. It's easy to get sucked into thought strongholds of guilt, remembering the past with deep regret.
But God's Word tells us to forget the past, even as He promises to remember our sins no more (Hebrews 8:12). 
In Isaiah 43, God commanded Israel to forget the past, and He promised that a time was coming when He would do a new thing. This is a specific reference to the coming of the Messiah, who would fulfill the Old Covenant and usher in a New Covenant. 
1. Forget the Former (Isaiah 43:18)
"Forget the former things; do not dwell on the past."

The Lord called Israel and tells us now in this text, to let go of the crumbling ruins of the old covenant, where righteousness was demanded but never fully delivered by fallen man (Exodus 19:5-6). Under the Mosaic law, every crack of failure was met with the hammer of judgment (Deuteronomy 28:15). 
Yet the cross would swing a new door wide open; the door of forgiveness. Like trying to sail a ship chained to a sinking anchor, clinging to the old covenant drags our soul down. It stirs up guilt and shame, and more sin. 
Christ, the true Ark, has come to carry us through the storm of sin into the calm waters of mercy (Hebrews 8:6-13). Let the wreckage of self-righteousness sink behind you. Don't dwell on your failures, but on His faithfulness. "Brothers, I do not consider myself yet to have taken hold of it. But one thing I do: Forgetting what is behind and straining toward what is ahead, 14 I press on toward the goal to win the prize of God’s heavenly calling in Christ Jesus" (Philippians 3:13-14).
2. Focus on the Fresh (Isaiah 43:19a)
"See, I am doing a new thing! Now it springs up; do you not perceive it?"

The "new thing" is no temporary patch on an old garment (Matthew 9:16); it is a new creation altogether (2 Corinthians 5:17). Through Christ, God was sowing seeds of salvation that would blossom into a garden of grace. 
In Isaiah 42:1-4, the Servant is announced; the gentle Redeemer who would not break the bruised reed. This is no dusty renovation but a divine revolution. The gospel sprouted up in dry, cracked soil; grace in the wilderness of judgment, forgiveness where only failure once grew. Open your eyes, God says, and see the sunrise of redemption breaking over the law-darkened land.
3. Follow the Flow (Isaiah 43:19b)
"I am making a way in the wilderness and streams in the wasteland."

The wasteland of works-based righteousness (Romans 3:20) is being washed over by rivers of living water (John 7:38). Jesus forged a way where none existed; a blood-paved path through the desert of despair. He floods our barren souls with grace, like rivers suddenly bursting into a desert, bringing green life where death once reigned. 
The New Covenant doesn’t call us to build our own bridges to God; it provides the Bridge Himself (John 14:6). As God promised newness after judgment (Isaiah 42:9), so He delivers it fully in Christ.
Conclusion:
Don’t dwell on the past—dwell on the gospel! Don't let the vivid reminders of failure, the guilty pangs of conscience, the deep regret, and sorrow leave you in ruins. The old covenant demanded; the new covenant delivers. The law condemned; the gospel cleanses. The old way cursed; the new way blesses (Galatians 3:10-14). Lift your eyes from the cracked ground of self-effort to the rivers of redemption flowing from the cross.
How can you stop dwelling on past failures and instead focus on the new thing God is doing in your life through the gospel of Jesus Christ?


Biblical Illustration: Peter
Peter’s New Memory
Peter knew the sound of shame. Every time a rooster crowed, it must have pierced his heart like a jagged dagger, dragging him back to the night he denied his Lord (Luke 22:61-62). The echo of that rooster became a cruel reminder of his failure, a soundtrack of guilt that could have haunted him forever. 
But Jesus, full of mercy, would not let Peter dwell on the past. On the shores of Galilee, beside a crackling fire and a breakfast of fish (John 21:9-13), Jesus called Peter not to guilt, but to grace. Three times Peter had denied; now three times Jesus restored him: “Feed my sheep” (John 21:15-17).
Christ didn’t erase Peter’s memory; He redeemed it. From that day forward, every time Peter smelled the salty aroma of fish cooking or broke bread by the sea, he wouldn’t just hear the rooster; he would remember the Redeemer. The bitter crow of condemnation was swallowed up by the sweet taste of forgiveness.
In the same way, Jesus invites us to stop feasting on old failures and start savoring the meal of mercy He has prepared through the gospel. The past is not our prison, His love is our new memory.

Just as Peter had to stop dwelling on the rooster’s crow and start dwelling on the Risen Christ, so we are commanded in Isaiah 43:18-19 to forget the former things and behold the new. The gospel is God's "new thing": a fresh start in Christ where old sins are forgiven, old shames are washed away, and new life springs up. 
When we feast on the grace of Jesus, our memories no longer chain us to the past; they point us to the cross, the empty tomb, and the endless streams of living water He has made in the wasteland of our lives.
Why is it important for us to focus on the grace and forgiveness of Jesus rather than allowing our past failures to define us?

Practical Application
Amid the noise of past mistakes and regrets, it's easy to fall into the trap of guilt and shame. Yet, as believers in Christ, we are invited to renew our minds each day, focusing not on our failures but on the power of the gospel. By meditating on Christ’s love and victory, we can replace the memories that bind us with fresh reminders of His grace. His Word restores and strengthens us, and as we share the message of forgiveness with others, we celebrate the freedom He has given us. Let the redeeming love of your Savior silence the echoes of guilt and lead you into the new life He offers.

    Renew your mind daily by meditating on the gospel, not your guilt (Romans 12:2).
    Replace old memories of failure with fresh reminders of Christ’s love and victory (2 Corinthians 5:17).
    Feed on His Word, letting it restore and strengthen you (John 21:15-17).
    Celebrate grace by sharing the good news of forgiveness with others (2 Corinthians 5:18-19).

Let the "roosters" of your past be drowned out by the song of your Savior’s redeeming love.
What specific actions can you take each day to meditate on His love and victory over your failures?


Day 127: Your Record Has Been Replaced
Jesus is Your New Record
Many people around the world have been blessed by the writing of Erick Hurt, who is on the board of directors at Setting Captives Free. Today, I want to share with you a writing he gave to our SCF mentors for the weekly check-in. Here it is:
Dear brothers and sisters,
Have you ever had a hard time forgiving others? This passage will remind you, not only how to forgive others who have sinned against you, but that you were made ALIVE and your own mountain of debt was completely forgiven! It was ALL nailed to the CROSS! There is no RECORD OF DEBT! No LEGAL DEMANDS standing against you! It was SET ASIDE in Christ through His shed blood on the cross!
Colossians 2:13-14 And you, who were dead in your trespasses and the uncircumcision of your flesh, God made alive together with him, having forgiven us ALL our trespasses, 14 by canceling the record of debt that stood against us with its legal demands. This he set aside, nailing it to the cross.
Bad News:

    We were dead
    Trapped in trespasses
    And had uncircumcised flesh

Good News:

    God made us alive
    Together with Him
    He circumcised our hearts
    Forgave ALL our trespasses
    Cancelled our record of debt
    Which stood against us
    With its legal demands
    He set it aside
    And nailed it ALL to the cross

Jesus became that “legal document” (so to speak) as He became sin for us, absorbing the wrath and waves of judgment, and the sentence of death against sin in His flesh! The “record of debt” that “stood against us” with its “legal demands” (requiring our death) was now hanging on a cross as if He were the one who deserved death! 
Everything that stood against us was “set aside” so Jesus could take the place of sinful man! Jesus reversed the curse and restored what was lost in the garden! Through Jesus’ death, we were taken out of Adam and placed into Christ, and through His resurrection, we were given His life! 
Let’s celebrate THIS GOOD NEWS, our FREEDOM, our new life, and our new identity in Christ!
How does understanding the complete forgiveness we have in Christ, as described in Colossians 2:13-14, impact your ability to forgive others and live in the freedom of your new identity in Him?

Biblical Illustration: Barabbas
I loved Erick's writing, didn't you? 
I was thinking of one striking illustration of this truth, which is found in the story of Barabbas (Matthew 27:15-26). Barabbas was a known rebel, a murderer, and a prisoner rightly condemned to die. His record of crimes was public, and the legal demands against him were clear: death by crucifixion. 
Yet on the day of Jesus’ trial, Barabbas stood in the shadows while the innocent Son of God stood before Pilate. The crowd was given a choice: Jesus or Barabbas. In a shocking twist, the sinless Savior was condemned, and the guilty man was released.
Barabbas walked free, not because he was righteous, but because Jesus took his place. Jesus bore the penalty Barabbas deserved, died on the cross Barabbas deserved, and Barabbas was treated like Jesus should have been. 
In the same way, our mountain of sin and guilt was not ignored; it was nailed to the cross! Christ became the legal document of our debt, paying the full debt and absorbing the full punishment we owed. The curse that was against us fell upon Him. He died on my cross, and your cross; took our nails and spear, was buried in our grave. We now walk free, like Barabbas.
Barabbas’ freedom is a vivid picture of ours. We were guilty, but Jesus was crucified. We were condemned, but Jesus set us free. Through His death and resurrection, He canceled our record of debt and gave us new life. Barabbas’ story is ours; the story of undeserved mercy through the cross.
How does the story of Barabbas illustrate the truth of Christ's substitutionary death for us, and what does it teach us about the freedom we have through His sacrifice?

Practical Application
Because Jesus has fully forgiven us by nailing our debt to the cross, we are invited to live in the freedom He has purchased for us. This freedom is not just a one-time event but a daily walk of grace, where we continually come to Him, set our hearts on Him, and share the message of His mercy. As we walk in this freedom, we can silence the voice of shame, extend grace to others, and tell our story of redemption. Let today be the day you fully embrace the life Jesus has given you. Here are some action points for today:

    Come to Him often, remembering you are fully forgiven (Matthew 11:28).
    Set your heart on Christ, not on past failures (Colossians 3:1-2).
    Silence the voice of shame with the voice of the gospel (Romans 8:1).
    Extend grace to others, just as grace has been lavished on you (Colossians 3:13).
    Tell your story—how Jesus took your place and gave you life (1 Peter 2:9).

You are free. Walk hand in hand with your Savior today.
What specific actions can you take to silence the voice of shame, extend grace to others, and share your story of redemption with someone in your life?

Day 128: What To Do With Guilt
Guilt Drives Us to Sin, Jesus Frees Us From Both
Key Passage
Whoever keeps the whole law but stumbles at just one point is guilty of breaking all of it.
James 2:10
Teaching
Guilt is the heavy weight on the soul that whispers, "You are condemned." It then proceeds to give us all the evidence as to why we are condemned. Guilt is the inward ache that gnaws at our conscience, a deep knowing that we have wronged God. Like an undertow beneath the surface of a peaceful beach, guilt tugs us back into the dangerous waters of sin if it is not dealt with. 
But through the cross of Christ, we are rescued and brought into the safe harbor of forgiveness.
Today, let’s consider three truths about guilt and the gospel.
1. Guilt Condemns (Romans 3:19-20; James 2:10)
The law of God holds up a mirror to our hearts and shows us our sin. It silences every defense and makes the whole world accountable before God (Romans 3:19-20). Even if we keep the law in many points but stumble in one, we are guilty of all (James 2:10).

Guilt is like a courtroom verdict pronounced over the soul: "Guilty as charged!" It bears down on our conscience, making us restless and driven to hide, deny, or indulge further in sin to numb the pain, even as Adam and Eve hid from God after they had sinned (Genesis 3:8-10).
2. Guilt Corrupts (2 Corinthians 7:10; Matthew 27:3-5)
Unchecked guilt doesn’t stay idle; it festers and multiplies. Worldly sorrow, which is mere regret without repentance, leads to death (2 Corinthians 7:10). Judas felt guilt, but instead of fleeing to Christ, he fled deeper into darkness (Matthew 27:3-5).
3. Grace Cleanses (1 John 1:9; Hebrews 9:14; Romans 8:1)
At the cross, Jesus bore our guilt and shame, absorbing the full penalty we deserved (Isaiah 53:5-6). When we confess our sins, He is faithful and just to forgive and cleanse us from all unrighteousness (1 John 1:9). His blood purifies our conscience from dead works so we can serve the living God with joy (Hebrews 9:14).

In Christ, there is now no condemnation (Romans 8:1). His forgiveness brings us safely to shore, into the harbor of God’s love, where the undertow of guilt is broken forever (Psalm 103:12; Micah 7:19).
Conclusion:
Guilt can be a cruel slave-driver, chaining us to the past and dragging us into deeper sin. But Jesus is the Rescuer who shatters the chains, lifts us from the swirling depths, and places us on the Solid Rock of Jesus Christ (Psalm 40:2), and firmly in the strong, welcoming arms of the Father.
Run to Him today. Rest in the harbor of His grace.
How does understanding the truth that "guilt condemns, guilt corrupts, and grace cleanses" change the way we approach sin and forgiveness in our daily lives?

Biblical Illustration: Judas
The Tragedy of Judas and Worldly Sorrow
Judas Iscariot stands as a somber warning about the destructive power of unresolved guilt. After betraying Jesus for thirty pieces of silver, Judas was seized with regret (Matthew 27:3-5). But instead of turning to God for mercy, he turned to the religious leaders; men as helpless to save him as a boat without a rudder in a raging sea (Matthew 27:4). When Judas tried to return the blood money, the priests coldly dismissed him. His attempt to make amends was rejected by both men and God, for guilt cannot be cleansed by human effort.
Crushed under the unbearable weight of guilt and worldly sorrow, Judas chose suicide over repentance. He drowned beneath the undertow of guilt rather than reaching for the saving hand of Christ. Judas' story shows that guilt not brought to Jesus festers into despair. Like an undertow dragging a swimmer far from shore, guilt pulled Judas into deeper darkness because he refused the only One who could rescue him.
In contrast, godly sorrow leads to life and forgiveness (2 Corinthians 7:10). Jesus was ready to forgive even Judas, had he only turned; but instead, Judas turned away. Let us learn from him and run to Christ, not from Him.
What does Judas' story teach us about the dangers of unresolved guilt and the importance of turning to Christ for forgiveness?

Day 128: What To Do With Guilt
Practical Application
There are two kinds of guilt: false guilt and true guilt. 
False guilt is like the young child who feels his parents' divorce is his fault: "If only I had been more obedient, if only I had not pitted mom against dad, if only I had..." This false guilt can be terrible on a person, and can take a long time to resolve. 
True guilt is actual guilt before God, which is always punishable by death. 
What do we do with both kinds of guilt?
To help you discern whether you have false guilt or true guilt, ask a trusted friend, someone who has godly wisdom and discernment and who will tell you the truth, whether the issue you are struggling with is your fault and therefore you are guilty, or whether it is not your fault and therefore you should accept it as false guilt. Sometimes you simply have to bring someone else into the situation, a trusted believer with biblical understanding, to help you see your way through. 
If you have true guilt, here are five things you should do:

    Confess your sin openly to God, not hiding or justifying it (Psalm 51:4).
    Believe that the blood of Jesus cleanses you completely, leaving no stain behind (Hebrews 9:14).
    Repent by turning from your sin and walking in the freedom Christ purchased for you (Acts 3:19).
    Receive His forgiveness like a weary traveler drinking deeply at a spring of fresh water (Ephesians 2:8-9).
    Rest in His finished work, dropping your guilt like a heavy anchor at the foot of the cross, never to drag it with you again (Romans 8:1).

Guilt is too heavy for you to carry. Jesus carried it for you. Lay it down and live free.
How can you distinguish between false guilt and true guilt, and what steps can you take to deal with each in a biblical and healthy way?

Day 129: Knowing the Lord
The Relationship That Solves All Problems
Key Passage
More than that, I count all things as loss compared to the surpassing excellence of knowing Christ Jesus my Lord, for whom I have lost all things.
Philippians 3:8
Teaching
When we come to truly know the Lord through the gospel, we find the solution to all our problems—not by escaping them, but by walking with the One who leads us through them.
Today, we are looking at Philippians 3:7-10.
1. Priceless Pursuit (Philippians 3:7-8)
Paul once prized religious achievements like jewels in a crown, but after encountering Christ, he counted them as rubbish; garbage tossed into a dung heap. Knowing Jesus became his priceless treasure, a pearl of great price (Matthew 13:45-46). When we truly know the Lord, worldly props like porn and food shows and gambling ads, etc. lose their glitter. No longer do we need to self-medicate with pleasures, distractions, or addictions. Like a traveler who tosses aside heavy baggage to run freely (Hebrews 12:1), we cast off lesser loves for the supreme joy of Christ.
2. Perfect Position (Philippians 3:9)
In knowing Christ, Paul gained not just a friend but a perfect righteousness. His standing with God no longer depended on law-keeping but on the spotless record of Jesus imputed to him (2 Corinthians 5:21). Picture a pauper clothed in the King’s royal robes; the rags of sin stripped away, the silk of Christ’s obedience wrapped around him. This is the believer’s perfect position: justified, accepted, and embraced. And because we are secure in Him, we don't need to numb our hearts with counterfeit comforts. Jesus Christ has become our refuge and righteousness (Proverbs 18:10).
3. Powerful Partnership (Philippians 3:10)
Paul longed to know Christ in the power of His resurrection and the fellowship of His sufferings. This is no sterile acquaintance; it’s a living partnership, like a vine nourishing its branches (John 15:5). We walk with Christ daily, talk with Him intimately, and suffer alongside Him triumphantly. He does not remove every hardship but walks us through them, steadying us like a Shepherd carrying a lamb across rocky ground (Psalm 23:4). In this union, we find strength that no pill or pleasure could ever provide.
Promise of Intimacy (Jeremiah 31:34)
Jeremiah foretold a day when God's people would "all know Me, from the least to the greatest." Through Christ’s blood removing our sin, that promise is fulfilled (Hebrews 8:11). Knowing the Lord is not reserved for the spiritual elite; it’s offered freely to every weary heart. It’s like a river flowing into every dry valley, bringing life where death once reigned.
When we know Him, we no longer need to scramble for substitutes. We have the solution to every longing: a living relationship with the Risen Christ, who walks with us, talks with us, and leads us home.
How does knowing Christ transform our pursuit of worldly pleasures, our position before God, and our daily walk with Him?

Biblical Illustration: Anna
Anna the Prophetess
Anna the prophetess shows what it means to truly know the Lord and find Him as the solution to all of life’s sorrows. After only seven years of marriage, Anna became a widow (Luke 2:36-37). In a culture where widowhood often meant vulnerability and loneliness, Anna could have turned to bitterness, isolation, or even self-indulgence to ease her grief. But she didn't. Instead, she devoted herself to worship, prayer, and fasting in the temple, day and night, drawing near to God as her Comforter.
Anna’s life was like a tree rooted beside a river, thriving even in drought (Jeremiah 17:7-8). When the infant Jesus was brought to the temple, Anna immediately recognized Him. Decades of walking and talking with God had tuned her heart to the voice of the Spirit. Overwhelmed with joy, she praised God and shared the good news with all who were waiting for redemption (Luke 2:38).
Through Christ, Anna’s faith was fulfilled. Her longings were not medicated with temporary relief but satisfied with the arrival of the Savior Himself. In knowing Jesus, Anna found life, joy, and purpose; and so do we when we forsake lesser things and cling to Him alone.
What can we learn from Anna’s example about how to seek God and find comfort in Him during times of sorrow and loss?

Practical Application
To truly know the Lord, we must cultivate a deep, personal relationship with Him. This requires intentional time spent in His presence—through prayer, reading His Word, and turning away from distractions. As we draw near to God, we are reminded of the hope and joy found only in Jesus, our perfect righteousness. Like Anna, we are called to seek Him daily, find our rest in His grace, and share the good news of His redemption with others.

    Sit daily at Jesus’ feet by reading His Word and listening to His voice (Luke 10:39; John 10:27).
    Draw near to God in prayer, pouring out your heart like Anna did (Luke 2:37; Philippians 4:6-7).
    Turn from worldly distractions that pull you away from intimacy with Christ (James 4:8).
    Cling to the gospel, remembering that Christ’s righteousness is your covering (Philippians 3:9; 2 Corinthians 5:21).
    Proclaim the good news to others as Anna did, pointing them to the hope found only in Jesus (Luke 2:38; Romans 1:16).

When we truly know the Lord, we need no other remedy; He becomes our joy, our righteousness, and our constant companion.
How can you intentionally set aside time each day to draw near to God through prayer, reading His Word, and turning away from distractions?

Day 130: God Finishes His Work
God is Faithful To Finish What He Begins
Key Passage
...being confident of this, that He who began a good work in you will carry it on to completion until the day of Christ Jesus.
Philippians 1:6
Teaching
God Finishes What He Begins
 Philippians 1:6
Have you ever felt like a broken tool tossed into the back of a dusty shed, rusty, forgotten, and useless? Maybe you believe you've been placed on a shelf by God, left to gather dust because of your past sins. Perhaps you imagine God shaking His head, arms folded, tired of your failures, and ready to move on without you. Does it seem like the clay of your life is so marred that the Potter has abandoned it altogether (Jeremiah 18:4)? I've thought this way numerous times.
The problem is that this discouragement, this hopelessness and dread, can bring strong temptations; thinking we'll just go back to sin and at least have some excitement in life.
Friend, if you've struggled at all with these thoughts,  hear this: God finishes what He begins.
Paul’s words in Philippians 1:6 are a blazing torch of hope: "He who began a good work in you will bring it to completion at the day of Jesus Christ." God is not like a fickle craftsman who discards a project when it gets messy. He is the faithful Finisher who continues shaping, sanding, and polishing until the masterpiece reflects His glory (Ephesians 2:10).
Even when our hearts condemn us, God is greater than our hearts (1 John 3:20). Though our sins are like deep stains on a white garment, through Christ, they are washed whiter than snow (Isaiah 1:18). If God started the work of salvation in you, He promises to breathe life into the smoldering wick (Matthew 12:20), not extinguish it.
The gospel reminds us that Jesus Christ took every one of our failures to the cross. He was not merely bruised by our sins; He was crushed for them (Isaiah 53:5). And having risen from the grave, He now guarantees that the life He began in us will not end in ashes but in resurrection glory (Romans 8:30).
When you feel sidelined, remember: you are not an abandoned project. You are a living stone, still being built into a spiritual house for His glory (1 Peter 2:5). You are like a painting still wet on the canvas; though the brushstrokes seem chaotic now, the Artist sees the beauty He is bringing forth.
Lift your eyes from your faltering steps to Christ’s unfailing grip (John 10:28). The God who saved you is the God who sanctifies you, sustains you, and will glorify you. In Christ, your story is not one of abandonment, but of faithful completion, finished by the One who declared on the cross, “It is finished” (John 19:30).
Trust Him. He's not through with you yet.
How does the promise in Philippians 1:6 encourage us when we feel inadequate or abandoned due to our failures?

Biblical Illustration: John Mark
John Mark knew the sting of failure. Once entrusted to accompany Paul and Barnabas on a missionary journey, he abandoned them when the road became too hard (Acts 13:13). To Paul, it was such a serious breach that later he refused to take Mark along again (Acts 15:37-38). Imagine how Mark must have felt, like a cracked vessel left on the shelf, labeled “Unreliable.”
But God was not finished with John Mark.
In time, under the steady encouragement of Barnabas and the patient work of the Spirit, Mark was restored. So complete was God’s work in him that Paul, near the end of his life, requested, “Get Mark and bring him with you, for he is very useful to me for ministry” (2 Timothy 4:11).
Mark’s story is a living portrait of Philippians 1:6. Though his wick nearly went out, Christ rekindled it. Though he stumbled badly, Christ upheld him (Psalm 37:24). And through it all, the gospel of Jesus Christ was the solution: Mark’s worth was never in his flawed performance but in Christ’s finished work on the cross. God completed in Mark what He began; He will do the same for all who are His.
What does John Mark's story teach us about God's ability to restore and complete the work He begins in us, despite our failures?

Practical Application
In times of failure and discouragement, it's easy to feel as though God has abandoned His work in us. But the truth is, God promises to finish what He begins. Here’s how to encourage yourself in the Lord and trust in His ability to restore and complete His work in you:

    Confess your failures to God, knowing He is faithful to forgive and cleanse (1 John 1:9).
    Preach the gospel to yourself daily, remembering that your worth is rooted in Christ’s finished work, not your performance (Galatians 2:20).
    Rely on the Spirit’s power, not your own strength, to walk in newness of life (Romans 8:11).
    Stay connected to godly encouragers, like Barnabas was for Mark, who will point you back to Jesus (Hebrews 10:24-25).
    Persevere by faith, trusting that He who began a good work in you will bring it to completion (Philippians 1:6).


God is not finished with you. In Christ, your failures are not your finale; they are the canvas upon which His grace paints a masterpiece of redemption.
What is one area of your life where you feel like a failure, and how can you apply one of these truths today to speak hope and remind yourself that God is still at work?


Day 131: Our Two Worst Enemies
Our Enemies Revealed and Displayed
Key Passage
But each person is tempted when they are dragged away by their own evil desire and enticed. Then, after desire has conceived, it gives birth to sin; and sin, when it is full-grown, gives birth to death.
James 1:14-15
Teaching
The Two Enemies—The Devil and the Flesh
We have many spiritual enemies in this life (Ephesians 6:12), but two of them conspire together in terrifying harmony: the devil and our own flesh. Like a master chef and a hungry glutton, Satan knows how to craft the precise dish our flesh craves, then serves it piping hot at just the right moment.
The devil holds something up; something alluring, sparkling like bait on a hook; and our flesh, without hesitation, lunges at it. There is no discernment in the flesh. It does not weigh consequences. It does not seek God. Like a famished wolf, it pants after whatever smells of satisfaction (Romans 8:7-8). The flesh doesn't ask whether the offering is wise or holy. It only asks, "Do I want it?"
And Satan? He is the tempter (Matthew 4:3), the accuser (Revelation 12:10), and the deceiver (John 8:44). He studies our patterns, watches our weaknesses, and crafts temptations so personal, so tailor-made, that we often think the idea was ours to begin with. He lays his traps like a seasoned fisherman, using the same lure over and over, the one that shimmers just right for our particular appetites.
This partnership between devil and flesh is like fire and gasoline. The enemy knows our sinful nature like a puppeteer knows his marionette. One tug and we dance to his tune. He doesn’t need brute force, just suggestion, just timing. Like a whisper in the shadows or a breeze carrying the scent of sin, he knows when and where we are most vulnerable (James 1:14-15).
Wow. One would think, with enemies so cunning, so spiritually intelligent, so armed with millennia of human experience, plus our own willing flesh working against us, that we would be utterly hopeless. What chance do we have against an enemy who knows us better than we know ourselves, and a flesh that betrays us with a kiss?
But here’s the good news: tomorrow, we’ll discover the two divine weapons God gives us; two unstoppable forces that empower us to crush Satan under our feet (Romans 16:20) and put to death the misdeeds of the flesh (Romans 8:13). Through these, we not only stand firm. We win.
How does understanding the partnership between the devil and your own flesh help you recognize and resist temptation more effectively?

Biblical Illustration: Annanias and Saphira
Ananias and Sapphira were members of the early church; witnesses of miracles, participants in fellowship, and even givers of generous offerings. But then the devil whispered, and their flesh leaned in. Like a serpent curling around their hearts, Satan offered them prestige without sacrifice, honor without honesty (Acts 5:3). They sold a piece of property, kept back part of the money, and brought the rest, pretending it was the full amount. No one demanded they give it all, but pride did. The flesh longed for the applause of men (Matthew 6:1), and Satan gladly supplied the script.
Peter’s words were piercing: “Why has Satan filled your heart to lie to the Holy Spirit?” Their sin wasn’t just financial deception; it was spiritual treason, prompted by Satan, welcomed by their flesh. They fell dead, not simply because they lied, but because they joined the devil in his rebellion and let their sinful desires take the reins.
Yet the gospel holds a different outcome. Jesus died not only for sins like theirs, but to break the power of the devil and crucify the flesh (Hebrews 2:14; Galatians 5:24). What Ananias and Sapphira needed, and what we need, isn’t better behavior, but a perfect Savior.
What does James 1:14–15 reveal about the progression of temptation, and how can recognizing this process help you break its power before it leads to sin?

Practical Application
Temptation is not just a battle of willpower; it's a spiritual war involving two powerful enemies: the devil and our flesh. To stand firm, we must fight strategically, using the weapons God provides. Victory begins when we bring our struggles into the light and anchor our hearts in truth.

    Confess your weakness honestly before God (1 John 1:9); the flesh loses power when brought into the light.
    Recognize the enemy’s strategy (2 Corinthians 2:11); name the bait he uses and expose the lie beneath it.
    Fill your mind with God’s Word daily (Psalm 119:11); Scripture sharpens your discernment and strengthens your resistance.
    Walk by the Spirit (Galatians 5:16); only His power can silence the cravings of the flesh.
    Fix your eyes on Christ crucified (Hebrews 12:2); His cross not only forgives sin—it breaks its grip and promises final victory.

What specific temptation do you face regularly, and how can you prepare in advance using God’s Word and the Spirit’s power to resist it?

Day 132: Our Two Powerful Weapons of Warfare
God Has Given Us All We Need to Win
Key Passage
The one who practices sin is of the devil, because the devil has been sinning from the very start. This is why the Son of God was revealed, to destroy the works of the devil.
1 John 3:8
Teaching
The Two Weapons That Win
Yesterday, we saw our two fiercest enemies: the devil and our own flesh. The devil tempts like a skilled puppeteer, pulling strings with surgical precision. The flesh, like a ravenous beast, lunges for whatever the enemy dangles before it. Together, they form a devastating alliance. But praise God, they are no match for the weapons God has given His people!
God does not leave us defenseless. He has armed us with two divine weapons: the gospel of Jesus Christ and the power of the Holy Spirit. These aren’t soft suggestions, they are spiritual sledgehammers, able to demolish strongholds (2 Corinthians 10:4).
First, the gospel. It is outside of us, not born from our willpower or moral resolve. It is news; good news about what Christ has done for us. The gospel forgives our sin (Colossians 1:14), crucifies our guilt (Romans 8:1), and buries our shame (Hebrews 12:2). The cross of Christ doesn’t merely polish us up; it performs a transfer! We are taken out of Adam and placed into Christ (1 Corinthians 15:22; Romans 5:19), rescued out of darkness and put into light (Colossians 1:13). We die with Him, are buried with Him, and raised to walk in newness of life (Romans 6:3-5).
Second, the power of the Holy Spirit. He is inside of us. The Spirit is not a concept or a cloud; He is a Person, indwelling every believer with resurrection power (Romans 8:11). His strength is greater than the unholy spirit that opposes us (1 John 4:4). Satan is like a shadow, but the Spirit is light. And light always wins in a fight with darkness. A single candle can scatter a room full of darkness. So too, the Spirit expels the lies of Satan and breaks the grip of the flesh (Galatians 5:16-17).
The gospel gives us position: righteous in Christ. The Spirit gives us power: victory in Christ. The gospel justifies us; the Spirit sanctifies us. One declares us holy; the other makes us holy. And both are gifts; gracious, unstoppable, and fully ours in Christ.
So today, you don’t fight for victory; you fight from it. You are armed with the blood of the Lamb and filled with the breath of heaven. Go forward with joy, because in Jesus and by His Spirit, you absolutely win.
How do the gospel of Jesus Christ and the power of the Holy Spirit equip believers to overcome sin and the devil’s influence?

Biblical Illustration: The Believers in the Early Church
In Acts 4, the early church faced threats from powerful enemies, religious authorities who tried to silence the name of Jesus. Externally, Satan worked through persecution; internally, their flesh could have yielded to fear. But instead of retreating, the believers lifted their voices to God. They didn’t rely on clever plans or self-confidence. They prayed, “Lord, consider their threats and enable your servants to speak your word with great boldness” (Acts 4:29).
And what happened? The place where they met shook; not from fear, but from power. They were filled with the Holy Spirit and spoke the word boldly (Acts 4:31). The gospel emboldened them with truth, and the Spirit empowered them with courage. Neither the schemes of Satan nor the weakness of the flesh could stop them. They were armed with the same two weapons we are today, and they won.
How did prayer and reliance on the Holy Spirit enable the early church to overcome fear and boldly proclaim the gospel in the face of opposition?

Practical Application
God has not only given us powerful weapons; the gospel and the Holy Spirit, but He also invites us to actively engage with them in daily life. These are not passive truths; they are meant to be practiced and lived out with intention and perseverance.
How do we utilize the two weapons God gives us? We do the below, and we practice, practice, practice, for practice makes permanent:

    Preach the gospel to yourself daily (1 Corinthians 15:1-6); remind your heart you are forgiven, accepted, and clothed in Christ.
    Confess your identity in Christ (2 Corinthians 5:17); you are no longer in Adam; you are new, righteous, and free.
    Acknowledge that you are filled with the Holy Spirit (Ephesians 5:18); yield to His control and trust His power over sin.
    Walk in the Spirit, not in the flesh (Galatians 5:16); follow His leading moment by moment.
    Resist the devil by submitting to God (James 4:7); the victory is Christ’s, and through Him, it’s yours too.

How can we actively engage with the gospel and the Holy Spirit each day to strengthen our faith and resist sin?


Day 133: Don't Bow
Stand Firm
Key Passage
Shadrach, Meshach, and Abednego replied to the king, “O Nebuchadnezzar, we have no need to answer you in this matter. If the God whom we serve exists, then He is able to deliver us from the blazing fiery furnace and from your hand, O king. But even if He does not, let it be known to you, O king, that we will not serve your gods or worship the golden statue you have set up.”
Daniel 3:16-18
Teaching
Don’t Bow — Daniel 3:14 to 4:25
 Theme: Stand Firm, See Christ
1. The Pressure to Conform
Nebuchadnezzar’s golden image towered over the plain like a god demanding allegiance (Daniel 3:1-7). The pressure to bow wasn’t subtle, it was systemic. Like heat from a furnace turned up with cultural threats, Shadrach, Meshach, and Abednego faced the seductive lure of self-preservation and the crushing weight of social expectation. But they did not bow. 
In every age, believers are tempted to kneel before idols; acceptance, comfort, security, porn, substances, pride, excessive food. Yet Romans 12:2 commands, “Do not be conformed to this world.” Like reeds that won’t bend, God's people must resist the sway of sin’s breeze. We either learn this the easy way, through study of God's Word and learning from the body of Christ, or we learn it the hard way which brings with it much loss, grief and shame.
2. The Presence in the Fire
Thrown into the fire, the Hebrews were not alone. Nebuchadnezzar’s astonished cry reveals the heart of the story: “I see four men… and the fourth is like a son of the gods” (Daniel 3:25). Most see this as a reminder that Jesus is with us in the furnace of affliction, and He is (Isaiah 43:2; Matthew 28:20). 
But the deeper truth is more fiery: Jesus entered the ultimate furnace on the cross. He did not merely stand beside us in suffering; He substituted Himself under God’s wrath. The cross was the burning oven of divine judgment, and like the Hebrews, we walk out unscathed because another stood in our place (Isaiah 53:5; 2 Corinthians 5:21).
3. The Power of a Testimony
The king who once demanded worship now declares, “There is no other god who is able to rescue in this way” (Daniel 3:29). Their unyielding faith ignited a national testimony. Their stand sparked praise. 
Likewise, when we endure trials by faith, refusing to bow to compromise, we show the watching world the worth of Christ. Like Paul in prison (Philippians 1:12-14), or Stephen facing the stones (Acts 7:55-60), faith in the fire reveals the glory of the gospel. 
Each one of us has this opportunity every day. Picture the world telling us to bow to it's ideology, or Satan telling us to worship him to get the whole world, or our own flesh driving us to gratify its cravings. Every day this opportunity calls to us.  
Conclusion
Don’t bow. Not to fear, not to comfort, not to cultural gods or worldly ideology. Jesus entered the inferno of judgment for you so you could walk with Him through every trial without the smell of smoke; that is, without even a hint of evil. His presence is not only comfort; it is conquest. He came to conquer not only the fire, but your heart, and the fire that was meant to destroy will become the place of deepest intimacy with the Son of God. This is what I love. This is what keeps me fleeing immorality, resisting the devil, and dying to my flesh. This intimacy with the Son of God is the best thing I've ever know. How about you?
Stand firm. Step in. He is already there.
How does the story of Shadrach, Meshach, and Abednego challenge us to stand firm in our faith amid cultural pressure?


Biblical Illustration: Mordecai
The Man Who Wouldn’t Bow
There is another illustration of this refusal to bow.
In the book of Esther, Mordecai stands as a striking portrait of unwavering courage in the face of tyranny. When Haman rose to power and commanded all to bow before him, Mordecai refused (Esther 3:2). He would not lower his convictions, even as the gallows were being built for his destruction (Esther 5:14). His solitary stance seemed foolish, suicidal even, but it mirrored the courage of the Hebrew boys before the furnace. Mordecai’s resistance wasn’t just civil disobedience; it was a declaration of allegiance to a higher King.
Though he faced certain death, deliverance came from an unexpected place, through Esther’s bold intervention and God's providential hand (Esther 4:14; 7:9-10). 
But even this rescue points forward to a greater redemption. Like Mordecai, Jesus did not bow to the kingdoms of this world (Matthew 4:8-10). He stood alone, condemned by earthly rulers, yet by His death and resurrection, He reversed the verdict. The gallows meant for us became the cross where Christ took our place. Mordecai’s deliverance was temporal, but Christ’s is eternal.
When you refuse to bow to evil, know that Christ has already stood for you in the fire of wrath. His victory ensures yours. His courage becomes your strength.
How does Mordecai’s refusal to bow point us to the greater courage and victory of Christ?

Practical Application
When trials intensify and the pressure to conform grows stronger, believers are called to stand firm in faith. But how do we stay faithful when the heat is on? Scripture gives us practical, powerful ways to remain unshakable in the face of opposition.

    Refuse to Compromise: Like Mordecai, resolve not to bow to sin, fear, or cultural pressure (Romans 12:2).
    Fix Your Eyes on Christ: Remember that Jesus entered the fire of wrath for you (Hebrews 12:2; Isaiah 53:5).
    Draw Strength from the Gospel: Let His substitution embolden your stand—He took your place, so you can stand in His (2 Corinthians 5:21).
    Speak Boldly and Live Faithfully: Your testimony in the fire can turn hearts to God (Philippians 1:14).
    Pray for Courage: Ask the Spirit daily to strengthen your resolve (Ephesians 6:18-20).

How can we apply the example of Mordecai and the strength of the gospel to stand firm in faith when facing trials and cultural pressure?

Day 134: Jesus Came For Sinners
My Sins, My Sins, My Savior
Key Passage
“It is not the healthy who need a doctor, but the sick. I have not come to call the righteous, but sinners." - Jesus Christ
Mark 2:17
Teaching
"My sins, my sins, my Savior" is a hymn text written by John Samuel Bewley Monsell in 1863. The words express conviction of sin and the comfort found in a Savior. It is the cry of every believer who can't believe the depth and length of their sins, believing that if anyone deserves hell, they do. Yes, we lament our sins, we weep over our sins, we would drown in tears of guilt over our sins, were it not for our Savior!
If you're disturbed in the least by your sins, look with me at what Jesus said about sinners.
“Jesus Came for Sinners”
 Mark 2:15-17
Theme: Jesus didn’t come for the righteous, but for the ruined. If your sin feels like your shadow, always close and unavoidable, this text is your invitation.
1. The Table of the Tarnished (Mark 2:15)
“As He reclined at table in his house, many tax collectors and sinners were reclining with Jesus and His disciples...”
Jesus didn’t just tolerate sinners; He shared a table with them. In a world where reputations mattered more than reality, Jesus chose companionship with the condemned. Like a doctor who sits beside the sick rather than shouting advice from a distance, Christ drew near to sinners. 
The table of grace is never gated, never closed or barred to certain people; it’s laid with mercy for those who feel unworthy. Are you burdened with guilt? Come hungry. The Bread of Life (John 6:35) is broken for the broken. Has impurity wrapped its tentacles around you like it did me? Come to the One Who washes us clean and then says, "You are already clean, because of the Word I have spoken to you" (John 15:3). Has food mastered you, or substances brought temporary relief along with eternal guilt? Jesus takes your sin to the cross and your guilt to the grave!
2. The Grumbling of the Godless (Mark 2:16)
“When the scribes... saw that He was eating with sinners... they said, ‘Why does He eat with tax collectors and sinners?’”
Legalists loathe grace. They want holiness to be earned, not given. But grace flips the system; it makes the last first (Matthew 20:16). The scribes couldn’t see that Jesus was doing heart surgery while they judged the symptoms. 
Like a lighthouse on a rocky shore, Jesus shines not to spotlight how lost we are, but to lead us home. The gospel isn't for the clean; it's for the corrupt who cry out, “God, be merciful to me, a sinner!” (Luke 18:13). Have you ever cried out to Jesus, from the depths of your soul, from the guilt and stench of your sin knowing He is Who and what you need?
3. The Mission of the Merciful (Mark 2:17)
“Those who are well have no need of a physician, but those who are sick. I came not to call the righteous, but sinners.”
Here is the heart of Christ: not to condemn, but to cure (John 3:17). The wonderful thing about Jesus is that He doesn't wait for sinners to improve; He came to rescue us in our ruin (Romans 5:8). 
Your sin may feel like chains, but Christ came with keys. His cross bore your curse. His blood silences every accusation against you (Colossians 2:13-14). He rose, not just to forgive you, but to free you. And even now, He’s preparing a place for you (John 14:2), because His grace doesn’t stop at the empty grave, it goes to a full heaven to make space for you. Oh what love!
Are you sick with sin? Come. He is your physician.
Are you weary of guilt? Rest. He is your righteousness.
Are you broken inside? Rejoice. He came for you.
The gospel doesn’t require you to get better first; It is the cure.
