But when we forgive, we release ourselves from that prison and walk in the light of God’s peace (John 8:36). Just as Jesus offers us grace and freedom, He calls us to extend that same grace to others (Matthew 6:14-15). The gospel is all about liberation; freedom from sin, freedom from guilt, and freedom from the chains of unforgiveness.
Conclusion:
You are forgiven, not by the limit of 490 times, but by a grace that has no end. God's forgiveness is perfect and infinite. Now, as one forgiven, forgive others. Allow grace to flow through you, and experience the freedom that only comes when we let go of the bitterness that binds us. Your forgiveness is not just a nice idea; it’s the very heart of the gospel. Who can you forgive today? Remember, God’s grace is unlimited. Let it change you, and let it change the world around you.
What does Jesus’ response to Peter in Matthew 18:21-22 teach us about the limitless nature of God’s grace, and how should it shape our own approach to forgiveness?

Biblical Illustration: Joseph
Joseph and His Brothers
Joseph had every reason to harbor bitterness. Betrayed by his own brothers, sold into slavery (Genesis 37:28), falsely accused in Egypt, and left forgotten in prison (Genesis 40:23), his life was a trail of injustice. Yet when Joseph rose to power and his brothers stood before him in need, he did the unthinkable; he forgave (Genesis 50:20-21). He could have repaid evil for evil. Instead, he chose mercy over revenge, grace over grudge.
Joseph's forgiveness was not rooted in denial of the pain, but in trust in a greater plan. He said, “You meant evil against me, but God meant it for good.” He broke the cycle of sin and chose to release the debt.
But Joseph’s forgiveness, as powerful as it was, points to a greater forgiveness found in Christ. Jesus, too, was betrayed, falsely accused, and crucified. Yet from the cross, He offered forgiveness to His enemies (Luke 23:34). His resurrection sealed our pardon once and for all (Isaiah 53:5). Joseph forgave his brothers, but Jesus forgives us all, not just once, or seventy times seven, but forever. The gospel gives us both the model and the power to forgive like Joseph, because we’ve been forgiven by Jesus.
How does Joseph’s act of forgiveness in Genesis foreshadow the greater forgiveness found in Jesus, and what does this reveal about how we are called to forgive others?

Practical Application
Living the Gospel of Unlimited Forgiveness
Forgiveness is at the heart of the gospel, and as followers of Christ, we are called to live out that forgiveness daily. It’s not just a one-time act, but a way of life shaped by the cross and empowered by grace. 
Jesus forgave us fully, freely, and forever, and He invites us to extend that same mercy to others. Living the gospel of unlimited forgiveness means remembering what Christ has done for us and allowing that truth to transform how we respond to hurt, offense, and injustice. This kind of forgiveness frees us from bitterness and reflects the heart of God to a watching world. 

    Remember the Cross Daily: Let the gospel remind you that you are fully forgiven (Ephesians 1:7).
    Release the Offense: Choose to forgive, not because they deserve it, but because you didn’t either (Colossians 3:13).
    Pray for the One Who Hurt You: Jesus said to bless your enemies (Luke 6:27-28); this softens your heart.
    Speak Grace, Not Grudges: Let your words reflect the mercy God has shown you (Ephesians 4:29-32).
    Live Free, Not Bitter: Forgiveness sets you free (Galatians 5:1). Walk in the freedom Christ died to give.

What specific actions can you take today to forgive those who have sinned against you so that you can reflect the forgiveness you've received in Christ?

Day 148: Definition of Forgiveness
What Does Forgiveness Mean?
Key Passage
Then Jesus said, “Father, forgive them, for they do not know what they are doing.”
Luke 23:34
Teaching
Daily Devotional: "Here Is What Forgiveness Means"
Often people remain ensnared in sin because they are living in bitterness. Something done to them, a wrong word, a real offense, some sin committed against them has them hurt by it, dwelling on it, and becoming angrier by the day. 
Genuine forgiveness removes all that.
Forgiveness is one of the most beautiful and challenging acts we can offer, as it mirrors God's grace toward us. When we forgive, we become like Christ, showing mercy, love, and a willingness to release the past. But what does forgiveness really mean in practice? Let’s look at three key principles that illustrate what it means to forgive as Christ forgives us.
1. Forgiveness means I won’t bring the sin up to you ever again.
Forgiveness is a promise that we will not use someone’s wrong against them. God’s forgiveness is a perfect example: "I will remember their sins no more" (Jeremiah 31:34). God doesn’t hold our sins over our heads once He has forgiven us. This is the kind of forgiveness we are called to extend, especially in marriage. Too often, in the heat of an argument, spouses bring up past wrongs as weapons—"Remember when you did this?" This is not forgiveness; it’s like trying to heal a wound while reopening it over and over again. Real forgiveness means we bury those wrongs in the grave of mercy, never to dig them up again.
2. Forgiveness means I won’t talk about your sin behind your back.
True forgiveness involves guarding the dignity of the person we forgive. In Proverbs, we are told that "love covers over a multitude of sins" (Proverbs 10:12; 1 Peter 4:8). When we forgive, we protect the person from further harm by not discussing their sin with others. Imagine the sting of hearing someone talk about your faults behind your back. Forgiveness closes the door to gossip, choosing instead to speak well of others and protect their honor. This is a reflection of Christ, who, though He knew all our sins, never publicized them. Instead, He covered our sins with His own blood.
3. Forgiveness means I won’t dwell on your sin.
Forgiveness is not only a matter of what we say but also what we think. We often replay the offense in our minds like a broken record, letting it take root in our hearts. But "forgive, as the Lord forgave you" (Colossians 3:13). Jesus, hanging on the cross, prayed, “Father, forgive them, for they do not know what they are doing” (Luke 23:34). Even in His pain, He chose not to dwell on the wrongs of those who crucified Him. He let go of the bitterness that could have consumed Him. When we forgive, we do the same, choosing not to dwell on the offense, but to release it, knowing that our Savior has forgiven us much more than we could ever forgive anyone else.
Unforgiveness is a cancer.
Unforgiveness is like poison in our hearts. It eats away at us, slowly consuming our peace, joy, and relationships. It leads to bitterness, and like a wound left untreated, it spreads, infecting all areas of our lives. If we let unforgiveness fester, it drags us back into sin, indulging our flesh with anger and resentment. "Get rid of all bitterness, rage and anger" (Ephesians 4:31-32). Forgiveness is the antidote, the cure. It breaks the chains that bind us, heals our wounds, and restores our joy in Christ.
By applying these principles; choosing not to bring up past sins, not to talk about them behind someone’s back, and not to dwell on them—we walk in the freedom and peace that Christ has given us. Just as Jesus forgave us at the cross, we are called to forgive. It is only through His forgiveness that we can forgive others.
According to Jesus’ example and the three principles outlined, what does true Christlike forgiveness look like in our daily relationships, and how does it reflect the heart of the gospel?

Biblical Illustration: Noah's Sons
Love Covers Over Sin
After the floodwaters receded and the ark came to rest, Noah planted a vineyard. One day, he drank of its wine, became drunk, and lay uncovered in his tent; vulnerable, exposed, and disgraced (Genesis 9:20-23). 
It’s a scene that startles us: the man who walked with God, who obeyed when the world mocked, now fallen into weakness. It can happen to any of us. Noah's son Ham saw his father’s nakedness and, rather than covering it, ran to tell his brothers, broadcasting Noah’s shame.
But Shem and Japheth responded differently. With quiet reverence and deep love, they took a garment, walked backward into the tent, and covered their father. They refused to look upon his shame. Their love didn’t deny the sin, it covered it. This is the heartbeat of "love covers over a multitude of sins" (1 Peter 4:8).
This picture is a shadow of the gospel.
Like Noah, we’ve all been exposed; laid bare by our sin, uncovered before a holy God. But Jesus didn’t shame us. He didn’t turn away in disgust or announce our failure to the world. Instead, He took off His own righteousness (the perfect robe of His sinless life) and placed it over our guilt (Isaiah 61:10). At the cross, He walked backward, so to speak, into the tent of our shame and covered us, not with fabric, but with His blood. Thank you, Jesus.
Forgiveness means we do the same. We don’t run and tell others. We don’t keep peeking back at the failure. We honor the fallen by covering their sin with grace, just as Christ did for us.
Unforgiveness is Ham; exposing, broadcasting, and delighting in the weakness of another. Forgiveness is Shem and Japheth; quietly loving, honoring, and protecting. Jesus is the better brother who covers not just one sin, but all, with His eternal garment of righteousness. And He calls us to do likewise.
Please note: This teaching does not advocate for hiding sin that is against the Law; such sins must be reported to legal authorities. It also does not encourage the hiding of offenses in a workplace, church setting, or other place when a real, grievous offense has occurred. 
How does the story of Noah and his sons illustrate the biblical principle of love covering over sin, and what does this teach us about forgiveness in light of Christ’s sacrifice?

Practical Application
Living Out Gospel-Centered Forgiveness
Gospel-centered forgiveness is more than just a moral duty; it’s a reflection of Christ’s love and redemption. Rather than exposing the faults of others, we are called to cover them with grace, mirroring Jesus’ own sacrifice. This kind of forgiveness requires intentionality: guarding our minds against resentment, choosing words that uplift, and remembering that we, too, have been forgiven completely. Just as Shem and Japheth honored their father by covering his shame, we are invited to walk in love, extending the same mercy Christ has shown us.

    Cover, don’t expose. When someone sins against you, resist the urge to broadcast it—pray for them instead (Matthew 5:44).
    Guard your mind. Take every thought captive when bitterness tries to replay the offense (2 Corinthians 10:5).
    Clothe others in grace. Speak words that build up, not tear down (Ephesians 4:29).
    Forgive as you've been forgiven. Remember the cross—Jesus covered your sin fully and finally (Colossians 3:13).
    Walk in love. Like Shem and Japheth, walk backward in humility and let Christ’s love lead you (1 Corinthians 13:5-7).

When someone wrongs you, how can you intentionally choose to "walk in love" by covering their offense with grace instead of exposing it, just as Christ has covered your sin?


Day 149: Behold the Lamb!
Jesus Takes Away the Sin of the World
Key Passage
The next day John saw Jesus coming toward him and said, “Look, the Lamb of God, who takes away the sin of the world!"
John 1:29
Teaching
Behold the Lamb!
Introduction: The Weight of Sin
Sin is the worst thing in the world. It’s not a weakness, it’s a wrecking ball. It tears apart families, crushes joy, and leaves behind a trail of broken promises and bleeding hearts. Sin is like poison in the bloodstream: silent, spreading, and always fatal if untreated (Isaiah 59:2). It breaks our fellowship with God, stains our conscience, and traps us in a prison of our own making—chained to cravings, impulses, and regrets (Romans 6:23). It makes us blame instead of believe, lust instead of love, and lie instead of live. No disease is more deadly. No enemy more deceitful.
So, what can wash away this stain? What can reach into the soul’s inner darkness and bring light?
1. The Pattern: A Lamb for the Sinful People

From the earliest pages of Scripture, God gave His people a vivid, unforgettable image: the lamb. Whether it was the animal (most likely a lamb) that was put to death to cover Adam and Eve (Genesis 3:21), the lamb in Egypt whose blood shielded households from death (Exodus 12:5–13), or the daily offerings in the tabernacle to cover Israel’s sin (Exodus 29:38–42), the pattern was clear; innocence for guilt, blood for pardon. The lamb didn’t speak. It didn’t struggle. It simply died, a substitute for the sinner (Leviticus 4:32–35). This was God’s way of showing the cost of sin and the path back to fellowship (Leviticus 16:15–16). But these sacrifices were only shadows; temporary covers, not cures (Hebrews 10:1–4).
2. The Proclamation: A Lamb in the Spotlight

Then, one day, a rugged prophet by the Jordan River saw a man approaching; and he pointed with fire in his voice and awe in his soul: “Behold the Lamb of God, who takes away the sin of the world!” (John 1:29). This wasn’t just another teacher or prophet. What he said pointed forward to the fulfillment of every blood-soaked altar, every bleating lamb, every longing heart. The Lamb had come; not to cover sin, but to carry it away (Isaiah 53:6–7), atone for it.
In the simple statement of John the Baptist is not merely an introduction, not merely a proclamation, but rather the complete solution to sin. Man would have us make rules and laws to stop our sinning, or give us steps and counseling and accountability: God just has us look. "Look, the Lamb of God who takes away the sin of the world." Look, the cross. Look, the atonement. Look, your freedom. 
3. The Power: A Lamb Who Saves the Soul

Jesus Christ, the sinless Son, became the sacrificial Lamb. On the cross, He bore the full fury of sin’s judgment and broke sin’s curse forever (1 Peter 1:18–19; Revelation 5:6–9). Like a bridge over a chasm, His blood reconnects God and man (1). Like light scattering darkness, His forgiveness cleanses the deepest shame. He didn’t just remove sin—He reconciled the sinner. All who trust in this Lamb are restored, redeemed, and made new (2 Corinthians 5:17–21).
Today, don’t look at your sin—look at your Savior. Behold the Lamb.
Why did John call Jesus 'the Lamb of God,' and what does that tell us about what Jesus came to do?

Biblical Illustration: The Thief on the Cross
The Thief on the Cross
He was a criminal; condemned, crucified, cursed. A life wasted on rebellion and sin, now ending on a cross beside Jesus (Luke 23:39–43). He had no offerings to give, no lambs to sacrifice, no temple access to plead his case. His hands were nailed, his future gone, his past drenched in guilt. Sin had enslaved him, stained him, and was now about to silence him forever.
But in his final breath, he looked, not at himself, but at the Lamb. “Lord, remember me,” he said, not with polished words but with desperate faith. And Jesus, bloodied, dying, yet still reigningm replied with shocking mercy: “Today you will be with me in Paradise.”
No rituals. No law-keeping. Just grace.
In that moment, the Lamb of God didn’t just cover the thief’s sin, He carried it away. The cross became an altar. The dying man became a redeemed soul. Sin had ruined him, but Jesus restored him.
His story is a portrait of hope: that even in our darkest hour, the Lamb’s blood is strong enough to save, close enough to reach, and pure enough to cleanse. The thief died forgiven, not because he was worthy, but because Jesus was, and the thief looked at Him. "Behold, the Lamb of God who takes away the sin of the world." There's the solution to the worst problem you and I have. Have you looked? Do you see Him?
How does the story of the thief on the cross show us that salvation comes through faith, not works?

Practical Application
Living in Light of the Lamb
When we truly understand who Jesus is, the Lamb of God who takes away our sin, it changes how we live. His sacrifice doesn’t just secure our salvation; it shapes our daily response. Living in light of the Lamb means walking in grace, gratitude, and gospel-centered obedience.

    Confess sin daily (1 John 1:9) — not to earn forgiveness, but to enjoy restored fellowship.
    Trust fully in Christ’s finished work (Hebrews 10:12–14) — not in performance or penance.
    Worship with gratitude (Romans 12:1) — offering ourselves as living sacrifices.
    Share the Lamb with others (John 1:36–37) — pointing them to the only One who can save.
    Live holy lives (1 Peter 1:15–19) — because we’ve been ransomed at great cost.

What does it look like in your daily life to live in light of Jesus being the Lamb of God?

Day 150: Behold the Lamb - Part 2
Looking At Christ
Key Passage
For Christ, our Passover lamb, has been sacrificed.
1 Corinthians 5:6
Teaching
Like we saw yesterday in John 1:29, John says the purpose of Jesus Christ, the Lamb of God, was to take away our sins, thereby making us righteous, without sin, spotless and holy (Hebrews 10:14, Colossians 1:22, 2 Corinthians 5:21). Having this identity is a wonderful deterrent to practicing sin (1 John 3:3–6). After all, we live our lives based on who we believe ourselves to be (Proverbs 23:7, Romans 6:11).
There was this anticipation of a coming Lamb, ever since the very beginning when Adam and Eve sinned and God put to death a sacrifice—probably a lamb—to cover them, to make them worthy of God’s presence (Genesis 3:21). And people would ask, “Where is the Lamb that will cover us, that will make us fit to be in God’s presence?” (Exodus 25:22, Hebrews 10:19–22)
Ever since Abel offered a blood sacrifice, and he was accepted “because of his sacrifice” (Genesis 4:4, Hebrews 11:4). And people might ask, “Where is the sacrifice that will make us acceptable?” (Ephesians 5:2)
When Abraham and Isaac were going up the hill of sacrifice, Isaac said, “We have the fire and knife, but where is the lamb?” And Abraham responded saying, “God will provide Himself a Lamb” (Genesis 22:7–8, John 8:56, Romans 8:32). And people might ask, "Where is this Lamb that will be provided?"
In the Law, there was the daily sacrifice. Every morning and evening lambs had to die as a continual reminder of sin (Exodus 29:38–42, Numbers 28:3–4, Hebrews 10:1–4), so that people would understand that God allows a substitute to die in the place of sinners (Leviticus 17:11, Isaiah 53:4–6). And people might wonder, "Where is my Lamb, my Substitute, that would not only remind me of the sinfulness of sin, but remove it from me forever?" (Hebrews 9:26, 1 Peter 2:24, Psalm 103:12)
Then there’s the Passover Lamb. The Passover was where two main things happened:

    God poured out His wrath on the firstborn sons of Egypt (Exodus 12:12),
    He delivered His people from slavery (Exodus 12:31–42, Deuteronomy 6:21).

And people would wonder, "Where is my Passover Lamb, that can save me from wrath and set me free from slavery to sin?" (Romans 6:6–7, Romans 5:9, John 8:36)

Paul answered this clearly in 1 Corinthians 5:7 – For Christ, our Passover lamb, has been sacrificed.
Like the song, The Mighty Cross says:

    On His brow, a crown of sorrow
    For a King whose weakness was our strength (2 Corinthians 13:4)
    No word He spoke, His love was shown
    For all to see (Isaiah 53:7, Luke 23:34, John 19:9)
    Oh, the cross of Jesus Christ
    He's the reason I'm alive (Galatians 2:20)
    For His blood, has set me free (Revelation 1:5, Romans 5:9)
    It will never lose, its power for me! (Hebrews 9:12, Revelation 12:11)

Notice one point in John 1:29: Jesus came as a Lamb to take away the sin of the world. He takes it away and purchases forgiveness for us (Ephesians 1:7, Colossians 2:13–14), cancelling our debt of sin and freeing us from the burden of guilt (Psalm 32:5, Romans 8:1–2). He has taken away the sin of the world, He has taken away my sin and your sin. And all that is left in us who believe is purity and holiness (Hebrews 10:10, Titus 2:14, 1 Peter 1:18–19).
Think for a minute of all the different kinds of leaves—beautiful oak, maple, cypress, etc. Did you know that God didn't make leaves just for beauty, but also to perform a wonderful function in the health of our environment?
God designed leaves to drink in poison from the air. Leaves consume gases that are poisonous to us: Carbon dioxide, Acidic Sulfur Dioxide, and various other noxious compounds. And what do you think leaves do with the poisons? Leaves transform the bitter poison into life-sustaining oxygen by the process of photosynthesis. They remove poisonous substances and leave oxygen for us. It is quite miraculous!
As we look to the cross and believe (John 3:14–16, Isaiah 45:22), we experience the cross drawing all our bitterness out of us, all our sins, all our anger, and rebellion, all our cursing, our impurity—all sin—out it all comes (Isaiah 53:5, Titus 3:3–5). Jesus is taking all that right into Himself; and like a leaf on a tree, He is photosynthesizing it all. He takes the noxious compounds into Himself and leaves us with purity and holiness and righteousness (Romans 3:25–26, 2 Corinthians 5:21, Hebrews 9:14).
And if you look, John 1:29 shows us what we are to do: did you see it? What are we to do? We are to look, behold or watch the Lamb (John 1:29, Hebrews 12:2). See Him taking your sins on Himself? (1 Peter 2:24) See God nailing your record of wrongs to the cross? (Colossians 2:13–14) With each nail driven into Jesus on the cross He is expunging your record, and the blood that flows is wiping your slate clean (1 John 1:7, Revelation 7:14). Watch as He sinks under the weight of your guilt, as your sin condemns Him and crushes Him to death (Isaiah 53:10, 2 Corinthians 5:21), knowing that there is now no condemnation for you who are in Christ Jesus (Romans 8:1), and that we are completely free from our sins because Jesus bled all over them and died to pay for each one (Hebrews 10:17–18).
Watch the Lamb long enough and you’ll begin to see your old self there; the old sinful you, your old nature died with Christ (Romans 6:6, Galatians 5:24). I have been crucified with Christ and I no longer live (Galatians 2:20). I will not boast in anything except the cross, through which the world was crucified to me, and I to the world (Galatians 6:14).
We are to watch the Lamb so that we would develop hatred for the sin that crucified Him (Romans 6:1–2, Hebrews 12:4), and our hearts will begin to see His love for you (Ephesians 3:18–19, Romans 5:8).
What do you see personally, as you look at the Lamb taking away your sins?

Biblical Illustration: The Leper
The Leper in Luke 5:12–14
There was a man, utterly cast out; untouchable, unclean, condemned to live outside the camp. A leper. His disease not only ate at his flesh but declared him unworthy of God’s presence. He could not enter the temple. He could not be embraced. He could not be clean. Every day, he carried in his body a visible sermon: Sin defiles. And perhaps like many, he asked, Where is the Lamb who can make me clean?
But one day, Jesus came near. The man fell on his face and cried, “Lord, if You are willing, You can make me clean.” And Jesus, God in flesh, touched the untouchable, saying, “I am willing. Be clean.” And immediately, his leprosy was gone.
This is what Jesus, the Lamb of God, does. He doesn’t just cover sin, He removes it. He doesn’t keep us at a distance, He draws near. The leper’s flesh was made whole, but more than that, he was restored to community, to the temple, to worship. The Lamb had taken away what excluded him from God’s presence.
So too with us. We were defiled, but the blood of Jesus makes us clean. Not covered, cleansed. Not distant, brought near. Not ashamed, made holy. Behold the Lamb.
What does Jesus’ healing of the leper show us about how He deals with our sin and shame?

Practical Application
When we behold Jesus as the Lamb of God, it transforms how we live each day. His sacrifice not only cleanses us but invites us into a life of daily nearness, honest confession, joyful worship, and growing holiness. These practices are not burdens; they are responses of love to the One who made us clean.

    Draw near to Jesus daily—like the leper, come honestly and humbly, trusting that He is both willing and able to cleanse (Hebrews 10:22).
    Behold the Lamb—fix your eyes on Christ crucified through reading God's Word and meditating on His sacrifice (John 1:29).
    Confess your sin to God—not to earn forgiveness, but to freshly remember the Lamb who already paid for it (Psalm 32:5).
    Worship in freedom—because you are clean, worship boldly with joy and thanksgiving (Hebrews 13:15).
    Hate sin and love holiness—see sin as what nailed Jesus to the cross and grow in holiness out of love for Him (Romans 6:6).

How can you build a daily habit of drawing near to Jesus with honesty, trust, and worship

    Over the past 30 days I’ve learned how to view myself and my husband as sinners who have been forgiven! I would recommend the course, especially as a triage, pouring in the truth of the gospel in those early days of discovery when you need something to cling to. Start immediately, I wish I had had this that first week. 
    As my fear and anger were still strong I worried this course might lead to enablement, it is NOT! I appreciated the very clear steps for how to deal with future sin and how to engage in those conversations. But I also loved the encouragement to be hopeful and pray for your spouse to grow in the truths of what Christ has done for them. Prayer for your spouse is vital! Jesus is more powerful than all sin! 

    Thank you for what you are doing with your story and sharing the hope of the gospel with others in this way! It’s brought so much healing! And I cannot even believe what huge things it’s doing in my husband’s walk with the Lord. I’m shocked at the changes, I’d never have imagined! The gospel is transforming! And because the gospel has been working in me I can say I love my husband more now than ever before and that was before I started to see changes in him, while I didn’t know if he would step into obedience or not. Because that’s what the gospel can do for your soul.
